### `scholarly_argument_linjamaa_free_will_and_moral_accountabi_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and moral accountability in Valentinianism — The Tripartite Tractate employs concepts of will (Greek: thelēma/boulē) and self-determination that draw on Stoic philosophical psychology (prohairesis, assent, impression) while reconfiguring them within a Christian theological framework; moral accountability is maintained through the pedagogical structure of the cosmos as a 'school' where pneumatic individuals assist psychic ones in moral formation
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed (p. 144, §3.6). The node turns Linjamaa's negative thesis into a positive one. His summary: TriTrac's ethics 'did not rest on the doctrine of free will'; while passion and false impressions based in matter persist 'there could be no self-determination'; 'unlike Stoicism, TriTrac presents a moral system where no individuals in the cosmic creation of the Logos possessed complete self-determination'; 'No one in the cosmos is described as being in the possession of a self-determining will', only a proairesis, good or bad, more or less prone to assent to the Savior. Stoic-derived psychology (proairesis, assent, impression) and βούλησις/θέλημα are indeed discussed (ch. 2-3), but the description omits the rejection of free will and adds 'moral accountability', not his term.
  - « In conclusion, the ethical theory in TriTrac, contrary to many other early Christian systems, did not rest on the doctrine of free will. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:7420-7421]
  - « No one in the cosmos is described as being in the possession of a self-determining will, instead the creatures in the cosmic system have a proairesis, which can be good or bad, that is, more or less prone to assent to the Savior. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:7430-7433]
- Description proposée : Linjamaa (§3.6): TriTrac's ethical theory, unlike many early Christian systems, 'did not rest on the doctrine of free will'. As long as passion and false impressions rooted in matter are viable there can be no self-determination; unlike Stoicism, no one in the Logos' cosmic creation possesses complete self-determination. Like the Stoics, TriTrac equates free will with moral perfection—a mind wholly in tune with the Father's will—which is not viable in the cosmos. Cosmic creatures have instead a proairesis, good or bad, more or less prone to assent to the Savior; the three classes correspond to the material, psychic and pneumatic powers, and one's substance becomes known at the Savior's appearance.
- Pages : 144 (§3.6); ch. 3, 112-158

### `scholarly_argument_linjamaa_social_and_political_involveme_5` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : social and political involvement of psychic Christians — The Tripartite Tractate reflects an attitude toward social and political involvement that distinguishes between pneumatic and psychic Christians: psychic Christians are expected to participate in society and pursue honor in conventional ways, while pneumatic Christians transcend such involvement; this represents a cosmogonic-political commentary where the visible world's political structures reflect the fallen Logos' disordered state
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed (p. 248). Two misreadings. (1) Psychics are not 'expected to ... pursue honor in conventional ways': they are 'described as someone who is driven by the pursuit of honor, but who must listen to advice and help pneumatics' (p. 184); TriTrac calls 'to abandon the blind pursuit of honor', the ideal being metriopatheia (p. 253); pneumatics need support 'from Christians who were not blinded by their involvement in the pursuit of worldly honor'. (2) The oikonomia 'is not presented as evil or solely bad'; the Logos' system 'is good in its foundations', though material powers are linked to instability and 'love of glory'. The node's 'fallen Logos' disordered state' overstates the negative side.
  - « However, the oikonomia is not presented as evil or solely bad. The system the Logos sets up is good in its foundations, established in accordance with the will of the Father. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:12480-12482]
  - « A psychic is described as someone who is driven by the pursuit of honor, but who must listen to advice and help pneumatics. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:9283-9284]
  - « TriTrac encourages everyday Christians to support the leadership of the community and retain positions in society that enable them to do so. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:12490-12491]
- Description proposée : Linjamaa (ch. 6): TriTrac's cosmogony reads as political commentary—material powers, 'love of glory' and 'lust for command' evoke third-century instability—but the oikonomia is not evil: the Logos' system is good in its foundations and set up according to the Father's will. Psychics are driven by the pursuit of honor yet must not be blinded by it; they are to retain positions in society and use them to support the pneumatic leadership, on the model of patron and teacher (Ambrosius and Origen). The ideal for those in the cosmos is metriopatheia, not apatheia. TriTrac fits a context where Christians could attain powerful positions.
- Pages : 227-258, esp. 248, 253; cf. 184

### `scholarly_argument_linjamaa_stoic_influence_on_valentinian_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic influence on Valentinian ethics and psychology — The Tripartite Tractate's theory of emotions, cognitive psychology, and ethical terminology is deeply indebted to Stoicism, particularly the cognitive theory of emotions (judgments/beliefs as constitutive of passions), the concept of apatheia/therapeia/eleutheria, and the structure of prohairesis and assent; however, this is integrated with a distinctively Christian cosmogonic narrative
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed (p. 110). Linjamaa's own conclusion is broader than 'deeply indebted to Stoicism': 'an advanced reception of Aristotelian, Platonic, and Stoic thought, appropriated within a very particular Christian creation myth'; he warns against 'placing Christian texts within this or that philosophical tradition'; apatheia is viable only for the Savior, so 'much as Plutarch and Philo maintained, cosmic life entailed being entangled with emotions', some useful, passions serving as pedagogical tools. The Stoic elements (cognitive theory, proairesis/assent, apatheia-therapeia-eleutheria) are discussed, but the node loses the Aristotelian/Platonic side and the metriopatheia-like outcome.
  - « What we have in TriTrac is an advanced reception of Aristotelian, Platonic, and Stoic thought, appropriated within a very particular Christian creation myth. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:5748-5750]
  - « Thus, much as Plutarch and Philo maintained, cosmic life entailed being entangled with emotions, some of which in fact can be useful and necessary for the functioning of cosmic existence. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:5752-5754]
- Description proposée : Linjamaa (ch. 2 conclusion): reading TriTrac against Stoic and Platonic theories of emotion illuminates its ethics, whose theoretical basis is a theory of cognition; but the text is 'an advanced reception of Aristotelian, Platonic, and Stoic thought, appropriated within a very particular Christian creation myth'. The ideal is freedom from the control of passion, yet apatheia is a divine ideal viable only for the Savior; as in Plutarch and Philo, cosmic life is entangled with emotions, some useful and necessary. Passions function as pedagogical tools and are associated with the heavenly powers; being controlled by them, as the Greeks are, leads to error.
- Pages : 71-111, esp. 110; 135-144

### `scholarly_argument_linjamaa_three_classes_of_humans_and_re_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : three classes of humans and restricted moral choice — The Tripartite Tractate's threefold anthropology (pneumatic, psychic, hylic) represents a 'fixed anthropology' that nevertheless allows for restricted choice within categories; psychic humans have limited moral progress possible through education and formation by pneumatic moral experts, while pneumatic humans are essentially determined by their divine nature and hylic humans are excluded from salvation
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed (p. 184). 'Fixed anthropology' and the exclusion of the material class are Linjamaa's (material substance 'not part of the plan of salvation', 'destroyed', pp. 169 etc.). But 'pneumatic humans are essentially determined by their divine nature' loses his explicit point that 'moral improvement is an integral component in the nature of both the in-group categories: psychic and pneumatic. The pneumatics are told to gain expertise of good and evil'; and the classes 'were fixed in theory but would most likely have been fluid in practice' (p. 264). Psychics are dependent on pneumatic advice rather than 'limited' in progress.
  - « We are not dealing with hard determinism; there is room for personal improvement. In fact, moral improvement is an integral component in the nature of both the in-group categories: psychic and pneumatic. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:9294-9296]
  - « TriTrac presents the three human categories as Stoic dispositions, categories that did not allow for the conversion from one to another. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:9309-9310]
  - « partial salvation while the material substance is destroyed. » [06_Patristique/[Nag Hammadi and Manichaean Studies, 95] Paul Linjamaa - The Ethics of The Tripartite Tractate (NHC I, 5). A Study of Determinism and Early Christian Philosophy of Ethics (2019, Brill) - libgen.li.md:8540]
- Description proposée : Linjamaa (ch. 4): TriTrac's tripartite anthropology is 'an early Christian deterministic system' but 'not ... hard determinism; there is room for personal improvement'. The categories are Stoic-like dispositions that do not allow conversion from one to another—the material substance is destroyed, the psychic partly saved—yet moral improvement is integral to both in-group classes: pneumatics are told to gain expertise of good and evil, psychics to learn from them. Fixed in theory, the categories were probably fluid in practice, which helped explain membership fluctuations; the social structure clearly favors the pneumatics, whose expertise required costly education.
- Pages : 159-184, esp. 184; cf. 169, 264

Vocabulary: 'libertarian'/'compatibilist' appear in O'Keefe, Byerly and de Monneron's own text, so their use in those nodes is the authors' own; 'autonomy', 'moral agency and determinism', 'deterministic soteriology' in the Narbonne nodes are not Narbonne's words. ### `scholarly_argument_narbonne_contemplative_demiurgy_and_cau_5` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Contemplative demiurgy and causal autonomy (Study Five) — Develops 'Plotinian contemplative demiurgy' as 'new type of causality' where production occurs through contemplation/θεωρία rather than deliberate technical making; this preserves divine and intellectual autonomy against Gnostic models of deficient demiurgic creation
- Constat : Quote verbatim at md 4955-4959 = printed p. 117 (Study Five opening; PDF page 130, offset 13 anchored on the TOC). Core faithful: new type of causality, generation 'not based on reflection or calculation'. But 'preserves divine and intellectual autonomy against Gnostic models of deficient demiurgic creation' is not Narbonne's vocabulary: his anti-Gnostic point is that contemplative, non-deliberative production counters a Gnostic cosmogony that is 'a series of accidental episodes' tied to a fall of the soul (pp. 119-120). The Greek θεωρία in the description is not in the file (only Latin-script 'theoria', md 4949).
  - « Nonetheless, this paradox is the inevitable consequence, though undoubtedly extreme, of a new type of causality introduced by Plotinus, which holds that things naturally flow from one to another in a continuous flux, with a spontaneous creativity flawlessly linking the realities among themselves. » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:4955-4959] (Narbonne 2011, p. 117)
  - « In postulating that the generation is not based on reflection or calculation, but is rather naturally effectuated by an essentially productive power, Plotinus attacks the very essence of the Gnostic cosmogony, which is a series of accidental episodes » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:5076-5079] (Narbonne 2011, p. 120)
  - « Indeed, the ultimately anti-gnostic motive for this Plotinian position, presumably already brewing in previous treatises, manifests itself on several occasions in Treatise » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:5042-5044] (Narbonne 2011, p. 119)
- Description proposée : Study Five presents Plotinian 'contemplative demiurgy' (Enn. III.8) as 'a new type of causality': things 'naturally flow from one to another in a continuous flux', so generation 'is not based on reflection or calculation' but 'naturally effectuated by an essentially productive power', and cannot be assimilated to creationism or to a literal Platonic demiurgy. Narbonne reads this as 'ultimately anti-gnostic': it 'attacks the very essence of the Gnostic cosmogony', a 'series of accidental episodes' tied to a fall of the soul, and the non-inclination of the soul is 'offered precisely to counter the Gnostic type of production'.
- Pages : 117-127 (quote p. 117; anti-Gnostic point pp. 119-120)

### `scholarly_argument_narbonne_god_as_causa_sui_and_self_dete_6` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : God as causa sui and self-determination (Study Six) — Examines Plotinus's concept of God as self-caused (causa sui) and its possible Gnostic sources; the self-caused nature of the One preserves absolute autonomy and freedom from determination, contrasting with Gnostic models of derived divine power
- Constat : Quote verbatim at md 5828-5842 = printed p. 138 (Study Six conclusion). The description's 'freedom from determination' and 'Gnostic models of derived divine power' misread Narbonne. He says the One's 'will' 'wills in fact nothing' and is 'its infinite potency asserting itself as absolute necessity and eternity'; 'the true self-determination, which is not the freedom of contingencies, is that one which the God enjoys' (p. 137). The contrast with the Gnostic God is that its production 'is a specific event stemming from a decision', not 'derived power'. The Greek in verified_reference (οἷον αἴτιον ἑαυτοῦ) is corrupted in the extraction (md 5514) and cannot be confirmed.
  - « The “will” of the Plotinian One wills in fact nothing, it is simply the remodelled expression of its infinite potency asserting itself as absolute necessity and eternity. The “will” of the Gnostic God is of another sort, since it accords to the divine, with regard to the sensible world, a sort of prior existence » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:5836-5841] (Narbonne 2011, p. 138)
  - « In short, the true self-determination, which is not the freedom of contingencies, is that one which the God enjoys. » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:5792-5793] (Narbonne 2011, p. 137)
  - « On the whole, the hypothesis of a Gnostic adversary is that which is most solidly founded. With the Gnostics, we possess the probable motif, the general context and the vocabulary, namely, that which Plotinus borrows momentarily from his opponents. » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:5828-5831] (Narbonne 2011, p. 138)
- Description proposée : Study Six argues that the objector against whom Plotinus develops the causa sui argument of Ennead VI.8 is most plausibly Gnostic: Plotinus 'borrows momentarily' their vocabulary of a First Principle that 'begets and wills itself', another 'transfer or refocusing' induced by anti-Gnosticism. The results differ: the Plotinian One's 'will' 'wills in fact nothing', being 'its infinite potency asserting itself as absolute necessity and eternity' — 'the true self-determination, which is not the freedom of contingencies' — whereas the Gnostic God's production 'is a specific event stemming from a decision'.
- Pages : 129-141 (quote p. 138; self-determination p. 137)

### `scholarly_argument_narbonne_plotinus_s_anti_gnostic_polemi_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Plotinus's anti-Gnostic polemic and free will/moral responsibility — Argues that Plotinus's response to Gnosticism is not confined to explicit polemical treatises but is 'slowly orchestrated' across multiple treatises; the Gnostic 'file' represents ongoing theoretical debates about soul's descent, matter's generation, and ascent—topics with direct bearing on moral agency and determinism
- Constat : 'Slowly orchestrated' located at md 263 = printed p. 2 (not pp. 1-2 generally wrong, but the node title and last clause import a free-will framing). Narbonne's Introduction speaks of discussions 'woven in with other important theoretical debates'; it says nothing about 'moral agency and determinism'. The clause 'topics with direct bearing on moral agency and determinism' is the graph's own gloss, not Narbonne's claim.
  - « during which the response to Gnosticism is slowly orchestrated, with Treatise , Against the Gnostics, representing, of course, the culmination of the entire enterprise. But the greater cycle has decisive roots in Plotinus’ initial writing period » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:262-265] (Narbonne 2011, p. 2)
  - « discussions which would have been subject, naturally, to the usual high points and low points, and which would have found themselves woven in with other important theoretical debates, but which Plotinus nonetheless never lost sight of. » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:253-257] (Narbonne 2011, p. 2)
- Description proposée : Narbonne holds that Plotinus' response to Gnosticism is not confined to Treatise 33 but 'slowly orchestrated' over the Großzyklus, with Against the Gnostics as 'the culmination of the entire enterprise'; the discussions with his 'Gnostic — yet Platonizing — friends' were 'woven in with other important theoretical debates' which Plotinus 'never lost sight of'. The studies then track this on the generation of matter, the partly undescended soul, the soul's weakness and evil, the modes of reascent, contemplative demiurgy and the causa sui. No free-will or determinism framing is Narbonne's.
- Pages : 1-5 (quoted phrase p. 2)

The node's ἀμέθεξις is nowhere in the file. (2) The thesis is inverted: Narbonne argues the partly undescended soul IS 'the Plotinian reformulation of the Gnostic consubstantiality' of elect souls, 'transposed onto a scale that encompasses all human beings' (p. 60); the contrast is universal vs. elect, not 'autonomous reascent' vs. 'deterministic soteriology'. Note 6 even qualifies the automatic character of Gnostic salvation.

  - « which guarantees the human soul, while incorporated in the sensible world, a direct and uninterrupted link with transcendent realities. » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:2448-2449] (Narbonne 2011, p. 55)
- Description proposée : Study Two: the partly undescended soul, which guarantees the incarnate soul 'a direct and uninterrupted link with transcendent realities', is 'a transfer or, more precisely, a reformulation of a teaching' traceable to the Gnostics. The lead is the term consubstantiality (Greek corrupted in the extraction): the Plotinian doctrine is 'the Plotinian reformulation of the Gnostic consubstantiality — consubstantiality of elect souls that guarantees their return to the Pleroma — now transposed onto a scale that encompasses all human beings', against Gnostics 'for whom consubstantiality with the divine was a privilege granted by birth'.

### `scholarly_argument_narbonne_soul_s_weakness_and_relation_t_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)

- Constat : Quote verbatim at md 4097-4101 = printed p. 94. 'Preserving soul's responsibility' inverts Narbonne: matter as by-product of the alterity-infinity from the One serves to 'reduc[e] the responsibility of the higher principles' (p. 88), Plotinus had 'to exonerate the principles, and especially Soul, for evil's presence', and was 'forced to minimize the role of Soul and to maximize the role of matter in the emergence of evil' (p. 94).   - « He had to showthat it was indeed possible to exonerate the principles, and especially Soul, for evil’s presence in the sensible world » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:4090-4092] (Narbonne 2011, p. 94)
- Description proposée : Study Three asks whether the late treatment of the soul's weakness (Enn. I.8) marks a doctrinal evolution. Sensible matter is for Plotinus 'not a creation of the Soul, but a by-product of the alterity-infinity coming from the One', which reduces 'the responsibility of the higher principles'. Narbonne concludes that, opposing Gnostics 'whose teachings were becoming increasingly influential in his own school', Plotinus had to 'exonerate the principles, and especially Soul, for evil's presence' and 'was forced to minimize the role of Soul and to maximize the role of matter in the emergence of evil'.

### `scholarly_argument_narbonne_two_modes_of_soul_s_reascent_a_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Two modes of soul's reascent and moral autonomy (Study Four) — Identifies two modes of ascent in Plotinus: solitary ascent 'alone to the Alone' (μόνος πρὸς μόνον) in Ennead 9 (VI.9) vs. 'accompanied ascent' with intellect in Ennead 38 (VI.7), 36; critiques Gnostic elitism where 'Gnostic' has privileged soteriological status over other humans, other divine beings, and even intellect itself—Plotinus's alternative preserves universal philosophical access to the Good and intellectual autonomy
- Constat : No quote. The two modes and the three-fold critique of Gnostic privilege are in the TOC (pp. 97, 100, 105, 108, 111). But the flat conclusion 'Plotinus's alternative preserves universal philosophical access to the Good and intellectual autonomy' turns Narbonne's open question into an assertion: he asks whether Plotinus offers 'a real alternative' and says 'Interpreters have yet to offer an answer' (p. 107), then leaves undecided whether the shift in VI.7 is 'doctrinal evolution' or 'a shift of emphasis' (p. 114), ending with 'selective, but significant, developments' and 'Prudence... prevents us here from speaking of evolution pure and simple' (p. 116). 'Intellectual autonomy' is not his vocabulary. The Greek phrase in the description is corrupted in the file and cannot be confirmed. Study Four ends at p. 116, not 115.
  - « Does Plotinus, who protests so vehemently against Gnostic elitism— which is itself apparently subject to interpretation23—, indeed offer a real alternative? Interpreters have yet to offer an answer to this question. One can assume, by insisting on the necessityof personal effort in the reascent » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:4575-4578] (Narbonne 2011, p. 107)
  - « We are therefore compelled to recognize, at the very least, the existence of selective, but significant, developments within Plotinus’ thought, which might be considered as traces of an evolution. Prudence, however, prevents us here from speaking of evolution pure and simple. » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:4935-4938] (Narbonne 2011, p. 116)
  - « Nevertheless, the modified description of the ascent of the soul in (VI ), , frees Plotinus from the threat of being associated with the Gnostics and their errors. » [Plotinus-in-Dialogue-with-the-Gnostics-.md:4855-4857] (Narbonne 2011, p. 114)
- Description proposée : Study Four contrasts the solitary ascent of the soul in Treatise 9 (VI.9) with the 'accompanied ascent' of Treatise 38 (VI.7), where the soul 'no longer rises alone' but is borne up by the Intellect, and sets between them Plotinus' critique of the Gnostic's privileged status over other men, other divine beings and the Intellect. Whether Plotinus offers 'a real alternative' to Gnostic elitism is left open ('Interpreters have yet to offer an answer'); the shift 'frees Plotinus from the threat of being associated with the Gnostics', but Narbonne speaks only of 'selective, but significant, developments', not 'evolution pure and simple'.
- Pages : 97-116 (elitism p. 107; conclusion pp. 114-116)

### `scholarly_argument_o_keefe_aristotle_and_epicurus_on_orig_5` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle and Epicurus on origins of character and action — Epicurus' theory of freedom has closer affinities with Aristotle's thought than with modern free will debates, particularly in its emphasis on rational action and the origins of character and action. Chapter 3 explores this comparative dimension.
- Constat : Quote verbatim at md 2136-2141 = printed p. 48; chapter 3 = pp. 48-64 (chapter 4 begins p. 65). The description keeps only the positive half ('closer affinities with Aristotle') and drops O'Keefe's explicitly negative half: 'nothing that Aristotle says in these texts helps us understand the role of the swerve', because nothing in Aristotle's account of agency 'need be interpreted in an antideterminist way'; for 'neither Aristotle nor Epicurus does this have any anti-determinist implications' (p. 9).
  - « I will argue that Epicurus would be sympathetic to Aristotle’s contention that an agent needs to be the origin of both her own character and her actions. However, nothing that Aristotle says in these texts helps us understand the role of the swerve. That is because nothing in Aristotle’s discussions of agency need be interpreted in an antideterminist way. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:2137-2141] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 48)
  - « I agree with others who have thought that both Aristotle and Epicurus wish the agent to be the ‘origin’ of both his actions and his own character. However, for neither Aristotle nor Epicurus does this have any anti-determinist implications. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:504-508] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 9)
- Description proposée : Chapter 3 compares Epicurus with Aristotle's NE 3 on the agent as 'origin' (archê) and cause of his actions and character. Conclusions 'in part positive and in part negative': Epicurus 'would be sympathetic to Aristotle's contention that an agent needs to be the origin of both her own character and her actions', but 'nothing that Aristotle says in these texts helps us understand the role of the swerve', since 'nothing in Aristotle's discussions of agency need be interpreted in an antideterminist way' — for neither author does the internal-origin requirement carry anti-determinist implications.
- Pages : 48-64 (quote p. 48; summary p. 9)

  - « Epicurus helped to form a libertarian conception of the freedom of the will that he himself would have repudiated. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:225-226] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 2)
- Description proposée : Faithful as written; only the page of the quote_verbatim is wrong.

### `scholarly_argument_o_keefe_lucretius_on_swerve_and_volunt_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Lucretius on swerve and voluntas — Lucretius' account of the swerve and 'free volition' (libera voluntas) in De rerum natura needs careful interpretation. The swerve is introduced as a third cause of atomic motion alongside weight and past motions/collisions, but its exact relationship to voluntas is disputed and has been subject to multiple competing explanations.
- Constat : Quote verbatim at md 1203-1214 = printed p. 26; ch. 2 = pp. 26-47. The description stops at 'disputed... multiple competing explanations' and loses O'Keefe's own conclusions: DRN 'undercuts the thesis that Epicurus' concerns are much like those of modern libertarians' (p. 26); the voluntas at stake is the ability 'to move ourselves as we wish' (pp. 1, 9); and DRN 2.251-257 shows the swerve saves us from fate 'by breaking the chains of cause and effect', not 'by being itself the origin of our actions' (p. 30).
  - « I will first examine DRN 2 251–293, and then DRN 4 877–896, and show that the De rerum natura undercuts the thesis that Epicurus’ concerns are much like those of modern libertarians. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:1212-1214] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 26)
  - « It does not indicate that the swerve saves us from fate by being itself the origin of our actions; instead, it saves us from fate by breaking the chains of cause and effect that otherwise would stretch backwards into infinity. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:1387-1390] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 30)
  - « his description of the libera voluntas that the swerve helps safeguard makes it clear that libera voluntas is not a libertarian freedom of the will. Instead, Lucretius cares about our ability to move ourselves as we wish, in order to get what we desire. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:499-503] (O'Keefe 2005, pp. 8-9)
- Description proposée : Chapter 2 examines Lucretius DRN 2.251-293 and 4.877-896. O'Keefe's conclusion on the swerve is 'mostly negative': Lucretius 'tells us remarkably little about the role it is supposed to play'. But the passages show 'what sort of freedom the Epicureans are concerned to defend': libera voluntas is 'not a libertarian freedom of the will' but 'our ability to move ourselves as we wish, in order to get what we desire'. DRN 2.251-257 suggests the swerve saves us from fate 'by breaking the chains of cause and effect', not 'by being itself the origin of our actions'; the text 'undercuts' the assimilation of Epicurus to modern libertarians.
- Pages : 26-47 (quote p. 26; p. 30; summary pp. 8-9)

### `scholarly_argument_o_keefe_swerve_and_collisions_swerve_a_6` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Swerve and collisions / swerve and fate — The swerve's role in preserving freedom from fate is not through providing indeterministic gaps for volition, but rather through ensuring that atomic collisions have non-necessitated origins, thereby undermining Democritean fatalism at the cosmological level. The swerve saves us from fate not by making individual actions undetermined but by ensuring that the causal history of the cosmos is not fully necessitated from eternity.
- Constat : No quote. The kernel (swerve not an indeterministic gap for volition) is right, but the account of how it saves us from fate is not O'Keefe's. Ch. 5: the swerve is 'an explanatory archê of atomic collisions' answering Aristotelian sufficient-reason objections, and O'Keefe expressly says it 'is not needed as a temporal archê, a starting-point, of collisions' (p. 122) — the opposite of 'non-necessitated origins' of the causal history. Ch. 6 ('bivalence interpretation'): Epicurus blocks Megarian/dialectical fatalist arguments by denying the Principle of Bivalence, and the swerve is his physical basis for that denial and for the non-fixity of the future; these arguments 'do not operate at the level of psychological determinism' (p. 151). 'Democritean fatalism at the cosmological level' conflates ch. 4's Democritus with ch. 6's logical fatalism.
  - « Indeed, the cosmogonic argument that Lucretius gives for the swerve seems to be very weak, given the standard interpretation of it, since the swerve is not needed as a temporal archê, a starting-point, of collisions. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:5247-5250] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 122)
  - « Furthermore, it is well documented that Epicurus accepted the Interentailment Thesis: he thought he needed to give a physical basis for his rejection of the Principle of Bivalence, and the resultant fixity of the future, and the swerve was his means of doing so. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:6507-6510] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 151)
  - « They do not operate at the level of psychological determinism, nor do they talk about the freedom to do otherwise, etc. » [O'Keefe-Epicurus-on-Freedom.md:6516-6517] (O'Keefe 2005, p. 151)
- Description proposée : Chapters 5-6 give O'Keefe's own view of the swerve. Ch. 5: with weight and collisions it answers Aristotelian sufficient-reason objections to Democritus; 'the swerve is needed as an explanatory archê of atomic collisions', not 'as a temporal archê, a starting-point, of collisions'. Ch. 6 ('bivalence interpretation'): Epicurus blocks the Megarian-dialectical fatalist arguments by denying the Principle of Bivalence and, accepting the Interentailment Thesis, posits the swerve as 'a physical basis for his rejection of the Principle of Bivalence, and the resultant fixity of the future'. These arguments 'do not operate at the level of psychological determinism'; Epicurus' reasons rest on mistakes later exposed by Chrysippus and Carneades.
- Pages : 110-152 (ch. 5 110-122; ch. 6 123-152)

### `scholarly_argument_byerly_definition_of_theological_dete_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : definition of theological determinism — Theological determinism is stipulatively defined as the view that all free actions that exist are causally determined, which is weaker than the common usage requiring all contingent facts be determined by divine will. This stipulative definition allows compatibilist Molinism.
- Constat : Quote verbatim at md 116-118 (note 5); the stipulative definition is at md 72-74. Description matches. No printed pages available (online-first PDF; '289-290' cannot be verified from the fonds).
  - « I will show that a theist who affirms that all the free actions that exist are causally determined (a theological determinist, as I will stipulatively define the view) 5 can adopt quite a lot from the approaches of these authors. » [Byerly_2017_Free_Will_Theodicies_Theological_Determinists.md:72-75]
  - « The term ‘theological determinism’ is often used with a different meaning, requiring that all contingent facts are determined by the divine will. This is not a requirement of ‘theological determinism’ as I have defined it » [Byerly_2017_Free_Will_Theodicies_Theological_Determinists.md:116-117]

### `scholarly_argument_de_monneron_source_incompatibilism_vs_pap_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : source incompatibilism vs. PAP — The author takes an intermediate position: rejects Stump and Widerker's arguments against the Principle of Alternate Possibilities (PAP), maintaining that free action requires the ability to do otherwise, while also rejecting hard determinism.
- Constat : Quote verbatim at md 226-229 = printed p. 8. The description keeps 'rejects Stump and Widerker's arguments against PAP' but loses the second half of the 'intermediate' position: the author prefers to base his critique of determinism on condition 2 (a free act has no sufficient cause) rather than on 1 (could have done otherwise), holding that 1 'is explained by' 2 'and therefore reduces to it' (md 229-230, p. 8; developed p. 9). 'While also rejecting hard determinism' is an inference; the passage speaks of 'critiquer le déterminisme'.
  - « Ma position dans ce débat est intermédiaire. Je n'admets pas les arguments de Stump et Widerker contre le principe des possibilités alternatives, mais je préfère m'appuyer sur 2 plutôt que sur 1 pour critiquer le déterminisme. Je pense en effet que la première idée s'explique par la deuxième, et donc s'y réduit. » [DE MONNERON thèse: Le Déterminisme et la Responsabilité morale.md:227-230] (de Monneron 2011, p. 8)
  - « 1. A est un acte libre (de S) → S aurait pu ne pas faire A. 2. A est un acte libre → A n'a pas de cause suffisante. » [DE MONNERON thèse: Le Déterminisme et la Responsabilité morale.md:223-224] (de Monneron 2011, p. 8)
- Description proposée : De Monneron distinguishes two incompatibilist conditions: (1) a free act is one the agent could have refrained from (the principle of alternative possibilities), and (2) a free act has no sufficient cause (the 'source incompatibilists' Stump and Widerker keep only 2). His position is 'intermédiaire': he does not accept Stump's and Widerker's arguments against the principle of alternative possibilities, yet prefers to rest his critique of determinism on 2 rather than 1, because he thinks 1 'is explained by' 2 'and therefore reduces to it' — wherever necessity seems to exclude responsibility, it is causal necessity.
- Pages : 8-9

### `scholarly_argument_moon_augustine_s_development_on_pre_2` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Augustine's development on predestination — Augustine's final reading of Romans 9:6-13 did not develop simply by 'a more careful and honest reading of Paul' but through complex polemical and intellectual development, including engagement with Manichaean determinism and his letter to Simplician
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located word for word at lines 5112-5119, printed p. 91 (section 2.5), i.e. outside the stated range 'ii, 42-68'. Content faithful: Moon's thesis point (4) (p. ii, p. 3) and his argument that the shift in To Simplician was 'influenced by his interaction with Manichaeism'; the later Donatist/Pelagian debates 'only served to solidify this reading' (p. 99). The Augustine section itself runs pp. 42-67 (1.2.4), To Simplician pp. 49-61.
  - « we argued that this shift did not result from nothing more than a “close reading” of Paul, but a reading of Paul that was influenced by his interaction with Manichaeism. » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:5112-5115] (p. 91)
  - « His later debates with the Donatists and Pelagians only served to solidify this reading. » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:5588-5589] (p. 99)
- Description proposée : Moon (thesis point 4) argues that Augustine's final reading of Rom 9:6-13 'did not develop simply by "a more careful and honest reading of Paul"': the 'radical shift' in To Simplician (396-397) was 'a reading of Paul that was influenced by his interaction with Manichaeism', in an anti-Manichaean work where Augustine's reading 'took on a level of determinism that was rivaled ... by only Manichaeism'; the later Donatist and Pelagian debates 'only served to solidify this reading'.
- Pages : ii, 3, 42-67, 91, 99

### `scholarly_argument_moon_cognate_passages_as_interpreti_5` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Cognate passages as interpretive key — The patristic appeal to cognate passages in Paul's letters (passages containing similar words and ideas) indicates a promising way forward in interpreting Romans 9:6-13, suggesting that the passage should not be read in isolation
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 5329-5337, printed p. 95 (section 2.7 'Romans 9:6-13 and Cognate Passages', pp. 95-99). Matches thesis point (5) on p. ii and p. 3. Description reproduces Moon's own wording ('promising way forward', 'passages containing similar words and ideas').
  - « We would like to conclude our thesis by submitting that the patristic interpreters themselves indicate such a context through their appeal to cognate passages in Paul’s letters (i.e. passages containing similar words and ideas). » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:5333-5335] (p. 95)

### `scholarly_argument_moon_free_will_vs_determinism_in_pa_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Free will vs. determinism in patristic debate — The patristic interpretation of Romans 9:6-13 reveals fundamental tensions between free will and determinism, with figures like Origen and Pelagius emphasizing human freedom and responsibility, while Augustine moved toward a more deterministic predestinarian position influenced by anti-Pelagian and anti-Manichaean polemics
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 5569-5589, printed p. 99 (Final Summary), outside the stated range '5-76'. The description flattens Moon's causal ordering: for Moon the Manichaean debate is what produced Augustine's reading that 'precluded free-will'; the Donatist and Pelagian debates 'only served to solidify' it — not a co-equal 'anti-Pelagian and anti-Manichaean' influence. It also drops Moon's own verdict that reading 9:14ff. 'in terms of the issues of determinism and free-will' is 'a concern that is somewhat removed from the issue Paul began with' (p. 95): the free-will/determinism frame is for Moon a gnostic/Manichaean-induced patristic lens, not something the passage 'reveals'. Pelagius is treated by Moon (pp. 68-70) as one of four Latin exegetes responding to Manichaean elements, not as a headline counterpart to Origen.
  - « Their approach then determined the approach of Origen, who in turn had an enormous influence on subsequent interpreters in the Greek and Latin traditions. » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:5576-5578] (p. 99)
  - « Augustine was one such interpreter, but as a result of his debate with the Manichaeans, he came to a reading of predestination in Romans 9 that precluded free-will and made God the sole decision-maker in the process of salvation. His later debates with the Donatists and Pelagians only served to solidify this reading. » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:5584-5589] (p. 99)
  - « such a reading caused 9:14ff. to be read in terms of the issues of determinism and free-will—a concern that is somewhat removed from the issue Paul began with in Romans 9. » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:5325-5327] (p. 95)
- Description proposée : Moon's summary: a predestinarian approach to Rom 9:6-13 arose among gnostic Christians, who raised the predestination of the patriarchs to 'a universal paradigm'; their approach 'determined the approach of Origen', whose 'free-will reading' Latin Nicene exegetes adopted against Manichaeism. Augustine, 'as a result of his debate with the Manichaeans', reached a reading that 'precluded free-will and made God the sole decision-maker'; the Donatist and Pelagian debates 'only served to solidify this reading'. Reading 9:14ff. 'in terms of the issues of determinism and free-will' is, for Moon, 'somewhat removed' from Paul's own issue.
- Pages : 5-79, 95, 99

### `scholarly_argument_moon_gnostic_and_manichaean_determi_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gnostic and Manichaean determinism — Gnostic and Manichaean interpreters of Romans 9 advanced strongly deterministic readings that divided humanity into fixed categories of elect and non-elect based on nature, which patristic orthodox writers both appropriated and resisted in formulating their own positions
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 1868-1882, printed p. 32 (section 1.2.1 'The Manichaean Context', pp. 27-36). The description says 'Gnostic and Manichaean interpreters of Romans 9 advanced strongly deterministic readings'; Moon states explicitly that the Manichaeans 'did not, as far as we know, provide a reading of 9:6-13' and that 'Nicene exegetes did not have to counter a Manichaean interpretation of 9:6-13, simply because there was none' — Manichaean influence is indirect (a reader 'familiar with the Manichaean myth could have easily read these elements into 9:6-13'). Only the Valentinians are credited with an actual reading based on natures (pneumatics / non-pneumatics, section 1.1.1, pp. 5-9). 'Appropriated and resisted' is a fair gloss of Moon's 'Their approach then determined the approach of Origen' plus the free-will defence.
  - « Although the Manichaeans did not, as far as we know, provide a reading of 9:6-13, we would nevertheless argue that the Latin Nicene interpretation of this passage was influenced by Manichaeism. » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:1868-1870] (p. 32)
  - « Nicene exegetes did not have to counter a Manichaean interpretation of 9:6-13, simply because there was none. » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:1864-1866] (p. 32)
  - « Thus, according to the Valentinian myth, humanity is divided at birth into the pneumatics and the non-pneumatics. Certain people are, by their very nature, destined for salvation » [03_Paul/UBC_History_Interpretation_Romans9_Patristic.md:545-547] (section 1.1.1, pp. 5-9)

Only quantum indeterminacy is indeterministic for him, and randomness 'sure isn't a manifestation of free will'. 'The kind of causation needed for meaningful agency' is not his wording. - Description proposée : Sapolsky reviews three scientific 'revolutions' — chaos theory, emergent complexity, quantum indeterminacy — in whose 'crevices' 'numerous thinkers believe that you can find free will', and concludes 'they are not where free will dwells'. Chaotic and emergent systems are for him deterministic though unpredictable (unpredictability is not indeterminacy); only quantum effects are indeterministic, and even if they 'bubble up', randomness 'sure isn't a manifestation of free will'. Believers in free will have either missed 'the determinism lurking beneath the surface' or 'erroneously concluded' that the indeterministic aspects of the universe explain character and behavior.

- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 1973-1977, printed p. 34 (section I.3.3; I.3.4 begins at line 1979). Tolan's own framing is theodicy: the De Principiis passage 'introduces, in terms of theodicy, a "free will defence"', evil is privatio boni, and corruptibility is what lets us 'make goodness our own'. The node's claim that the framework 'explains how moral responsibility emerges from the soul's capacity for choice and its embodied, corruptible state' is a gloss not made in the passage; Tolan's nearest statement is footnote 109 ('Origen frequently equates rationality with freedom ... freedom is the ability to choose'). Satiety and the cooling of the intellects are I.3.4's topic (via Harl, Chadwick, Bostock). Stated range 'Chapter I.3' is a section label, not pages.
  - « this passage also introduces, in terms of theodicy, a ‘free will defence’. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Tolan - 2021 - The Contemplation of the Transcendent Heart Tracing the ἡγεμονικόν from the Stoics to Origen.md:1972-1973] (p. 34)
  - « Importantly, this passage also tells us why it is better for the possibility of evil to exist than for it not to exist in a ‘perfect’, incorruptible creation: through our free motions we are able to make goodness our own. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Tolan - 2021 - The Contemplation of the Transcendent Heart Tracing the ἡγεμονικόν from the Stoics to Origen.md:1975-1977] (p. 34)
- Description proposée : Tolan (I.3.3 'Theodicy: Corruptibility as the Basis for Possessing the Good as One's Own') reads De Principiis II.9.2 as an account of evil as privatio boni that 'introduces, in terms of theodicy, a "free will defence"': diversity, or evil, is 'something we introduce to creation through our own free will, ergo God is not the cause of evil', and a corruptible creation is better than an incorruptible one because 'through our free motions we are able to make goodness our own'. I.3.4 then treats satiety in the Good and the cooling of the intellects (Harl, Chadwick, Bostock, Philo).
- Pages : 27-40 (quote p. 34)

### `scholarly_argument_tolan_romans_and_divine_sovereignty__5` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Romans 9 and divine sovereignty vs. human responsibility — Origen addresses Paul's 'not of the one willing' (οὐ τοῦ θέλοντος) and 'from the same lump' (ἐκ τοῦ αὐτοῦ φυράματος) in De Principiis III.1 §§18-24, offering an original contribution to the free will-determinism debate that engages Stoic psychology within a biblical framework
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 6539-6545, printed p. 121 (II.12, §21-4). The sections are II.11 (§18-20, p. 116) and II.12 (§21-4, p. 119). But the node's flat 'offering an original contribution to the free will-determinism debate' erases Tolan's own hedging: II.13 is titled 'An Original Contribution?' and opens by conceding that being 'in all likelihood, the first Christian apologia for self-determination ... does not, however, guarantee its content's originality', quoting Frede's 'rather disappointing' verdict that terminology and claims are 'through and through Stoic'. Tolan's answer is qualified: the aim is pastoral and anti-determinist, the anthropology of a liminal middle state opposes the three-class scheme, and Origen 'ought not' be maligned 'simply for producing a Stoic account of freedom'.
  - « This text is, in all likelihood, the first Christian apologia for self-determination. This does not, however, guarantee its content’s originality. Consider Frede’s opinion, » [01_Philosophie_antique/Tolan - 2021 - The Contemplation of the Transcendent Heart Tracing the ἡγεμονικόν from the Stoics to Origen.md:6582-6583] (p. 122)

  - « Origen retains the language of God ‘fashioning’ (κατασκευάζοντος) this process, but he ensures that God’s ‘determination’ of humanity is contingent upon prohairesis. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Tolan - 2021 - The Contemplation of the Transcendent Heart Tracing the ἡγεμονικόν from the Stoics to Origen.md:6543-6545] (p. 121)
- Description proposée : Tolan reads De Principiis III.1 §18-24 on Rom 9:16 and 9:21: Origen 'establishes four models of the divine-human relation, affirming only one', placing salvation 'on both sides' while making God's 'determination' of humanity 'contingent upon prohairesis'. On originality (II.13 'An Original Contribution?') he is guarded: the treatise is 'in all likelihood, the first Christian apologia for self-determination', but its terminology and claims are Stoic (Frede); its purpose is pastoral, 'to counteract determinism', and its anthropology of a liminal middle state opposes the gnostic three-class scheme.

### `scholarly_argument_tolan_what_depends_on_us_and_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : τὰ ἐφ' ἡμῖν (what depends on us) and προαίρεσις — Origen develops a concept of what depends on us (τὰ ἐφ' ἡμῖν) drawing from both Aristotelian and Epictetan προαίρεσις, with particular attention to the akratic man and the complexity of a monistic soul; the telos is to harmonise the motions of the soul with the motus Dei
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 5401-5403, printed p. 100 (II.6). The node says Origen draws 'from both Aristotelian and Epictetan' prohairesis. Tolan's II.9 ('From Aristotle to Epictetus') recalls 'both the Aristotelian and Epictetian understanding of prohairesis in order to situate Origen's understanding'; his conclusion is that Origen follows Epictetus (p. 100: 'Origen follows Epictetus by dividing ...'; p. 128: Epictetus-inspired use of prohairesis), Epictetus' use being itself 'divergent from, but also based upon the Aristotelian one'. Aristotle is background for situating, not a direct source Origen draws on. The akratic man (II.4), the monistic soul (II.10) and the telos of harmonising with the motus Dei (Abstract) are correctly reported.
  - « Origen follows Epictetus by dividing the ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν from the οὐκ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν. Where Epictetus develops the details of what is up to us, Origen tells us why things are up to us: we have the logos as a judge and examiner of phantasiai. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Tolan - 2021 - The Contemplation of the Transcendent Heart Tracing the ἡγεμονικόν from the Stoics to Origen.md:5401-5403] (p. 100)
  - « Thus, let us recall both the Aristotelian and Epictetian understanding of prohairesis in order to situate Origen’s understanding. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Tolan - 2021 - The Contemplation of the Transcendent Heart Tracing the ἡγεμονικόν from the Stoics to Origen.md:5676-5678] (II.9, p. 105)
- Description proposée : Tolan: 'Origen follows Epictetus by dividing' what is up to us from what is not, adding why things are up to us ('we have the logos as a judge and examiner of phantasiai') and placing the division within his categories of motion. In II.9 he recalls the Aristotelian and the Epictetan understandings of prohairesis 'in order to situate Origen's understanding', Epictetus' use being 'divergent from, but also based upon the Aristotelian one'; Origen's use is Epictetan. II.4 treats the akratic man, II.10 the complexity of a monistic soul; the telos is 'to harmonise the motions of our soul with the motus Dei'.
- Pages : iii, 90-93, 99-100, 105-116 (quote p. 100)

### `scholarly_argument_andresen_sin_and_grace_in_justin_s_theo_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Sin and grace in Justin's theology — The problem of sin and grace plays no role in Justin's theology; Justin does not need Pauline grace because he operates within a Middle Platonist framework where the agent is naturally capable of choosing good, distinguishing him from later Christian developments (Augustine)
- Constat : Curated extraction only. Quote_verbatim present verbatim at line 125 (p. 195). But everything after the first clause — that Justin 'does not need Pauline grace because he operates within a Middle Platonist framework where the agent is naturally capable of choosing good, distinguishing him from later Christian developments (Augustine)' — is the extractor's commentary under 'Significance for Understanding Justin' (lines 129-131), not Andresen's words; the extraction elsewhere flags such passages as 'MON interprétation d'Andresen, pas ses mots' (line 248). Andresen's sentence is a question-raising remark in his conclusion ('Die Frage ist um so mehr zu stellen ...'); the question it qualifies is not preserved in the extraction.

- Description proposée : In his conclusion Andresen observes that 'the question is all the more to be raised because the problem of sin and grace plays no role in Justin's theology' (p. 195). The extraction preserves only this sentence; the inference that Justin has no need of Pauline grace because a Middle Platonist agent is naturally able to choose the good, and the contrast with Augustine, are the extractor's comments and should not be attributed to Andresen.

Sources utilisées : 04_Littérature_secondaire/03_Paul/Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/03_Paul/L'épître aux Colossiens. Un exemple de réception de la théologie paulinienne.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/03_Paul/La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/03_Paul/Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/03_Paul/Dunn_Theology_Paul_Apostle_1998.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Timo Eskola - Theodicy and Predestination in Pauline Soteriology (Wissenschaftliche Untersuchengen Zum Neuen Testament_ 2. Reihe)-Wipf and Stock.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/CR de GILL A Free Will  Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought.md · source trouvée : True
Notes de l'auditeur : All seven works confirmed in the fonds via MANIFEST.md and on disk; the hinted files are the right works (Gill = the review in The European Legacy 19.6, 2014, 797-798, not Frede's book). Tally: 16 FAITHFUL, 11 OVERSIMPLIFIED, 5 DISTORTED, 1 PAGE_WRONG, 1 NOT_FOUND, 1 MISATTRIBUTED-type issue folded into DISTORTED (Gill node 3, where Gill only reports Frede). Systemic findings: (1) page_map offsets — Dunn_Theology_Paul_Apostle_1998.md maps 2-3 pages LOW against the PDF folios (map 377/412/472/510 vs printed 379/414/475/513); the Eskola file maps 4 pages LOW (map 183/292/298/176/204 vs printed 187/296/302/180/208); both files are AUDIT-FAIL in agentic_search, so I read every printed page directly from pdftotext folios — the page_map for these two files should be re-audited before any citation. (2) The two archive-ouverte Dettwiler PDFs (1995, 1998) have page_map entries at conf 0.50 that return PDF indices, not printed pages; printed pages were read from the running folios inside the extraction (Foi et Vie 26-40; ETR 333-347, two printed pages per PDF page: left column = even page, right column = odd page). (3) Dettwiler 2020 is a provisional, unpublished UNIGE teaching text (header: 'texte tout à fait provisoire, non destiné à être publié'); pages from its own footer. (4) Currado is an undated, non-peer-reviewed anti-Calvinist web essay (Micah Currado); 'Currado 2014' in the lot id is not supported by the file (no year anywhere); pages are the essay's 'Page N of 15'. The graph should flag it as non-academic; three of its five nodes attribute to Currado what he presents as patristic consensus or merely block-quotes (Hippolytus, ANF translation), and one node inserts a Greek lemma absent from the source. (5) Several nodes import modern labels the authors do not use: 'compatibilism' (Gill), 'double predestination' (Dunn), 'libertarian' (Dunn node 0), 'free will/cooperative' (Dettwiler 1998), 'markers of ethnic identity' (Eskola — the very reading he rejects). Greek in this report is limited to the three terms copied verbatim from Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:130-131.

### `scholarly_argument_currado_divine_election_and_foreknowle_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Divine election and foreknowledge — God's election is based on foreseen qualities and human response, not arbitrary divine decree. 'Called according to purpose' (Romans 8:28) lacks a possessive pronoun in Greek and can refer to God's purposeful decision to call those He foreknew would freely choose to respond. The gift of faith is universal—everyone can submit to or resist God's grace
- Constat : Quote is verbatim on the essay's p. 3, but it sits in the list headed 'Introduction to Chrysostom and Origen's commentary on Romans 9', which Currado introduces as the matters on which 'they both have a clear consensus'. The node presents this as Currado's own thesis and drops the framing (it is his summary of what Chrysostom and Origen agree on, which he endorses). 'Called according to purpose' pronoun remark and 'gift of faith is universal' are in the same list. Pages are the essay's internal 'Page N of 15' pagination; the work is an undated, non-peer-reviewed essay by Micah Currado.
  - « Nevertheless, they both have a clear consensus in the following matters: » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:133-135] (Currado, p. 3)
  - « God’s Election is based on foreseen qualities – God “will render to every man according to His deeds” (Romans 2:6) – those who yield to Him in humility (while not deserving His grace) He grants fellowship with Himself; those who resist Him, He condemns. Election is not arbitrary. » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:151-153] (Currado, p. 3)
  - « The gift and potential of faith toward God is universal – everyone can submit to the grace and goodness of God or can resist Him. » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:147-148] (Currado, p. 3)
- Description proposée : Summarising what he takes to be the shared position of Chrysostom and Origen on Romans 9, Currado lists: election 'is based on foreseen qualities' and 'is not arbitrary'; 'called according to purpose' (no possessive pronoun in the Greek, he notes) can mean God's decision to call those he foreknew would respond; the 'gift and potential of faith' is universal, so everyone can yield to or resist God. Currado endorses this consensus as his own reading.
- Pages : 3

### `scholarly_argument_currado_early_church_consensus_on_free_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Early Church consensus on free will — The early church fathers consistently affirmed libertarian free will as foundational to Christian anthropology, even in their catechetical instruction; this consensus was maintained despite engagement with diverse philosophical systems (Stoic, Platonic, Jewish, etc.)
- Constat : Quote verbatim, p. 1. 'Libertarian' is Currado's own word ('an essentially libertarian view of the will which is at odds with Calvinism'). The claim about diverse philosophical systems is on pp. 1-2 ('numerous philosophical models of free will and determinism'; Josephus on Pharisees/Sadducees/Essenes; Hippolytus). Note the polemical, non-academic character of the piece (anti-Calvinist web essay, no date, no peer review).

  - « In consensus, they affirm an essentially libertarian view of the will which is at odds with Calvinism » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:23-24] (Currado, p. 1)
  - « the early church had to interact with numerous philosophical models of free will and determinism within Greco-Roman culture. There was no monolithic “pagan view.” » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:34-35] (Currado, p. 1)

### `scholarly_argument_currado_hippolytus_on_human_self_deter_4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Hippolytus on human self-determination — Hippolytus presents a Christian anthropology where humans possess 'capacity of self-determination' (αὐτεξούσιον); evil arises from human misuse of this capacity, not from God. God created humans with free will and gave law for guidance, not to enforce necessity but to summon humans to liberty through spontaneous choice
- Constat : Currado offers no analysis of Hippolytus: he block-quotes the ANF translation of Refutatio X ('Chapter 28-29' in his heading) and moves on. The content of the node matches the quoted translation, but (a) it is Hippolytus' claim in a 19th-c. translation, not a 'Currado' argument, and (b) the Greek term inserted in the node does not occur anywhere in the source file (English only) — it is an addition. The block quotation spans pp. 2-3 of the essay.
  - « Now man, that was brought into existence, was a creature endued with a capacity of self-determination, yet not possessing a sovereign intellect » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:104-105] (Currado, p. 2)
  - « man has free will, a law has been defined for his guidance by the Deity, not without answering a good purpose. » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:112-113] (Currado, p. 3)
  - « not bringing him into servitude by force of necessity, but summoning him to liberty through a choice involving spontaneity. » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:121-123] (Currado, p. 3)
- Description proposée : Currado reproduces, without comment, the ANF translation of Hippolytus, Refutatio X (his 'Chapter 28-29'): man was created 'endued with a capacity of self-determination' though 'a slave to his passions'; evil arises 'from the fact of his possessing a capacity of self-determination' and had no existence from the beginning; because man 'has free will' God gave a law for his guidance, the Word 'summoning him to liberty through a choice involving spontaneity', not 'by force of necessity'. No Greek is given in the source.
- Pages : 2-3

### `scholarly_argument_currado_irenaeus_on_pharaoh_s_hardenin_3` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Irenaeus on Pharaoh's hardening — Irenaeus explicitly rejects the interpretation that God arbitrarily hardens Pharaoh's heart, identifying this view as heretical
- Constat : Faithful to Currado's own claim (p. 1), which he backs on pp. 13-14 with a long quotation of Irenaeus, AH IV, the chapter 'Refutation of the arguments of the Marcionites, who attempted to show that God was the author of sin, because He blinded Pharaoh'. Caveat for users: in the passage as quoted, Irenaeus does not deny that God hardened Pharaoh; he explains it by foreknowledge of unbelief. 'Arbitrary hardener' is Currado's gloss, and the Marcionite target concerns Exodus, not Romans 9 as such.
  - « Irenaeus specifically says that an interpretation of Romans 9 which makes God the arbitrary hardener of Pharaoh's heart is a view held by heretics » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:24-25] (Currado, p. 1)
  - « God, knowing the number of those who will not believe, since He foreknows all things, has given them over to unbelief, and turned away His face from men of this stamp, leaving them in the darkness which they have themselves chosen for themselves » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:649-652] (Currado, p. 13, quoting Irenaeus AH IV)

### `scholarly_argument_currado_romans_interpretation_in_early_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Romans 9 interpretation in early church — Romans 9 does not reject libertarian free will; rather, Paul employs a rhetorical strategy of confusing a hostile interlocutor (9:15-23) before providing the clear answer in 9:30-10:4. The 'potter and clay' imagery does not support unconditional election but rather shows God responding to human free choices—those who flee from God become vessels of wrath by their own will, not by divine arbitrary decree
- Constat : The quote_verbatim is on the essay's p. 15 (Summary), not p. 3. The rhetorical-strategy claims (confusing the hostile inquisitor in 9:15-23, clear answer in 9:30-10:4, people 'their own cause of being formed into a vessel for wrath') are on p. 3 and are framed by Currado as the consensus of Chrysostom and Origen, not as his own exegesis. The node also drops the Summary's key formula: Romans 9 rejects 'two errors: legalistic works-salvation and ethnocentric entitlement'.
  - « Paul is not about to provide a clear answer until he leaves his opponent speechless, confused, and thus receptive to the answer. This is the point of Romans 9:15 – 9:23. » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:137-138] (Currado, p. 3)
  - « While God is most certainly the Potter, people are ultimately their own cause of being formed into a vessel for wrath, of their own libertarian free will. » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:143-144] (Currado, p. 3)
  - « they did not see Romans 9 as a rejection of libertarian free will. They saw it as a rejection of two errors: legalistic works-salvation and ethnocentric entitlement. » [Currado_EarlyChurch_Romans9.md:737-738] (Currado, p. 15)
- Description proposée : Currado attributes to Chrysostom and Origen a shared reading (p. 3): Paul confronts a 'hostile inquisitor' relying on Jewish heritage, deliberately confuses and silences him in 9:15-23, and gives the 'clearest answer' in 9:30-10:4; God is the Potter, but people become vessels of wrath through their own choice to flee from God. His Summary (p. 15) concludes that the Fathers read Romans 9 not as rejecting 'libertarian free will' but as rejecting 'legalistic works-salvation and ethnocentric entitlement'.
- Pages : 3; 15

Intro (pp. 26-27): written by a disciple shortly after Paul's death; §1.1 (p. 28): years 70-80, and the 'collaborator during Paul's lifetime' hypothesis judged 'peu convaincante'. Printed pages read from the running folios in the file (the page_map for this archive-ouverte PDF returns PDF indices at conf 0.50 and must not be used).
  - « L'épître aux Colossiens n'a pas été écrite par Paul lui-même, mais par un de ses disciples, peu après la mort de l'apôtre. » [L'épître aux Colossiens. Un exemple de réception de la théologie paulinienne.md:56-58] (Dettwiler 1995, pp. 26-27)
  - « L'épître aux Colossiens fut écrite après la mqrt de Paul. Il faut penser aux années 70-80 » [L'épître aux Colossiens. Un exemple de réception de la théologie paulinienne.md:110-111] (Dettwiler 1995, p. 28)
  - « Selon certains exégètes, un collaborateur de Paul aurait formulé Col du vivant de l'apôtre, éventuellement durant la captivité dite éphésienne, au milieu des années 50. Mais cette hypothèse me paraît peu convaincante. » [L'épître aux Colossiens. Un exemple de réception de la théologie paulinienne.md:114-117] (Dettwiler 1995, p. 28)

Un exemple de réception de la théologie paulinienne.md:147-151] (Dettwiler 1995, p. 30)

- Nœud actuel : christological foundation of faith — The act of faith, understood as trust/confidence (confiance), is fundamentally determined by Matthew's christology of 'Emmanuel' (God-with-us); the christological dimension is the foundation of faith, with Jesus as divine and sovereign being

Node's 333-335 misses 344-345.
