### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_faith_and_doubt_in_matthew_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : faith and doubt in Matthew — Matthew's conception of faith does not simply distinguish between faith and unbelief, but also reflects on aspects such as doubt and fear within faith itself, accounting for the ambiguity of human experience where faith and doubt are intimately linked
- Constat : Verbatim in the introduction, p. 334; developed in §V.3 'La crise de la foi' p. 346 (oligopistia = 'l'incrédulité au sein de la foi'; 'Le doute naît plutôt au sein de l'acte de foi').
  - « elle ne distingue pas simplement entre foi et incrédulité, mais en ce qu'elle réfléchit également à des aspects tels que le doute et la crainte à l'intérieur de la foi. De cette manière, elle rend compte de façon impressionnante de l'ambiguïté de cette expérience humaine où foi et doute sont intimement liés. » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:103-107] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 334)
  - « Le doute naît plutôt au sein de l'acte de foi et constitue alors un élément qui lui est indissolublement lié. » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:442-443] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 346)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_free_will_and_human_agency_in__3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and human agency in faith — The text implies that faith involves human initiative and response—Peter actively requests to come to Jesus, steps out of the boat, and ultimately cries out in prayer; yet this human agency is contingent on Jesus' initiative and presence, suggesting a cooperative dynamic where human faith-response is enabled by divine grace
- Constat : Dettwiler says the opposite of 'human initiative' and 'cooperative dynamic': Peter's walk 'ne saurait résulter d'une initiative propre à Pierre', it is 'exclusivement rendue possible par la parole de Jésus' (p. 336); faith 'n'est pas un acte humain fondé en lui-même', 'n'est pas à elle-même son propre commencement' (p. 344); it is 'pas un acte dont l'homme serait maître' and consists in Peter renouncing to walk by himself (p. 345); praying, Peter 'ne peut plus recourir à ses propres possibilités et capacités' (p. 347). 'Free will', 'coopération', 'synergie' never occur in the article. Peter's request is read as a faith-filled response, not an initiative.
  - « cette proximité de Pierre avec Jésus ne saurait résulter d'une initiative propre à Pierre : la marche de Pierre sur les eaux est exclusivement rendue possible par la parole de Jésus » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:223-225] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 336)
  - « la foi est comprise comme une réponse. Elle n'est pas un acte humain fondé en lui-même, mais un acte conditionné par l'expérience de la présence salvatrice de Jésus. Elle n'est pas à elle-même son propre commencement » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:397-400] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 344)
  - « cette foi qui transgresse toutes les limites n'est, en définitive, pas un acte dont l'homme serait maître, ni un acte témoignant d'un héroïsme extraordinaire. » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:381-383] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 345)
- Description proposée : Dettwiler reads Peter's request ('Seigneur, si c'est toi...') as a faith-filled response, not an initiative: his walk 'ne saurait résulter d'une initiative propre à Pierre' and is made possible 'exclusivement' by Jesus' word (p. 336). Faith is 'une réponse', 'pas un acte humain fondé en lui-même', not its own beginning (p. 344); it is not an act of which man is master but a renunciation of walking by oneself and of all securities (p. 345); in the cry-prayer Peter can no longer draw on his own capacities (p. 347). No language of free will or cooperation.
- Pages : 336; 344-345; 347

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_moral_responsibility_and_failu_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : moral responsibility and failure — Peter's failure is attributed to his 'little faith' (oligopistia), a recurring Matthean concept, indicating that the disciples are morally responsible for their inadequate faith-response; yet the narrative emphasizes Jesus' saving action rather than condemnation, suggesting that failure is integrated into the faith journey rather than being its endpoint
- Constat : Dettwiler does say Peter recreated the distance 'de façon coupable' (p. 336) and that Jesus saves him, but the node loses his central qualifications: oligopistia is not apistia (the crowd is unbelieving, not the disciples), it is not a 'small' faith but 'l'incrédulité au sein de la foi'; the text does not demand 'de façon moraliste' a pure faith; doubt is neither idealised nor glorified, and faith 'vise à surmonter le doute' (p. 346). 'Morally responsible' is the node's vocabulary, not Dettwiler's. The oligopistia discussion is on pp. 346-347, not 334-335.
  - « Le manque de foi (oligopistia), ensuite, n'est pas à comprendre comme une foi petite à laquelle s'opposerait une foi plus grande (cf Mt 17119-20 !). L' oligopistia doit plutôt être comprise comme l'expression. de l'incrédulité au sein de la foi » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:423-426] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 346)
  - « il n'est pas demandé de façon moraliste d'avoir une foi absolument pure, une foi qui ne serait pas affectée par le doute. » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:446-447] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 346)
  - « une distance que Pierre a recréée de façon coupable » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:232-233] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 336)
- Description proposée : Peter's failure is 'oligopistia', a key Matthean concept distinct from apistia (unbelief, reserved for the crowd): not a smaller faith but 'l'incrédulité au sein de la foi', born of the tension between Jesus' word and the evidence of the world. Dettwiler stresses that Matthew does not demand 'de façon moraliste' a faith untouched by doubt — the failing Peter is precisely the one saved — yet doubt is neither idealised nor glorified: faith ultimately 'vise à surmonter le doute'. Peter recreates the distance 'de façon coupable' (p. 336).
- Pages : 336; 346-347

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_stages_of_the_faith_journey_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : stages of the faith journey — The narrative of Matthew 14 presents a paradigmatic journey of faith with four main stages: (1) christological foundation, (2) trust/confidence, (3) crisis (doubt/fear), and (4) restructuring of faith in the act of prayer
- Constat : The four stages are the chapeau's own list (p. 333) and the four headings of §V (pp. 344-347): '1 - Le fondement de la foi', '2 - La confiance de la foi', '3 - La crise de la foi', '4 - La prière de la foi'. 'Paradigmatic' is Dettwiler's ('à valeur paradigmatique'). Author confirmed in the file header.
  - « Le fondement christologique, la confiance, la crise et finalement la restructuration de la foi dans l'acte de la prière en constituent les étapes principales » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:64-65] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 333)
  - « décrire le parcours de la foi, à valeur paradigmatique, proposé par Mt 14. » [La conception matthéenne de la foi (Mt 14,22-33).md:63] (Dettwiler 1998, p. 333)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_constellative_anthropology_3` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Constellative anthropology — As a man of antiquity, Paul shares the premodern vision that the human being is not an abstract entity in itself but always a being in relation, defined by social, political, cultural and religious constellations; the human being is not first an isolated individual but inscribed in networks of relations that shape and determine them
- Constat : Verbatim, §1.5, p. 3 (page from the document's own footer). The second half of the node ('not first an isolated individual but inscribed in constellations of relations') is Konrad Schmid's formulation (SCHMID 2019, 375-376, trans. Dettwiler), quoted approvingly; 'anthropologie constellative' is Schmid's term after Assmann. The source is a provisional, unpublished UNIGE text ('texte tout à fait provisoire, non destiné à être publié').
  - « Paul participe aussi à la vision largement partagée à l’époque prémoderne selon laquelle l’être humain est perçu non comme une entité abstraite en soi, mais toujours comme un être en relation » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:138-140] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 3)
  - « Konrad Schmid, à la suite des travaux de l’égyptologue allemand Jan Assmann (ASSMANN 2009), parle d’anthropologie constellative » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:141-143] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 3)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_hebraic_vs_hellenistic_anthrop_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Hebraic vs. Hellenistic anthropology — Paul participates in Hebrew monistic anthropology that views the human being as a totality rather than through body-soul dualism; however, one should avoid fruitless oppositions between 'Hebraic' and 'Greco-Roman' thought as two unrelated universes
- Constat : §1.3 (monistic Hebrew anthropology, quoting Jacob 1973 and Becker 1995) and §1.4 (avoid 'oppositions infructueuses' between Hebrew and Greco-Roman thought; Second Temple Judaism internally plural, Henze) are both on p. 2. Node's 2-3 slightly wide.
  - « participe à la pensée hébraïque qui conçoit l’être humain dans sa totalité : « L’anthropologie israélite est moniste. » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:79-80] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 2)
  - « Il convient pourtant d’éviter des oppositions infructueuses entre « la » pensée hébraïque et « la » pensée gréco-romaine comme s’il s’agissait de deux univers de pensée qui n’auraient rien à voir l’un avec l’autre. » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:93-95] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 2)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_hellenistic_influence_on_pauli_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Hellenistic influence on Pauline anthropology — Despite Hebrew monistic tendencies, Paul's literature is also permeated by specifically Greco-Roman anthropological elements; Paul extensively uses typically Greek concepts like σῶμα (body), νοῦς (mind/reason), and συνείδησις (conscience) which have no direct Hebrew equivalent; some passages (2 Cor 5:1ff; 12:2-4; 1 Cor 7:1-7) show more direct Hellenistic influence with tendency to devalue corporeality following Platonic body-soul dualism
- Constat : Quote verbatim, p. 3. But the second claim (2 Co 5,1ss; 12,2-4; 1 Co 7,1-7 showing a more direct Hellenistic influence devaluing corporeality) is reported in the conditional and attributed to Bultmann ('selon Rudolf Bultmann, plusieurs passages ... témoigneraient'), not asserted by Dettwiler; the node states it flat. The three Greek terms are in the file as σῶµα, νοῦς, συνείδησις.
  - « Déjà le fait que Paul utilise de manière extensive des concepts typiquement grecs comme σῶµα (« corps »), νοῦς (« pensée, raison, entendement ») ou συνείδησις (« conscience morale ») qui n’ont pas d’équivalent direct en hébreu plaide dans ce sens. » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:129-132] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 3)
  - « De plus, selon Rudolf Bultmann, plusieurs passages (2 Co 5,1ss ; 2 Co 12,2-4 ; mais aussi 1 Co 7,1-7) témoigneraient d’une influence plus directe de la pensée hellénistique » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:132-134] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 3)
- Description proposée : Because Paul's Judaism, like any minority culture of the time, is influenced by Hellenistic culture, Pauline literature is also 'imprégnée' by specifically Greco-Roman anthropological elements: Paul's extensive use of typically Greek concepts (body, mind/reason, moral conscience) without direct Hebrew equivalent 'plaide dans ce sens'. Dettwiler then reports, in the conditional and on Bultmann's authority (BULTMANN 1984, 202-203), that some passages (2 Co 5,1ss; 12,2-4; 1 Co 7,1-7) would attest a more direct Hellenistic influence tending to devalue corporeality along Platonic body-soul dualism.
- Pages : 3

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_pauline_anthropology_and_syste_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Pauline anthropology and systematic treatment — Paul does not develop a systematic anthropology; his anthropological concepts are never abstract themes of reflection but are always tied to specific situational contexts and theological concerns
- Constat : Verbatim, §1.1, p. 1. The 'never a subject of reflection / never theoretically about man' sentence is Bornkamm (1971, 186) quoted with approval; the warning against over-systematising and the 'écrits de circonstances' point are Dettwiler's own, with Brookins 2018 added. Pages 1 only (p. 2 begins at §1.2's continuation).
  - « N’attendons pas de Paul une anthropologie développée, dans le sens où Paul analyserait systématiquement les différents concepts de l’être humain que sa culture lui a mis à disposition. » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:28-30] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 1)
  - « N’oublions pas que ses écrits sont, sans exception aucune, des écrits de circonstances qui présupposent un contexte d’énonciation précis » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:35-36] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 1)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_theological_anthropological_in_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Theological-anthropological integration — Pauline theology is simultaneously anthropology; every statement about God is simultaneously a statement about the human being and vice versa; anthropology and theology are inseparable in Paul
- Constat : §1.2, pp. 1-2. Dettwiler's own sentence is 'Anthropologie et théologie sont, chez Paul, étroitement liées'; the 'every sentence about God is also a sentence about man... Pauline theology is at the same time anthropology' is Bultmann quoted (n. 2 gives the German, BULTMANN 1984, 191-192), followed by Dunn 1998, 51. Node correctly flags this as a restatement of Bultmann.
  - « Anthropologie et théologie sont, chez Paul, étroitement liées l’une à l’autre. Rudolf Bultmann, dans sa Théologie du Nouveau Testament, l’a dit de manière limpide » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:50-51] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 1)
  - « Chaque phrase sur Dieu est en même temps une phrase sur l’être humain et vice versa. Pour cette raison et dans ce sens, la théologie paulinienne est en même temps une anthropologie »2. » [Paul termes anthropologiques Dettwiler 2020.md:69-71] (Dettwiler 2020, p. 2)

### `scholarly_argument_dunn_free_will_and_determinism_in_p_0` — **NOT_FOUND** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and determinism in Paul — Dunn does not systematically address free will vs. determinism as a philosophical problem; his focus is on Paul's theology of grace, faith, and the Spirit's enabling power rather than on individual libertarian free will
- Constat : This is a meta-observation about what Dunn does not do, not an argument of Dunn's, and no passage supports it as a claim. 'Libertarian' is imported vocabulary. §18 'The eschatological tension' (node's page range) is about flesh/Spirit and the divided I, not about free will as a philosophical problem.
  - « rg -n -i "free.?will|freedom of (the )?will|determinis|libertarian" → 0 hits in the whole file » [Dunn_Theology_Paul_Apostle_1998.md] (search result, no line)
- Description proposée : No corresponding argument in Dunn. ### `scholarly_argument_dunn_grace_and_human_initiative_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)

- Constat : Quote verbatim, but on printed p. 379 (end of §14.7; page_map says 377 — the map for this file is 2-3 pages low against the PDF folios and the file is AUDIT-FAIL). The appended gloss misframes Dunn: he does not say works of the law are excluded 'but human effort is not'; in §14.6 (p. 370) he argues it is 'difficult to sustain the claim that Paul was polemicizing against "self-achieved righteousness"' — the issue is Israel's works of the law vis-à-vis Gentile acceptability. Gal 5.6 is treated in §23.3 (pp. 637-638), where Dunn insists 'faith working through love' does not turn faith into a 'work' nor compromise the sola fide.
  - « It was a profound conception of the relation between God and humankind — a relation of utter dependence, of unconditional trust. Human dependence on divine grace had to be unqualified or else it was not Abraham's faith » [Dunn_Theology_Paul_Apostle_1998.md:23064-23066] (Dunn 1998, p. 379)

  - « This is not to imply (to use the terminology of later centuries) that Paul turned faith into a "work" or compromised the sola fide. » [Dunn_Theology_Paul_Apostle_1998.md:36587-36589] (Dunn 1998, p. 638)
- Description proposée : Justification by faith alone is, for Dunn, 'a relation of utter dependence, of unconditional trust': dependence on grace must be unqualified, which is why Paul opposed works of the law as an accompaniment or addition to faith (p. 379). Dunn denies that Paul was polemicising against 'self-achieved righteousness'; the issue is Israel's works of the law and Gentile acceptability (§14.6, p. 370). Gal 5.6, 'faith operating effectively through love', is a single concept, 'love-energized-faith', that does not make faith a 'work' nor compromise the sola fide (§23.3, pp. 637-638).

### `scholarly_argument_dunn_predestination_and_election_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)

Rom 11.25-26 is §19.6, not checked here.

### `scholarly_argument_eskola_human_moral_agency_and_sin_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)

But 'requires divine coercion for salvation' misreads Eskola's 'divine coercion' (§15.3, p. 303): it is God's act of imprisoning the whole of humankind in disobedience (Gal 3:22; Rom 11:32) so as to be merciful to all — a premise of the theodicy, not a coercion of individuals unto salvation. Reihe)-Wipf and Stock.md:8843-8845] (Eskola 1998, p. 187)

Reihe)-Wipf and Stock.md:13888-13891] (Eskola 1998, p. 303)

Reihe)-Wipf and Stock.md:14170-14172] (Eskola 1998, p. 310)
- Description proposée : Predestinarian theology explains the law's 'paradoxical function': God's good law kills instead of giving life (§10, p. 190). The 'works of the law' are the Avodat Israel, Jewish service to God — in Judaism a positive term for a life of obedience, in Paul's soteriology 'utterly negative in respect of salvation' (§10.3, p. 208). ### `scholarly_argument_gill_later_ancient_reception_of_sto_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : later ancient reception of Stoic free will — The Stoic complex of ideas influenced later Greco-Roman and Christian thought: stimulated Alexander of Aphrodisias to formulate an indeterminist Aristotelian conception; adopted by Plotinus within his metaphysical framework; influenced Christian thinkers Origen (all humans have free will, not just capacity) and Augustine (capacity limited by original sin, requiring divine grace)
- Constat : The paragraph opens 'According to Frede, this complex of ideas exercised a powerful influence on later ancient thought' — Gill is reporting Frede's thesis (which he goes on to call 'highly controversial'), not arguing it. The node turns Gill's report into Gill's position. Content otherwise accurate (Alexander's 'competing Aristotelian-style ("indeterminist") conception'; Plotinus 'wholesale... as far as his metaphysical framework allowed'; Origen: all humans have a free will; Augustine: capacity limited by original sin, needing divine grace). Pages 797-798 correct (Gill, European Legacy 19.6, 2014).
  - « According to Frede, this complex of ideas exercised a powerful inﬂuence on later ancient thought, both Greco-Roman and Christian. » [CR de GILL A Free Will  Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought.md:152-155] (Gill 2014, p. 797)
  - « they modiﬁed the Stoic view either by maintaining that all humans have a free will, not simply the capacity to develop one (Origen), or by claiming that this capacity has been limited by an “original sin,” hence requiring the intervention of “divine grace” to allow it to be realised fully (Augustine). » [CR de GILL A Free Will  Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought.md:174-180] (Gill 2014, p. 798)
- Description proposée : Gill summarises Frede's claim ('According to Frede') that the Stoic complex of ideas powerfully influenced later thought: it stimulated Alexander of Aphrodisias to a competing Aristotelian-style, 'indeterminist' conception of will; was adopted 'wholesale' by Plotinus so far as his metaphysics allowed; and shaped Christian thinkers, who modified it either by holding that all humans have a free will, not merely the capacity to develop one (Origen), or that this capacity is limited by 'original sin' and needs 'divine grace' (Augustine). Gill then judges the study 'highly controversial'.
- Pages : 797-798

### `scholarly_argument_gill_methodological_concerns_about__4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)

- Constat : Quote verbatim, p. 798.   - « For anyone interested in the emergence of the idea of free will in early European thought this short but powerfully stated study is indispensable. » [CR de GILL A Free Will  Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought.md:199-202] (Gill 2014, p. 798)
- Description proposée : Gill judges Frede's study 'bold and original, arresting in its claims and powerful in argumentation', yet 'also highly controversial and open to challenge, for instance' in its stress on the pervasive influence of Stoicism, or in the move — 'which some would see as a retrojection' — of analysing Stoic theory in the later ancient terminology of will and free will. ### `scholarly_argument_gill_stoic_conception_of_will_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic conception of will — The Stoic conception of will is more rational than the 'voluntarist' or 'volitional' idea presupposed by Dihle; all adult human beings are constitutively capable of exercising will and developing complete wisdom or 'freedom' of the will through liberating oneself from false ethical beliefs
- Constat : Quote verbatim, p. 797 (the '(8)' is Gill citing Frede's p. 8). The comparison with Dihle's 'voluntarist' idea is Gill's own gloss, but the whole paragraph answers 'What is that conceptual content?' — i.e. it expounds Frede's reconstruction of the Stoic/Epictetan notion (prohairesis, assent to a rational impression, freedom as liberation from 'self-enslavement'). The node presents it as Gill's independent position on Stoic will.

  - « This freedom depends on liberating oneself from the “self-enslavement” that consists in subjecting oneself to false and misguided ethical beliefs. » [CR de GILL A Free Will  Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought.md:135-138] (Gill 2014, p. 797)
- Description proposée : Expounding Frede's account, Gill explains that the will is 'our ability to make choices or decisions which make us act in the way we do' (Frede, p. 8), expressed in Stoic terms, especially by Epictetus, as prohairesis, with motivation depending on 'assent' to a 'rational impression'. Gill adds that this conception is 'thus more rational than the "voluntarist" or "volitional" idea presupposed by Dihle'; all adult humans are constitutively capable of exercising will and of developing 'freedom' of the will by liberating themselves from 'self-enslavement' to false ethical beliefs.
- Pages : 797

### `scholarly_argument_guillon_anglo_american_revival_of_inco_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Anglo-American revival of incompatibilism — In Anglo-American philosophy, the free will debate seemed definitively closed by a broad compatibilist consensus since Hobbes and Hume, but the 1970s-1980s saw the reappearance of an extremely lively controversy where nearly the full variety of positions from other eras of philosophy's history could be found again
- Constat : Sentence reproduced almost word for word from Guillon's Avant-propos (§4). Two errors of apparatus: the text is the 'Avant-propos', not an 'Introduction', and it stands on printed p. 5 (running folio '5' at the head of the page; PDF index 7), not 'pp. 1-5'. The same sentence recurs in the RÉSUMÉS on p. 1 (PDF index 3), which is probably how the range 1-5 arose.
  - « Dans les pays anglo-saxons en particulier, alors que le débat sur le libre arbitre semblait avoir été définitivement clos depuis Hobbes et Hume par un large consensus « compatibiliste », les années 1970-1980 ont vu réapparaître une controverse extrêmement animée » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/cdf-4937_OCR.md:298-301] (Guillon 2017, p. 5)

### `scholarly_argument_guillon_compatibilism_vs_incompatibili_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : compatibilism vs incompatibilism — The contemporary debate on free will is best understood through the traditional opposition between compatibilist and incompatibilist conceptions; compatibilism holds that free will is compatible with causal determinism, while incompatibilism (which has made a strong comeback since the 1970s-1980s) defends the intuition that determinism would deprive us of free will
- Constat : Guillon presents the compatibilist/incompatibilist opposition as a convenient sorting device ('il est commode de recourir à l'opposition traditionnelle'), not as the way the debate 'is best understood'. The node drops his two qualifications: compatibilism holds free will 'lorsqu'il est bien conçu' compatible with determinism 'de telle sorte qu'il n'y a nullement à s'inquiéter d'une éventuelle preuve du déterminisme par la science', and incompatibilist conceptions were 'largement majoritaires - peut-être seules représentées - avant le XVIIème siècle'. Page: p. 6 of the Avant-propos (§5), also in the résumé p. 1; not 'pp. 1-5'.
  - « Pour y voir plus clair dans cette variété de positions, il est commode de recourir à l'opposition traditionnelle entre « conceptions compatibilistes » du libre arbitre et « conceptions incompatibilistes ». » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/cdf-4937_OCR.md:314-316] (Guillon 2017, p. 6)
  - « Les conceptions incompatibilistes au contraire (qui étaient largement majoritaires - peut-être seules représentées - avant le XVIIème siècle, et qui font l'objet d'un retour en force depuis une quarantaine d'années) défendent par divers arguments l'intuition selon laquelle le déterminisme nous priverait du libre arbitre. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/cdf-4937_OCR.md:319-323] (Guillon 2017, p. 6)
- Description proposée : To sort the contemporary 'mosaïque de positions', Guillon finds it convenient ('commode') to use the traditional opposition between 'conceptions compatibilistes' and 'conceptions incompatibilistes'. Compatibilist conceptions, inherited mainly from Hobbes, Locke and Hume, hold that free will, when properly conceived, is compatible with causal determinism, so that a scientific proof of determinism need cause no worry. Incompatibilist conceptions, in the large majority (perhaps alone represented) before the 17th century and enjoying a 'retour en force' for some forty years, defend by various arguments the intuition that determinism would deprive us of free will.
- Pages : 6 (Avant-propos §5; also résumé p. 1)

### `scholarly_argument_list_determinism_fate_and_moral_res_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : determinism, fate, and moral responsibility in relation to transmigration — The doctrine of transmigration (metempsychosis) rejected by Justin in Dialogue 4 was foundational to the theological systems of his Christian demiurgical opponents (Simon and Helen, Basilides, Carpocrates), and Justin's rejection of this doctrine served to undermine their teachings and define Christianity on his own terms; the transmigration doctrine has implications for moral responsibility and the nature of the soul's journey
- Constat : The first half is List's thesis (abstract p. 1; §2 pp. 9-16; conclusion p. 21). The node's title ('determinism, fate, and moral responsibility') and its closing clause ('the transmigration doctrine has implications for moral responsibility') are not in List: the article contains no occurrence of 'fate', 'determinism', 'free will' or 'responsibility' (rg over the whole file). What List does discuss is judgement: for Basilides, martyrs 'suffering for sins in a previous embodiment'; for Justin, animals 'benefit in no way ... from such punishment' (Dial. 4.7). '§§3-4' in the reference are section numbers, not pages.
  - « The Platonic anthropology rejected by Justin happens to be foundational to the theological systems of his Christian demiurgical opponents: Simon and Helen, Basilides, and Carpocrates » [04_Apologistes_Justin/List_2024_Justin_Problem_Platonism_EN.md:35-37] (List 2024, p. 1)
  - « the appropriation of Platonic anthropology by Christian Demiurgists was integrally tied to a host of broader theological concerns, whether it be christology (Carpocrates' Jesus as remembering the forms), divine judgement (Basilides' martyrs suffering for sins in a previous embodiment), or cosmology » [04_Apologistes_Justin/List_2024_Justin_Problem_Platonism_EN.md:782-787] (List 2024, p. 16)
- Description proposée : List argues that the two Platonic doctrines Justin rejects in Dial. 4 (the soul's vision of God; transmigration) 'happen to be foundational' to the anthropologies of Carpocrates, Basilides and Simon/Helen, teachers probably refuted in the lost Syntagma. Their use of transmigration was tied to christology, 'divine judgement' (Basilides' martyrs suffering for sins of a previous embodiment) and cosmology. Justin's rejection was therefore 'religious competition', undermining rivals and defining Christianity on his own terms. List says nothing about fate, determinism or moral responsibility.

### `scholarly_argument_matteoli_free_will_libero_arbitrio_in_p_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will (libero arbitrio) in Pelagius — Pelagius defended free will against deterministic readings of Paul, particularly in Romans 1:24, 11:8, 7:14-21, and 9:16-24; the Expositiones show this was already central before the Augustine controversy
- Constat : Quote verbatim (p. 118) and the passages listed are indeed ch. 4 §1 (pp. 118-135). 'Deterministic readings' is Matteoli's own vocabulary ('visione deterministica', 'lettura in senso deterministico'). But the node loses her central conclusion: on free will Pelagius 'nella sostanza non innova' — the defence of human freedom is 'tema comune praticamente a tutte le fonti di Pelagio', he follows his predecessors on Rm 9, and it is rather Augustine from the Ad Simplicianum on who introduces a new perspective. 'Already central' is the node's phrasing, not hers.
  - « Del resto, la difesa della libertà dell'uomo è tema comune praticamente a tutte le fonti di Pelagio : per quanto concerne quella che diverrà una delle tematiche fondamentali del pelagianesimo nei suoi sviluppi successivi, non si riscontra nelle Expositiones una presa di posizione innovativa o estremista che rompa con la tradizione precedente. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:8614-8617] (Matteoli 2011, p. 190)
  - « Senza dubbio Pelagio avverte più dei suoi predecessori il problema della difesa del libero arbitrio, ma nella sostanza non innova, inserendosi al contrario in una tradizione di pensiero ben consolidata. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:8633-8635] (Matteoli 2011, p. 191)
- Description proposée : Matteoli reads the Expositiones on Rm 1,24 and 11,8 (God 'abandons' sinners to safeguard free will), 7,14-21 and 9,16-24 as Pelagius's defence of 'libero arbitrio' against a 'lettura in senso deterministico' of Paul, before the controversy with Augustine. Her conclusion, however, is that here Pelagius 'nella sostanza non innova': the defence of human freedom is common to practically all his sources (Origen above all), he keeps to his predecessors' explanations of Rm 9, and it is Augustine, from the Ad Simplicianum, who introduces a different perspective.
- Pages : 117-135, 190-191

### `scholarly_argument_matteoli_grace_gratia_and_merit_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : grace (gratia) and merit — Pelagius understood grace as forgiveness of sins, reconciliation with God, and adiutorium (help); there is tension in the Expositiones between grace as free gift and as recognition of merits, suggesting Pelagius's doctrine of grace was not simply 'salvation by works alone' but more nuanced
- Constat : Quote verbatim (p. 169; ch. 5 §3 = pp. 166-170, §2 = pp. 162-166). The node keeps 'tension' but drops Matteoli's resolution: the contradiction is 'apparente' and 'si risolve solo distinguendo due momenti diversi nel processo di giustificazione' (sola fide at conversion, works thereafter, cf. Rm 4,5 and Gal 3,10). Her conclusion is that, against expectation, gratia in the Expositiones has several meanings (exemplum Christi prevailing; forgiveness and reconciliation; adiutorium, e.g. Rm 8,26), that the two-phase scheme is attested in Ambrosiaster and Origen, and that this is an 'embryonic' phase of his thought. 'Salvation by works alone' is the node's phrase, not hers.
  - « si risolve solo distinguendo due momenti diversi nel processo di giustificazione. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:7677] (Matteoli 2011, p. 169)
  - « anche se a prevalere è il principio dell'exemplum Christi, non mancano riferimenti alla grazia come perdono dei peccati e riconciliazione con Dio, e alla grazia come adiutorium. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:8642-8644] (Matteoli 2011, p. 191)
- Description proposée : Matteoli finds that gratia in the Expositiones has several meanings: the prevailing exemplum Christi, but also forgiveness of sins and reconciliation with God, and adiutorium (Rm 8,26, the Spirit helping our weak capacities). The 'apparent contradiction' between gratuitous grace and merits, faith alone and works, 'si risolve solo distinguendo due momenti' of justification: sola fide at conversion, works necessary afterwards (Rm 4,5; Gal 3,10) — a scheme already in Ambrosiaster and Origen. She reads this as an embryonic, not yet dogmatic, phase of Pelagius's theology.
- Pages : 157-174 (quote p. 169), 191

### `scholarly_argument_matteoli_impeccantia_sinlessness_4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : impeccantia (sinlessness) — Pelagius believed moral progress toward perfection was possible and that humans could achieve sinlessness (impeccantia) through effort and free will, though this was contested by Jerome and Augustine
- Constat : Quote verbatim (p. 175), but it reports Jerome's term for the later Pelagian doctrine. Matteoli's own finding for the Expositiones is the opposite of the node's flat attribution: passages actually attributable to impeccantia 'sono ben pochi' (only 1Tim 1,5; Rm 6,7; Rm 8,3), 'espressa comunque con estrema cautela', in a phase where the concept 'non ha ancora trovato' its formulation; elsewhere only generic calls to a holy life such as Ambrosiaster also makes. The node turns her cautious, embryonic reading into a settled belief of Pelagius.
  - « Il principio secondo cui l'uomo può, se vuole, essere senza peccato, è espresso da Gerolamo con il termine impeccantia, che ancora oggi è spesso impiegato per indicare la dottrina dei pelagiani. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:7920-7922] (Matteoli 2011, p. 175)
  - « Solo nel commento di Pelagio a 1Tim 1, 5, Rm 6, 7 e Rm 8, 3 si possono ravvisare effettivi riferimenti alla dottrina dell'impeccantia, espressa comunque con estrema cautela. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:8688-8689] (Matteoli 2011, p. 192)
- Description proposée : Matteoli starts from Jerome's term impeccantia (the principle that man, if he wills, can be without sin), contested by Jerome (Ep. 133, Dialogus adversus Pelagianos, In Hieremiam) and Augustine. Her examination of the Expositiones finds that passages genuinely attributable to this doctrine 'sono ben pochi' — only 1Tim 1,5, Rm 6,7 and Rm 8,3 — and are 'espressa comunque con estrema cautela'; most are generic exhortations to a holy life like Ambrosiaster's. The concept has not yet found its full formulation at this stage.
- Pages : 175-188, 192

### `scholarly_argument_matteoli_original_sin_and_human_nature_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : original sin and human nature — Pelagius held that Adam's sin affected Adam alone, not transmitting guilt or corrupted nature to descendants; humans are born with the capacity for sinlessness, and the 'lex naturae' provides sufficient moral guidance
- Constat : Quote verbatim (p. 110; ch. 3 = pp. 55-116). Faithful on Adam: spiritual death 'acquisita tramite i peccati individuali per imitazione', which Matteoli calls Pelagius's 'vera novità'. But 'the lex naturae provides sufficient moral guidance' flattens her ch. 2 thesis: natural law and Mosaic law can guarantee salvation only 'limitatamente al tempo in cui furono in vigore', and each became insufficient because of the 'consuetudo peccatorum' (p. 49). 'Born with the capacity for sinlessness' is not stated in ch. 3.
  - « si tratta esclusivamente della morte spirituale, acquisita tramite i peccati individuali per imitazione della trasgressione del primo uomo. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:4889-4891] (Matteoli 2011, p. 110)
  - « egli attribuisce ad ogni epoca un valore autonomo, ritenendo sia la legge di natura sia la legge mosaica capaci di garantire la salvezza limitatamente al tempo in cui furono in vigore, quindi prima dell'avvento di Cristo. » [06_Patristique/Matteoli_2011_Origini_Teologia_Pelagio_IT.md:8566-8568] (Matteoli 2011, p. 189)
- Description proposée : For Matteoli, Pelagius's 'vera novità' is on Adam's sin: the term mortalitas is absent from his Romans commentary; the death introduced by Adam is exclusively spiritual death, acquired through individual sins 'per imitazione' of the first man, so the contradiction his sources faced (universal sinfulness vs. personal responsibility) is eliminated. On the lex naturae, Pelagius gives each of the three ages (nature, law, faith) an autonomous value: natural law and Mosaic law could guarantee salvation only while in force, before Christ, each later rendered insufficient by the 'consuetudo peccatorum'.
- Pages : 48-49, 55-116 (quote p. 110), 189-190

### `scholarly_argument_moore_josephus_s_philosophical_frami_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Josephus's philosophical framing of Jewish sects — Josephus deliberately presents Jewish sects using Greek philosophical categories (especially Stoic and Epicurean concepts of fate/heimarmene) to make them comprehensible to his Greek-speaking audience, which distorts their actual theological positions
- Constat : Moore never says Josephus 'deliberately' cast the sects in Greek philosophical categories. He calls the origin of the fate-classification 'a matter of guess-work'. His guesses: (a) Josephus's Greek literary assistants chose Heimarmene, Josephus's acquaintance with philosophy being 'hardly ... sufficient to make him aware how incompatible it was with Jewish religious thought', or Josephus used the word meaning 'divine determinism'; (b) his preferred guess: the classification 'came originally from a foreign source', Nicolaus of Damascus, a historian describing Jewish sects 'in terms of current Greek philosophical controversies'. Pages 382-384, not 371-375.
  - « How Josephus came, in the three passages quoted above, to classify the Jewish "philosophies" of his time by their attitude severally toward Fate is a matter of guess-work, and what I have to submit are guesses » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:583-586] (Moore 1929, p. 382)
  - « On the whole, however, I am more inclined to a different guess, namely that the classification of the Jewish 'philosophies' by their attitude toward Fate came originally from a foreign source. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:623-626] (Moore 1929, p. 383)
  - « All these things would be explicable enough in a general historian in Herod's time, who was trying to give his readers a brief account of Jewish sects in terms of current Greek philosophical controversies. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:644-647] (Moore 1929, p. 384)
- Description proposée : Moore holds that Heimarmene in its Stoic definition had no Hebrew equivalent and 'was a conception alien to all Jewish thinking'; Jewish determinism 'was theological, not philosophical'. How Josephus came to classify the sects by attitude to Fate 'is a matter of guess-work'. Either his Greek literary assistants supplied the word (Josephus hardly aware of its incompatibility), or — Moore's preferred guess — the classification came 'from a foreign source', Nicolaus of Damascus, whose brief account of Jewish sects 'in terms of current Greek philosophical controversies' passed into the War and Ant. xviii with Stoic-sounding explanations.
- Pages : 379, 382-384

### `scholarly_argument_moore_pharisee_doctrine_on_fate_and__1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Pharisee doctrine on fate and free will — Josephus presents inconsistent accounts: in Antiquities the Pharisees hold a mixed position (some things by fate, some in human power), while in Jewish War they attribute everything to fate with God as auxiliary, making the earlier War account (c. 75 CE, written two decades before Antiquities) the more deterministic-sounding one; Moore suggests Josephus in Antiquities was trying to clarify but may have distorted the original teaching
- Constat : Several misreadings. (1) In the War the Pharisees 'attribute everything to fate and to God', doing right or wrong 'lies chiefly in men's power, but fate is auxiliary' — it is fate, not God, that is auxiliary. (2) Moore does not rank the War as 'the more deterministic-sounding': Ant. xviii.1.3 also says 'all things are brought about by Fate' while preserving 'the volitional faculty'. (3) Moore says Josephus in Ant. xviii 'is apparently endeavoring to make plainer' the War statement, with explanations that 'do not make the attitude of the Pharisees any plainer to us' — not that he 'distorted the original teaching'. (4) 'c. 75 CE' is not Moore's; he says the War was written twenty years earlier, 'in the first years of his residence in Rome'. Pages 371, 374-375, 383-384.
  - « attribute everything to fate and to God. To do right or the contrary lies chiefly in men's power, but fate is auxiliary in each particular case. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:199-200] (Moore 1929, p. 374)
  - « Coining then to their attitude toward Fate, he is apparently endeavoring to make plainer what he had said on this subject in the War twenty years or more earlier: » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:232-235] (Moore 1929, p. 375)
  - « The striking thing in these passages is that the Jewish sects (as we are in the habit of calling them) should be primarily characterized by their attitude toward fate, and especially strange is the exposition of the doctrine of the Pharisees on this point. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:242-246] (Moore 1929, p. 375)
- Description proposée : Moore sets three Josephan accounts side by side: Ant. xiii.5.9 (some things the work of fate, some 'in men's own power'); War ii.8.14 (the Pharisees 'attribute everything to fate and to God', doing right or wrong 'lies chiefly in men's power, but fate is auxiliary'); Ant. xviii.1.3, where Josephus 'is apparently endeavoring to make plainer' the War statement, though his explanations 'do not make the attitude of the Pharisees any plainer to us'. He finds the Pharisee exposition 'especially strange', asks whether the phrasing (auxiliary causes, impulse, assent) is specifically Chrysippean, and treats the whole classification as guess-work, probably from Nicolaus of Damascus.
- Pages : 371, 374-375, 383-384

### `scholarly_argument_moore_the_fourth_philosophy_and_its__4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : The 'Fourth Philosophy' and its relation to free will — The Fourth Philosophy of Judas the Galilean emphasized human agency ('the Deity would help only those who did their best to help themselves') and made religious principle of political resistance; they agreed with Pharisees in all respects except political intransigence
- Constat : Quote verbatim (p. 373) and the Sadducus/Pharisee point is Moore's. But 'emphasized human agency' is a free-will reading Moore does not make: he reports Josephus's account of Judas's party (God sole sovereign, refusal of tribute, 'a party of action') only because its rise is 'the occasion for the introduction of the three older schools' in War ii.8 and Ant. xviii.1. Moore attaches no doctrine of fate or free will to the Fourth Philosophy; his subject is the three older sects.
  - « The occasion for the introduction of the three older schools in Bell. Jud. ii. 8, 2-14 and in Ant. xviii. 1,1 ff. is the rise of a new party which Josephus calls "the fourth philosophy." » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:110-112] (Moore 1929, p. 372)
  - « Judas associated with himself a certain Sadducus, a Pharisee, and his party are said in all respects except their political intransigeance to have agreed with the Pharisees. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Fate and Free Will in the Jewish Philosophies according to Josephus, George Foot Moore.md:146-148] (Moore 1929, p. 373)
- Description proposée : Moore introduces Judas the Galilean's 'fourth philosophy' only because its rise (census of Quirinius, 6 AD) is 'the occasion for the introduction of the three older schools' in War ii.8 and Ant. xviii.1. Following Josephus, he reports that the party 'made a religious principle of their political theory' (God sole ruler; tribute a recognition of mortal sovereigns), endured torture, assured partisans that 'the Deity would help only those who did their best to help themselves', were 'a party of action', and — Judas having associated the Pharisee Sadducus — agreed with the Pharisees in all but political intransigence. Moore draws no free-will doctrine from this.
- Pages : 372-373

- Nœud actuel : Rahner's theological anthropology of freedom — Rahner's transcendental neo-Kantian anthropology represents a valuable but dated framework that needs updating; Rahner Christianizes and radicalizes freedom by making it 'freedom from God and towards God' with highest responsibility before God's love, while paradoxically making it 'a demand and a task rather than freedom'

- Nœud actuel : Philo's influence on early Christian thought — Philo's influence on early Christian literature was extensive but often indirect and unacknowledged; direct citation was rare except in certain authors like Clement, Origen, and Ambrose
- Constat : Runia's conclusions (ch. 16) are more articulated than the node. He distinguishes three streams: a 'direct tradition' of reception mediated through the Alexandrian school (Clement, Origen, Didymus, then Eusebius, Gregory of Nyssa, Ambrose, Augustine), a broader pre-Clementine 'Philonism' (Barnabas, Justin, Theophilus, 'if they did not read Philo directly'), and an 'indirect tradition' after Origen and Eusebius. Naming is a separate matter: Philo 'is not explicitly named' before Clement; Ambrose uses him copiously but 'only once refers to his source by name', Jerome names him more often; most Patristic usage 'is not advertised by the mention of his name'. So 'direct citation rare except Clement, Origen, and Ambrose' conflates direct use with citation by name. Page map unavailable; folios inferred from running headers (ch. 16 = pp. 335-347).
  - « Philo is not explicitly named in Christian sources until the end of the 2nd century, when he is extensively used by Clement of Alexandria. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:8931-8933]
  - « His copious usage, predominantly in the area of allegorical interpretation, is somewhat camouflaged by the translation into Latin and the fact that he only once refers to his source by name. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:8975-8977]
  - « after Origen and Eusebius the reception of Philo splits up into a direct and an indirect tradition. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:9040-9041]
- Description proposée : Runia's conclusions distinguish three streams of Philonic reception: before Clement a broader 'Philonism' (Barnabas, Justin, Theophilus), whose direct dependence on Philo cannot be proved; a 'direct tradition' running through the Alexandrian school (Clement, who first names him, Origen, Didymus) and its sympathisers (Eusebius, Gregory of Nyssa, Ambrose, Augustine); and, after Origen and Eusebius, an 'indirect tradition' in which Philonic themes are taken over at second hand. Most Patristic use 'is not advertised by the mention of his name': Ambrose names Philo only once, Jerome more often.
- Pages : 335-343 (ch. 16; folios from running headers, page map unavailable)

### `scholarly_argument_runia_philo_s_role_in_christian_bibl_4` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Philo's role in Christian biblical exegesis — Philo's allegorical method and biblical interpretation profoundly influenced Christian exegetes, particularly Origen and the Alexandrian tradition, though often with Christian theological modifications
- Constat : Runia's own summary supports the node: 'Many of the rules and procedures of Christian allegorical exegesis are built on foundations laid by Philo. Origen appeals to Philo as a master and model of the allegorical method'; by the 4th century Philo's method is 'explicitly associated with the Alexandrian school'; Origen 'stood closest to Philo and absorbed his thought most deeply'; themes show 'appropriation and modification'. Caveat the node omits: in ch. 9 §3 (pp. 163-171) Runia stresses that scholarly treatments of Origen's debt 'represent strongly divergent points of view' (Wolfson/Hanson/Grant 'profound' vs. De Lubac/Crouzel minimising). Page map unavailable; folios from running headers.
  - « Many of the rules and procedures of Christian allegorical exegesis are built on foundations laid by Philo. Origen appeals to Philo as a master and model of the allegorical method. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:8996-8998]
  - « These six scholarly treatments of Origen's debt to Phi10 in his biblical interpretation represent strongly divergent points of view. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:4690-4691]

### `scholarly_argument_runia_philo_s_status_as_honorary_chu_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Philo's status as honorary Church Father — Philo became an 'honorary Church Father' due to Eusebius's portrayal of the Therapeutae as the first Christians (and of Philo as connected to the apostolic church) and the supposed Christian identity of the Therapeutae; this status was later lost when his Jewish identity was reasserted
- Constat : Runia describes a 'gradual development' of the legend, not a status conferred by Eusebius: Eusebius is 'the first recorded witness to make an explicit connection' (Therapeutae as first Christians in Egypt; meeting Peter), but 'in Eusebius (1-5) Philo is not explicitly presented as a Christian'; conversion appears only in Ps.Prochorus and Photius, and the Catena lemma 'Philo the Bishop' is Byzantine. The loss of status is dated by Runia to Fabricius's dissertation of 1693 establishing Philo's Platonism, after which Philo is 'studied primarily as a rather unusual author from the Greco-Roman world'; real Jewish acceptance comes only in the 19th century. 'Jewish identity reasserted' is not his explanation. Page map unavailable; folios from running headers (ch. 1 §1 pp. 3-6, §5 pp. 31-33).
  - « The first recorded witness to make an explicit connection between Philo and the Christians was Eusebius in his celebrated Ecclesiastical History » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:402-404]
  - « In Eusebius (l-5) Philo is not explicitly presented as a Christian, but he stands so close to the early Christians that his evidence is invaluable. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:474-475]
  - « From this time onwards Philo gradually loses his status as a honorary Church father and theological authority, and comes to be studied primarily as a rather unusual author from the Greco-Roman world » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:1172-1174]
- Description proposée : Runia traces the 'gradual development' of Philo's Christian status through testimonia: Eusebius, the first to connect Philo with the Christians (De vita contemplativa as witness to the first Christians in Egypt; meeting with Peter), does 'not explicitly' present him as a Christian; Jerome and Epiphanius bring him near conversion, Ps.Prochorus and Photius record it, and Byzantine Catenae head extracts 'Philo the Bishop'. The loss of this status begins with Fabricius's 1693 dissertation showing Philo's thought was Platonist; thereafter he is studied as a Greco-Roman author, and acceptance into the Jewish heritage comes only in the 19th century.
- Pages : 3-6, 31-33 (folios from running headers, page map unavailable)

### `scholarly_argument_runia_philo_s_survival_through_chris_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Philo's survival through Christian transmission — Philo's writings survived almost exclusively because they were preserved by Christians; had transmission been left to Jewish hands, the works would have been lost to posterity
- Constat : Sentence located verbatim in the Preface. The page map cannot project pages for this file; the following extracted page carries the running heads 'XII PREFACE / PREFACE XIII', so the quote stands on p. xi as the node says. 'Almost exclusively' is a fair gloss of 'preserved through their reception in the Christian tradition' plus the counterfactual.
  - « If their survival had been left in the hands of his fellow Jews, these precious documents would have been lost to posterity, together with almost the entire heritage of Alexandrian Judaism. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Runia - 1993 - Jewish Traditions in Early Christian Literature, Volume 3 Philo in Early Christian Literature A sur.md:210-212]

Pages 149-150 correct.

### `scholarly_argument_frankfurt_concept_of_a_person_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : concept of a person — The concept of a person should not be defined merely by the coexistence of mental and physical predicates (as in Strawson and Ayer), but by the structure of a person's will, specifically the capacity for second-order desires.
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 64-72 (p. 5). The Strawson/Ayer contrast is faithful. But (a) Frankfurt states 'one essential difference between persons and other creatures', not a definition of the person; and (b) the criterion he regards as essential is not the capacity for second-order desires generally but for second-order volitions (p. 10: 'it is having second-order volitions, and not having second-order desires generally, that I regard as essential to being a person'). The node collapses this distinction.
  - « It is my view that one essential difference between persons and other creatures is to be found in the structure of a person's will. » [Frankfurt-FreedomConceptPerson-1971.md:124-125] (p. 6)
  - « Now it is having second-order volitions, and not having second-order desires generally, that I regard as essential to being a person. » [Frankfurt-FreedomConceptPerson-1971.md:326-327] (p. 10; page_map conf 0.85, folio confirmed by running head at line 293)
- Description proposée : Frankfurt rejects Strawson's and Ayer's use of 'person' for any entity bearing both psychological and corporeal predicates, since that misappropriates a valuable philosophical term. He locates 'one essential difference between persons and other creatures' in 'the structure of a person's will': persons can form second-order desires. He then specifies (p. 10) that what is essential to being a person is having second-order volitions (wanting a desire to be one's will), 'and not having second-order desires generally'.
- Pages : 5-7, 10

### `scholarly_argument_frankfurt_first_order_vs_second_order_de_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : first-order vs. second-order desires — First-order desires are simply desires to do or not to do one thing or another, which humans share with animals. Second-order desires are desires to have or not to have certain desires and motives. No animal other than man appears to have the capacity for reflective self-evaluation manifested in second-order desires.
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 144-149 (p. 7). First-order desires 'simply desires to do or not to do one thing or another', shared with many animals; second-order desires = wanting to have or not have certain desires and motives (p. 7); 'No animal other than man, however, appears to have the capacity for reflective self-evaluation'. Description preserves Frankfurt's 'appears'.
  - « No animal other than man, however, ap- pears to have the capacity for reflective self-evaluation that is mani- fested in the formation of second-order desires. » [Frankfurt-FreedomConceptPerson-1971.md:147-149] (p. 7)

### `scholarly_argument_frankfurt_free_will_and_the_structure_of_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and the structure of the will — The essential difference between persons and other creatures lies in the structure of a person's will, specifically the capacity to form 'second-order desires' or 'desires of the second order'—desires to have or not to have certain first-order desires. This capacity for reflective self-evaluation is uniquely human.
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 122-126 (p. 6). Two qualifications are lost: Frankfurt writes that the capacity 'appears' peculiar to humans and explicitly holds that 'It is conceptually possible that members of novel or even of familiar nonhuman species should be persons' (p. 6) — so 'uniquely human' is a presumption, not his thesis; and the essential feature is second-order volitions, not second-order desires as such (p. 10). The node's title 'free will' is also not the topic of this passage (persons).

- Description proposée : Frankfurt holds that 'one essential difference between persons and other creatures' lies in 'the structure of a person's will': besides first-order desires, which many animals share, it 'seems to be peculiarly characteristic of humans' that they form 'second-order desires'. He stresses that person-attributes are not necessarily species-specific (nonhuman persons are conceptually possible) and later narrows the essential criterion to second-order volitions (p. 10).

Blackson' (line 866) — printed p. 99, not p. 84 (page_map gives p. 98 at conf 0.80; the running heads are decisive). The same claim is stated on p. 84 (line 150).

- Nœud actuel : Cyclical vs. linear time in Greek antiquity — The cyclical conception of Greek time, popularized by Nietzsche's eternal return, has been challenged: cyclicity is not intrinsic to time itself but a feature of its measurement (following Aristotle), and linearity was also present in Greek culture from the archaic period onward.
- Constat : The Introduction is co-authored by Richard Faure and Simon-Pierre Valli (not Faure alone). The second is misreported: the text says linearity 'is not absent from Ancient Greek culture', and reports Fränkel's view that the Greek world 'adopted a linear temporal conception after the archaic period' — not 'from the archaic period onward' — then, with Darbo-Peschanski, calls that stance 'somewhat teleological' and weak, concluding that Greek conceptions were 'multiple' (p. 4).
  - « First, cyclicity is not necessarily an intrinsic feature of time, but rather of the elements that help people witness time elapse, as Aristotle has it: » [10.1515_9783110736076.md:140-141] (p. 2)
  - « According to Fränkel (1931), the Greek world gradually left cyclicity and adopted a linear temporal conception after the archa- ic period. » [10.1515_9783110736076.md:205-207] (p. 4)
  - « shows, this novel (somewhat teleological) stance contained its own weaknesses, because it allowed for the possibility that Greek conceptions of time were not single or double, but multiple » [10.1515_9783110736076.md:209-211] (p. 4)
- Description proposée : Faure and Valli (Introduction): the view that Greeks conceived time as cyclical, popularised since Nietzsche's eternal return, has been challenged in two respects. First, following Aristotle (Ph. 223b28-224a2) and Sorabji, cyclicity is 'not necessarily an intrinsic feature of time' but of the elements by which time is witnessed; seasons and rituals are paradigmatic rather than cyclical. Second, linearity 'is not absent' from Greek culture — Fränkel held that the Greeks adopted linear time after the archaic period — but this 'somewhat teleological' stance has its weaknesses (Darbo-Peschanski): Greek conceptions of time were multiple.
- Pages : 2-4

### `scholarly_argument_faure_g_del_s_relativity_based_chall_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gödel's relativity-based challenge to objective time — Gödel's argument from Einsteinian relativity—that if simultaneity is relative, succession becomes illusory and only observer-dependent 'nows' exist—represents a 'radical thesis' that challenges both cyclical and linear conceptions by making 'round trips' in time conceivable.
- Constat : Co-authored by Faure and Valli. Quote located verbatim at lines 170-182, p. 3 (pdftotext folio). The authors present Gödel 'in contrast' to Winand/Sorabji as the one rationale that 'may lead to a "radical thesis"' — Sorabji's thesis that time itself may be cyclical — while noting that 'cyclical' is 'not suitable for his conception'. Nothing in the text says Gödel's argument 'challenges both cyclical and linear conceptions'; that framing is the node's.
  - « In contrast, Gödel's (19703 [19491]) rationale, based on Einstein's relativity theory, may lead to a "radical thesis." » [10.1515_9783110736076.md:171-172] (p. 3)
  - « Although he does not use the word cyclical, which is not suitable for his conception, since cyclicity still rests on the view that time elapses, he entertains the idea that "round trips" in time are possible » [10.1515_9783110736076.md:179-181] (p. 3)
- Description proposée : Faure and Valli (Introduction): whereas Sorabji finds no Greek 'radical thesis' that time itself is cyclical, and cyclical stances are extremely rare (Winand), Gödel's rationale based on Einstein's relativity 'may lead to a "radical thesis"': if simultaneity is relative, succession becomes illusory and only observer-dependent 'nows' remain, leading Gödel to an idealistic conclusion. Though 'cyclical' is not suitable for his conception, he entertains the possibility of 'round trips' in time, so that instances may be lived more than once.
- Pages : 3

### `scholarly_argument_fischer_compatibilism_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : compatibilism — Defends compatibilism: free will and moral responsibility are compatible with causal determinism. - Constat : Work confirmed: Fischer, Kane, Pereboom, Vargas, Four Views on Free Will (Blackwell 2007); Fischer's chapter 'Compatibilism' pp. 44-84. Quote verbatim located at lines 3236-3245, printed p. 71 (running header 'Compatibilism 71' at line 3207; agentic_search p.71, conf 0.95). Description matches Fischer's own terms (semicompatibilism, guidance vs regulative control, actual-sequence).
  - « Semicompatibilism contends that moral responsibility is compatible with causal determinism, quite apart from whether causal determinism threatens regulative control. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four views on free-will.md:3236-3238] (Fischer 2007, p. 71)

- Constat : Located in section 8 'An Account of Guidance Control', p. 78 (running header '78 John Martin Fischer' at line 3504). Substance faithful.

### `scholarly_argument_fischer_response_to_manipulation_argum_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : response to manipulation arguments — Responds to Pereboom's four-case manipulation argument by arguing that in the final deterministic case, if the agent's mechanism is moderately reasons-responsive, they can still be morally responsible. But clearly Plum is not blameworthy in cases one and two, given the provenance of the motivational states that lead to his behavior. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four views on free-will.md:8016-8020] (Fischer 2007, p. 185)
  - « moral responsibility is the "gateway" to such notions as praiseworthiness and blameworthiness; an individual is morally responsible to the extent that he is an apt candidate for the reactive attitudes and moral praise and blame, but further conditions have to be met » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four views on free-will.md:8021-8024] (Fischer 2007, p. 185)
- Description proposée : Replying to Pereboom's four-case argument, Fischer holds that Professor Plum meets the minimal conditions for guidance control, and so for moral responsibility, in all four cases; he does not distinguish the cases with respect to responsibility. Responsibility and blameworthiness require different histories.

### `scholarly_argument_fischer_sourcehood_and_reasons_respons_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : sourcehood and reasons-responsiveness — The kind of control relevant to moral responsibility is 'sourcehood'—the action must originate from the agent's own mechanism, and this mechanism must be 'moderately reasons-responsive.' This means the mechanism would respond differently to some different reasons, even if not to all possible reasons.
- Constat : Quote verbatim located at lines 3517-3519, p. 78. But Fischer does not call the control relevant to responsibility 'sourcehood': 'sourcehood' is his name for the source-incompatibilist requirement examined in section 6 (pp. 66-70), where he argues a compatibilist notion of sourcehood is available and the incompatibilist versions are inconclusive. His own positive notion is 'guidance control', whose two elements are the mechanism being the agent's own and 'appropriately' reasons-responsive. 'Moderately reasons-responsive' and the 'some different reasons, not all' gloss are not in this text.
  - « On my approach to guidance control, there are two chief elements: the mechanism that issues in action must be the "agent's own," and it must be appropriately "reasons-responsive." » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four views on free-will.md:3517-3519] (Fischer 2007, p. 78)
  - « Here I wish to explore some of the reasons why philosophers have contended that causal determinism rules out sourcehood in the sense required by moral responsibility, and I wish to offer some plausibility-arguments against this contention. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four views on free-will.md:3036-3039] (Fischer 2007, p. 67)
- Description proposée : Fischer's account of guidance control has two chief elements: the mechanism issuing in action must be the agent's own, and it must be appropriately reasons-responsive; 'mechanism' means only the process or way things go, and the locus of control remains the agent. He treats 'sourcehood' as the incompatibilist's worry (section 6) and argues that a notion of sourcehood compatible with causal determination is available, so that source incompatibilism is at best inconclusive.
- Pages : 66-70, 78

- Nœud actuel : Origen's view of free will and divine grace in Romans 9-11 — Origen presents a compatibilist position where divine foreknowledge and human free will are not mutually exclusive; God foreknows but does not predetermine human choices, and grace cooperates with human freedom rather than overriding it

### `scholarly_argument_gorday_patristic_exegesis_as_alternat_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Patristic exegesis as alternative to determinism/libertarian dichotomy — The patristic exegetes, particularly Origen, offer resources for rethinking free will and determinism that avoid the later Reformation-era polarizations; patristic interpretation sees Scripture as a unity that preserves both divine sovereignty and human responsibility without systematic resolution
- Constat : Quote verbatim located at lines 235-252 in the Author's Preface (folio 'vii' at line 170, 'ix' at 292: pp. vii-viii). Gorday's preface is about getting 'behind the Reformation-era interpretation of Paul wherein justification becomes the center of gravity' and 'Catholic-Protestant rapprochement'; it says nothing about free will versus determinism or a 'determinism/libertarian dichotomy'. The node's 'preserves both divine sovereignty and human responsibility without systematic resolution' is not in the preface; the only related remark (Conclusions, lines 20012-20015) reports Beyschlag's view that 'the Augustinian perspective, focusing initially on the determinism/indeterminism issue, must be banned from these chapters'.
  - « As a study in the patristic exegesis of Romans this work represents an attempt to get back behind the Reformation-era interpretation of Paul wherein "justification" becomes the center of gravity in explaining every aspect of the Apostle's thought. » [03_Paul/Gorday_Principles_Patristic_Exegesis_Romans9-11_1983.md:235-241] (Gorday 1983, pp. vii-viii)
  - « He then argued that the Augustinian perspective, focusing initially on the determinism/ indeterminism issue, must be banned from these chapters: in fact they deal with Jews and Gentiles as peoples in the divine plan, not with individuals » [03_Paul/Gorday_Principles_Patristic_Exegesis_Romans9-11_1983.md:20012-20017] (Gorday on Beyschlag, ch. VI)
- Description proposée : In his preface Gorday presents the book as an attempt to get behind the Reformation-era reading of Paul in which 'justification' is the center of gravity, hoping that a fresh evaluation of patristic exegesis may aid Catholic-Protestant rapprochement. Repristination would be a mistake, but the early centuries can help in 'rethinking the modern task'; the key is reading the patristic texts for 'the subtleties and structures by which Scripture is a unity for the fathers'. The free-will/determinism framing is not Gorday's here.
- Pages : vii-viii

### `scholarly_argument_graeser_plotinus_critique_of_stoic_com_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)

- Constat : Quote verbatim located at lines 5701-5707, ch. III 1 he says Plotinus 'does not want to enter into the discussion of the distinction drawn by Chrysippus', misrepresents the Stoics' 'rigid necessity', and argues from a standpoint close to Chrysippus' universal causation (#54-55, lines 2660-2703). In ch. VI he calls Plotinus a determinist 'as far as the question of empirical responsibility is concerned' and finds Plotinus and the Stoics 'basically in agreement'; the independence claim concerns the metaphysical, non-empirical self.

- Description proposée : Graeser holds that, as regards empirical responsibility, Plotinus is a determinist in the Stoic sense, though he would have objected to the comparison; neither Plotinus nor the Stoics take the empirical self to be the 'ultimate principle' of action. In criticizing fate (Enn. III 1) Plotinus misrepresents the Stoics' 'rigid necessity' and declines to engage Chrysippus' distinction of causes. Plotinus' real aim is to make the non-empirical, metaphysical self an independent principle prior to the events necessitated by fate; the problem of free will thus runs 'on two levels'.

### `scholarly_argument_graeser_plotinus_engagement_with_stoic_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Plotinus' engagement with Stoic doctrines on free will — Plotinus develops his own theory of free will in conscious opposition to Stoic determinism, particularly Chrysippus' compatibilist account of fate and moral responsibility, while also tacitly adopting certain Stoic ideas in cosmology and theodicy
- Constat : The second half is Graeser's preface (p. xi, lines 355-365): Plotinus 'makes use of Stoic ideas, especially in the context of Theodicy and Cosmology' alongside 'strong repudiation of Stoic doctrines, as well as tacit acceptance of some of them'. The first half ('develops his own theory of free will in conscious opposition to Stoic determinism, particularly Chrysippus' compatibilist account') is not Graeser's: ch. VI opens by answering affirmatively that Plotinus is a determinist as far as empirical responsibility goes, and 'compatibilist' is not his word. Graeser's own phrasing for the free-will topic is that the problem 'must be viewed on two levels'.
  - « Thus, one is confronted with the rather surprising fact that the writings of Plotinus apparently contain strong repudiation of Stoic doctrines, as well as tacit acceptance of some of them. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Plotinus and the Stoics- A Preliminary Study -- Andreas Graeser -- Philosophia Antiqua 22, 1972 -- Brill Archive -- 10.1163-9789004320437 -- 40e2ba2071c19c7f4c4ab4b045ddfb83 -- Anna’s Archive.md:363-365] (Graeser 1972, p. xi)
  - « Has Plotinus to be considered a determinist in the sense of the word which is applicable to the Stoics's idea of universal causation? As far as the question of empirical responsibility 1 is concerned, the answer to this must be on the affirmative » [01_Philosophie_antique/Plotinus and the Stoics- A Preliminary Study -- Andreas Graeser -- Philosophia Antiqua 22, 1972 -- Brill Archive -- 10.1163-9789004320437 -- 40e2ba2071c19c7f4c4ab4b045ddfb83 -- Anna’s Archive.md:5182-5185] (Graeser 1972, p. 112)
- Description proposée : Graeser's starting point is Porphyry's remark that Plotinus' writings are 'full of hidden Stoic doctrines': Plotinus makes use of Stoic ideas, especially in theodicy and cosmology, while openly attacking Stoic ontology, theology, psychology and physics, so that his work shows 'strong repudiation of Stoic doctrines, as well as tacit acceptance of some of them'. On free will, Graeser judges that with respect to empirical responsibility Plotinus is a determinist in the Stoic sense, the difference lying in the metaphysical self as an independent principle.
- Pages : xi; 112-125

### `scholarly_argument_jowers_free_will_and_determinism_in_b_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and determinism in biblical interpretation — The biblical text contains apparent contradictions regarding divine control and human freedom that have generated multiple interpretive traditions; the editor frames these as questions requiring theological answers rather than resolving them himself
- Constat : Quote verbatim located at lines 137-144 (Jowers' Introduction). Jowers speaks of 'an apparent breach' between assertions of God's righteousness and suggestions that he influences human beings to wicked acts, and leaves the answers to the four contributors. No printed folio in the extraction (ebook); cite by line.
