Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:137-139] (Jowers 2011, Introduction)

### `scholarly_argument_jowers_ante_nicene_providence_as_apologetic_complex` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Jowers's historical sketch argues that ante-Nicene appeals to providence were chiefly apologetic. Minucius Felix and Lactantius infer a wise and benevolent governor from the ordered cosmos; the problem of evil is answered by locating evil in sin and holding that preventing sin would nullify human freedom; and Irenaeus invokes the conjunction of divine justice and benevolence against Marcion's division of the two. Providence, freedom, and anti-heretical monotheism are therefore presented as one apologetic complex rather than independent doctrines.
- Constat : Located at lines 161-192 of Jowers' Introduction: Minucius Felix (orderly arrangement of nature as evidence of fatherly concern), Lactantius (a wise and benevolent God who 'designed, created, and now governs'), the problem of evil ('all evil results from sin, and God could have prevented sin only at the cost of nullifying human freedom', illustrated from Tertullian, whom the node omits), Irenaeus contra Marcion, and the summary that providence was invoked 'mainly in the context of apologetic arguments against paganism and heresy'. 'One apologetic complex rather than independent doctrines' is a fair paraphrase. No printed folio available (ebook).
  - « That evil exists in God's world constitutes no evidence against God's existence or his providence, ante-Nicene Christians typically argued, because all evil results from sin, and God could have prevented sin only at the cost of nullifying human freedom. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:179-182] (Jowers 2011, Introduction)
  - « In the ante-Nicene period, then, Christian authors referred to the doctrine of providence mainly in the context of apologetic arguments against paganism and heresy » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:190-191] (Jowers 2011, Introduction)

### `scholarly_argument_jowers_late_patristic_east_west_providence_divergence` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Jowers argues that the late patristic period leaves two opposed providential models. Augustine defines freedom as doing what one wishes, so an act can be voluntary and blameworthy even when the agent cannot refrain; regeneration consequently makes God the ultimate determinant of salvation. The Greek East, represented by John Damascene and John Chrysostom, instead identifies voluntary action with equal possibilities to act or not act and makes the human being's own choice the primary determinant of salvation. Jowers records the divergence without resolving it.
- Constat : Located at lines 207-240 of Jowers' Introduction: Augustine's freedom 'consists not in the ability to do otherwise than one actually does but in the ability to do that which one wishes'; an act 'can be voluntary and thus liable to praise or blame ... even if one cannot refrain from performing it'; 'it is God, not human beings, who ultimately determines which human beings receive salvation'; the Eastern Fathers (John of Damascus on 'equal possibilities', Chrysostom 'Of their own free choice') make human beings 'the primary determinants of who receives salvation'; 'two radically opposed conceptions'. Jowers does not adjudicate. No printed folio (ebook).
  - « Freedom, as Augustine understood it, consists not in the ability to do otherwise than one actually does but in the ability to do that which one wishes. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:216-217] (Jowers 2011, Introduction)
  - « The Eastern Fathers, moreover, considered human beings, not God, the primary determinants of who receives salvation. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:233-234] (Jowers 2011, Introduction)

### `scholarly_argument_kowalski_comparison_of_paul_and_epictet_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : comparison of Paul and Epictetus on free will — Similarities: both believe free will can exist in a divinely determined world, and human volition requires continuous education and subordination to God's will. Differences: Epictetus has a natural image of deity to be imitated in human pursuit of freedom, while Paul has a genuinely relational, corporeal and emotive character of free will; Paul's psycho-somatic nature of human personality and will invites dialogue with modern science but must be carried out cautiously due to different methodologies, transcendent deity, and Christological foundati
- Constat : Description restates the abstract almost verbatim; quote_verbatim is in the file (lines 32-39, printed p. 513, not 513-514). The body adds one qualification worth keeping: neither Paul nor Epictetus engages the theoretical problem of reconciling free will with divine determinism (p. 537).
  - « The elements that clearly distinguish Paul from Epictetus are the natural image of deity to be imitated in the human pursuit of freedom, and a genuinely relational, corporeal and emotive character of free will in the apostle. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Kowalski - 2022 - Divine and Human Spirit in Rom 816. Paul and Epictetus on Free Will.md:34-36] (Kowalski 2022, p. 513)
  - « how to reconcile human free will with divine determinism. They perceive such a symbiosis as natural and possible to argue for, as many contemporary philosophers would also do. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Kowalski - 2022 - Divine and Human Spirit in Rom 816. Paul and Epictetus on Free Will.md:1183-1184] (Kowalski 2022, p. 537)

### `scholarly_argument_kowalski_epictetus_on_free_will_and_pro_1` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Epictetus on free will and proairesis — Epictetus's idea of freedom is constructed around the notion of proairesis, understood as the true self of a person, responsible for free moral choices and actions. Proairesis represents the faculty of choice that remains free even in a deterministically ordered universe.
- Constat : Content faithful, but the quote_verbatim sits in the conclusion at lines 1128-1133 = printed p. 536, not pp. 513-514 (only the abstract sentence on proairesis as 'true self' is on p. 513).   - « He explains it with the notion of proairesis, which can be translated as “volition,” and which constitutes our true self, a rational particle of divinity within us. Thanks to it, we are capable of making free moral choices which Zeus himself does not interfere with. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Kowalski - 2022 - Divine and Human Spirit in Rom 816. Paul and Epictetus on Free Will.md:1130-1133] (Kowalski 2022, p. 536)

Paul and Epictetus on Free Will.md:374-375] (Kowalski 2022, p. 520)
- Description proposée : Epictetus, per Kowalski, speaks of freedom 'in an inner, psychological and attitudinal sense' and explains it through proairesis, translatable as 'volition', which 'constitutes our true self, a rational particle of divinity within us'; thanks to it we make free moral choices 'which Zeus himself does not interfere with'. Epictetus's 'God-directed world' (Long) is 'a deterministically constructed space' that nonetheless leaves room for free will and autonomous acts; proairesis needs constant care to stay unimpeded by desires, emotions and externals.

### `scholarly_argument_martin_determinism_and_free_will_in_j_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Determinism and free will in Josephus' account of the Pharisees — The Pharisees, according to Josephus, hold a paradoxical or 'fusion' position: they attribute everything to fate/God yet do not deprive human will of pursuing what is in man's power; this represents a compatibilist or cooperative view where fate and human virtue/vice are both admitted to the 'council-chamber of fate'
- Constat : Martin never uses 'compatibilist' (grep: no hit; 'cooperates' occurs only inside the Bell. II quotation). 'Fusion' and 'council-chamber of fate' are Josephus's words (Ant. XVIII,13, Thackeray) quoted on p. 127; 'paradoxical' is Wächter's conclusion which Martin reports (p. 129) and echoes (p. 130). Martin's own thesis runs the other way: Josephus's heimarmene is a non-technical, popular term and 'a semantic ploy' opposing life under Torah (pronoia, freedom, responsibility) to life apart from it (universal determinism), pp. 132-136. The node turns a heresiographic report plus Wächter's view into a theory of Fate/free-will cooperation attributed to Martin.
  - « These latter events depend upon human actions, but under God in obedience to Torah. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Josephus' Use of Heimarmene in the Jewish Antiquities Xiii, 171-3.md:127-129] (Martin 1981, p. 129)
  - « but, as Josephus concludes of the Pharisees, in the paradoxical attribution of everything to Fate and God. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Josephus' Use of Heimarmene in the Jewish Antiquities Xiii, 171-3.md:153-155] (Martin 1981, p. 130)
  - « His use of the term can be understood as a semantic ploy which sets up an apologetic contrast: life under and life apart from Torah. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Josephus' Use of Heimarmene in the Jewish Antiquities Xiii, 171-3.md:333-335] (Martin 1981, p. 134)
- Description proposée : Martin quotes Josephus's report that the Pharisees posit a 'fusion' in which the will of man 'with his virtue and vice' is 'admitted to the council-chamber of fate' (Ant. XVIII,13), and relays Wächter's conclusion that determinism and free will were 'paradoxical' for the Pharisees. His own reading: Josephus, 'not a philosophical thinker', uses heimarmene casually, in its popular negative sense, as 'a semantic ploy' contrasting life under Torah (pronoia, freedom, responsibility) with life apart from it (universal determinism).
- Pages : 127-130, 132-136

### `scholarly_argument_martin_josephus_use_of_heimarmene_to__0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Josephus' use of heimarmene to distinguish Jewish philosophies — Josephus uses heimarmene (fate/destiny) as the central criterion to differentiate the Pharisees, Sadducees, and Essenes in Ant. XIII, 171-3, with the Pharisees holding a mixed position (some events fated, others dependent on human action), the Essenes embracing complete fatalism (Fate as 'mistress of all things'), and the Sadducees denying fate entirely (attributing everything to human power and responsibility)
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 18-25 (p. 127); 'single issue which distinguishes the three philosophies' at p. 128. Free will is not the characteristic of human nature, since angels and demons share it, but 'son plus noble fleuron'.

### `scholarly_argument_pouderon_resurrection_and_moral_account_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : resurrection and moral accountability — The Traité sur la résurrection presupposes free moral agency as foundational: resurrection is necessary because God is just, and justice requires that humans be rewarded or punished for their freely chosen actions; without free will, resurrection would be meaningless
- Constat : Pouderon reports the opposite emphasis: the Supplique founds resurrection on the sole necessity of judgment, whereas the Traité says that reducing the necessity of resurrection to judgment alone is 'manifestement erroné', since even the irresponsible (infants) rise (D.R. XIV,6), p. 69; the Traité's primary arguments are cause/finality (XII-XIII, XXIV-XXV), the judgment argument (XVIII-XXIII) showing only that judgment applies to the composite, pp. 104-105. 'Without free will resurrection would be meaningless' is not in Pouderon; he ties free will to virtue ('sans ce dernier, ni Bien, ni Mal', p. 156). Pages 62-67 concern the Traité's authenticity.
  - « La Supplique fonde la croyance en la résurrection sur la seule nécessité d'un jugement (XXXI, 4 et XXXVI, 2), alors que l'auteur du Traité affirme au contraire que réduire la nécessité de la résurrection à celle du seul jugement est manifestement erroné, puisque même les êtres irresponsables, comme les jeunes enfants, sont appelés à ressusciter (XIV, 6) » [04_Apologistes_Justin/[Théologie historique 82] Bernard Pouderon - Athénagoras d’Athènes, philosophe chrétien (2000, Beauchesne).md:1639-1645] (Pouderon 1989, p. 69)
  - « en vue du Jugement (qui ne peut s'appliquer qu'au composé) [D.R. XVIII à XXIII] » [04_Apologistes_Justin/[Théologie historique 82] Bernard Pouderon - Athénagoras d’Athènes, philosophe chrétien (2000, Beauchesne).md:2480-2482] (Pouderon 1989, p. 105)
  - « libre-arbitre est indispensable à la notion de vertu: sans ce » [04_Apologistes_Justin/[Théologie historique 82] Bernard Pouderon - Athénagoras d’Athènes, philosophe chrétien (2000, Beauchesne).md:3724] (Pouderon 1989, p. 156)
- Description proposée : Pouderon: the Supplique grounds belief in resurrection on the sole necessity of a judgment (L. XXXI,4; XXXVI,2), whereas the Traité declares reducing resurrection's necessity to judgment 'manifestement erroné', since even infants rise (D.R. XIV,6). In the Traité the resurrection of the body follows from God's design and man's nature (XII-XVII), with judgment (XVIII-XXIII) requiring only that the composite, not the soul alone, be judged; the man as a whole is responsible for his acts (D.R. XVIII,4). Free will is 'indispensable à la notion de vertu'.
- Pages : 69, 73, 104-105, 156

- Nœud actuel : editorial methodology on determinism passages — Rousseau's critical edition carefully distinguishes Irenaeus's own voice from his reporting of heretical views, using sigla and apparatus to mark textual uncertainties; the edition does not itself take a philosophical stance on free will but provides the textual foundation for scholarly analysis of Irenaeus's anti-determinist arguments
- Constat : The fonds holds SC 264 (Livre I, tome II, texte et traduction, 1979; SC 263 introduction absent). No editorial stance on free will exists in this volume, but the node's positive claim about the apparatus is unfounded.

The distinction between Irenaeus's voice and the doctrines he reports rests on Irenaeus's own 'dicunt' formulas, rendered 'disent-ils' / 'prétendent-ils'. The volume takes no position on free will; the introduction (SC 263) is not in the fonds.

### `scholarly_argument_rousseau_free_will_in_gnosticism_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will in Gnosticism — The edition presents Irenaeus's report that Valentinian Gnostics held deterministic views where human natures are divided into spiritual, psychic, and hylic classes with no possibility of change between them; salvation or damnation is determined by one's essential nature rather than free choice
- Constat : Irenaeus's report as edited in SC 264 assigns free choice to the psychic class: I.6.1 the Saviour came to the psychic 'quia suae potestatis est' ('puisque celui-ci est doué de libre arbitre'), and I.7.5 the psychic 'if it chooses the better' rests in the Intermediate, 'if the worse' goes to what it has made itself like. Only the pneumatic (saved 'naturaliter', I.6.2) and the hylic (perishing 'ex necessitate', I.6.1) are fixed. The flat 'no possibility of change... determined by essential nature rather than free choice' misreads the text. Pages from printed running headers (page_map conf 0.50).

  - « L'élément psychique, s'il choisit le meilleur, aura son » [10_Ouvrages_reference/Irénée Contre les hérésies, Livre 1 Texte et traduction (1982)(Sources Chrétiennes 264) .md:2549] (AH I,7,5, SC 264, p. 111)
  - « mais du fait de leur nature, pneumatique, qu'ils seront » [10_Ouvrages_reference/Irénée Contre les hérésies, Livre 1 Texte et traduction (1982)(Sources Chrétiennes 264) .md:2119] (AH I,6,2, SC 264, p. 95)
- Description proposée : SC 264 presents Irenaeus's report (AH I,6,1-2; I,7,5) that the Valentinians posit three elements or races — hylic, psychic, pneumatic (Cain, Abel, Seth). The hylic perishes of necessity, incapable of incorruptibility; the pneumatic is saved 'du fait de leur nature', whatever its works; the psychic, 'doué de libre arbitre', is the object of the Saviour's coming and goes where it inclines — rest in the Intermediate if it chooses the better, corruption if the worse.
- Pages : 90-95, 110-111

### `scholarly_argument_rousseau_irenaeus_s_own_position_on_fre_1` — **NOT_FOUND** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Irenaeus's own position on free will — Book I of the edition is heresiography, an exposition of the Valentinian and Marcosian systems, so reading it as a presentation of Irenaeus's own positive doctrine of free will overreaches; his critique of Gnostic determinism does nonetheless imply that he affirms human moral responsibility and free choice, and Irenaeus argues against the Valentinian system by insisting that God created humans with free will and that salvation depends on human response to God rather than fixed nature
- Constat : In SC 264 (Book I) grep for 'libre arbitre|liberté|libre|suae potestatis' yields only the Valentinian psychic's free will as reported (I,6,1). No passage of Book I has Irenaeus 'insisting that God created humans with free will' against the Valentinian system; his positive doctrine is in Book IV (IV,37-39, SC 100) and the refutation begins in Book II. SC 263 (introduction) is absent from the fonds, so no editorial statement can be checked. The node itself concedes the overreach.
  - « doué de libre arbitre, afin de le sauver. Car il a pris, » [10_Ouvrages_reference/Irénée Contre les hérésies, Livre 1 Texte et traduction (1982)(Sources Chrétiennes 264) .md:2051] (AH I,6,1, SC 264, p. 91)
- Description proposée : SC 264 edits Book I, Irenaeus's exposition of the Valentinian, Marcosian and other systems; it contains no statement of Irenaeus's own doctrine of free will, which belongs to Book IV (IV,37-39) and to the refutation of Book II. Within Book I the only free-will language is Irenaeus's report of the Valentinian claim that the psychic element is 'suae potestatis' (I,6,1). Any inference about Irenaeus's own position must be drawn from those later books, not from this volume.

### `scholarly_argument_rousseau_marcus_s_deterministic_numerol_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

The label's date 1979 matches the SC 264 title page (the filename's 1982 is spurious). Pages from printed running headers.

Notes de l'auditeur : 34 nodes audited. Files confirmed via MANIFEST.md/page_map.json; all hinted files are the right works. Wolfson 1942 is present only as an empty 1-page image stub (pdfimages: single JBIG2 image; .md word_count 0) — 4 nodes SOURCE_ABSENT, acquisition of the HTR 35 article needed. Page-map caveats: Sorabji preface roman folios and Wang abstract (p. ii) resolved from running headers in the text; Donini and Blackwell are AUDIT-FAIL in agentic_search, so pages were read off the running headers printed in the extraction and citations use the [file:line] form; Butticaz page map is conf 0.40 (old-style figures stripped) so its pages are marked [page-conf<0.90]. Attribution: Barclay & Gathercole volume — the Introduction is by Barclay alone (correct); Still & Wilhite volume — Introduction by Wilhite, Afterword by Still (one node MISATTRIBUTED). Substantive distortions worth fixing first: Karamanolis ch. 4 (Christians take free will FROM the Stoics and fight astral determinists/Gnostics, not 'Stoic determinism'), Wang (three nodes turn 'not only ... but also' and 'reassesses' into flat rejection of determination/eternal decree), Vicens on Rea (Rea rejects fission as too costly), Still (he does reach a position: Paul 'clearly an apologist for the gospel'), Blackwell (explicitly frames Irenaeus with free will/self-determination and contrasts Paul). Donini n. 7 Greek is OCR-corrupt in the file; the node's romanised forms could not be authenticated from the fonds. No Greek token was written in this report except 'potestas' (Latin) and 'to eph' hemin / prohairesis / autexousion' in romanised form, all copied from the Blackwell and Karamanolis files respectively.

### `scholarly_argument_sorabji_aristotle_transformed_by_comme_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle transformed by commentators to suit Christian theology — The ancient commentators profoundly influenced subsequent Islamic and European philosophy by presenting an Aristotle transformed in ways that made him acceptable to the Christian Church; it is this transformed Aristotle embedded in commentary philosophy, not Aristotle himself, that lies behind later thinkers' views
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 131-139 (Preface, p. vii — roman folio confirmed by the running header 'viii Preface' at line 159; page map returns p.? for roman pages). But Sorabji writes that the commentators presented an Aristotle transformed 'in ways which happened to make him acceptable to the Christian Church': the acceptability is a contingent by-product, not a purpose. The label 'transformed by commentators to suit Christian theology' and the body ('in ways that made him acceptable') drop 'happened to' and imply intent. Also loses the first half of the causal claim (preservation of anti-Aristotelian material).
  - « still more to their presenting an Aristotle transformed in ways which happened to make him acceptable to the Christian Church. It is not just Aristotle, but this Aristotle transformed and embedded in the philosophy of the commentators, that lies behind the views of later thinkers. » [Richard Sorabji - Aristotle Transformed_ The Ancient Commentators and Their Influence (Ancient Commentators on Aristotle) (1990, Cornell University Press).md:135-139]
- Description proposée : Sorabji (Preface): the commentators' most significant importance is their profound influence on subsequent Islamic and European philosophy, due partly to their preserving anti-Aristotelian material that helped inspire medieval and Renaissance science, and still more to their presenting an Aristotle transformed 'in ways which happened to make him acceptable to the Christian Church'. It is this transformed Aristotle, embedded in the commentators' philosophy, not Aristotle himself, that lies behind later thinkers' views.
- Pages : vii

### `scholarly_argument_vicens_augustinian_original_sin_pressures_libertarianism` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Vicens argues that Augustine's two central ideas about original sin - original guilt and constitutional fault - are prima facie incompatible with libertarianism. Constitutional fault makes later sin inevitable because of Adam's act, while original guilt makes later persons responsible for Adam's act itself. Augustinian realists such as Oliver Crisp and Michael Rea attempt to restore control by making Adam and his heirs stages of one persisting entity or products of a pre-sin fission, but these solutions carry substantial metaphysical costs.
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 779-788, p. 14 (conf 0.98). Two misreadings: (1) Vicens does not present Rea as an Augustinian realist who 'attempts to restore control'; Rea 'considers a theory' of fission and 'suggests that the metaphysical commitments of ﬁssion theory come at "too high a price"' — Crisp is the one who 'favors' the one-entity view. (2) 'These solutions carry substantial metaphysical costs' flattens Vicens's point: evaluation 'would seem to depend a lot on one's starting point', and the difficulty Crisp tackles 'is generated in large part by a starting assumption of libertarianism' (p. 14). N. 12 adds that Crisp later repudiates universal guilt for Adam's sin.
  - « Michael Rea, in contrast, considers a theory according to which Adam and his posterity are distinct individuals who shared a common temporal state and who underwent ﬁssion at the time of Adam’s ﬁrst sin » [Christianity and the Problem of Free Will-Cambridge University Press (2023).md:796-799]
  - « Rea suggests that the metaphysical commitments of ﬁssion theory come at “too high a price” (2007, 345), but Crisp » [Christianity and the Problem of Free Will-Cambridge University Press (2023).md:801-802]
  - « But it should be noted that the difﬁculty Crisp is attempting to overcome is generated in large part by a starting assumption of libertarianism. » [Christianity and the Problem of Free Will-Cambridge University Press (2023).md:815-816]
- Description proposée : Vicens: Augustine's two central ideas, original guilt and constitutional fault, are prima facie incompatible with libertarianism — the former posits responsibility for Adam's sin itself, the latter determination to sin by something outside one's control. Some libertarians ('Augustinian realists') hold we participated in Adam's sin: Crisp favors one persisting entity of which Adam and his heirs are parts; Rea considers a fission theory but judges its costs 'too high a price'. How one evaluates such views depends on one's starting point; the difficulty Crisp addresses is generated largely by a starting assumption of libertarianism (p. 14).
- Pages : 14

### `scholarly_argument_vicens_compatibilism_avoids_constitutional_fault_not_original_guilt` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Vicens argues that the need for an Augustinian-realist mechanism of shared participation in Adam's act is generated largely by a prior libertarian assumption. A compatibilist can accept that inherited factors determine later sins without losing responsibility and therefore avoids the special puzzle of constitutional fault, although original guilt remains a separate difficulty because neither libertarianism nor compatibilism ordinarily permits responsibility for another person's sin. The comparison is a cost assessment, not Vicens's adjudication of Augustine's doctrine as true or false.
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 830-839, p. 15 (conf 0.98); the original-guilt qualification comes from n. 13 on the same page (lines 865-871), which also notes that original guilt is not a core tenet of historic doctrines (Crisp 2015) and is not entailed by Rom. 5. The description's 'cost assessment, not adjudication' matches 'it must be weighed against the costs'.
  - « What about original guilt? This doctrine seems as difﬁcult to make sense of by a compatibilist as by a libertarian, for neither allows that we could be responsible for someone else’s sin. » [Christianity and the Problem of Free Will-Cambridge University Press (2023).md:865-866]
  - « While giving up incompatibilism might seem a serious cost, it must be weighed against the costs of accepting that each of us somehow participated in the sin of the ﬁrst human. » [Christianity and the Problem of Free Will-Cambridge University Press (2023).md:837-839]

- Nœud actuel : free will and divine providence — Early Christian thinkers developed distinctive philosophical positions on freedom of choice (autexousion) in dialogue with and often against Stoic determinism and Platonist fate; their views constitute a recognizable school of thought within ancient philosophy
- Constat : Ch. 4 = pp. 130-165 (ch. 5 begins p. 166). Its framing is the reverse of the node: the Christians' opponents are the 'astral determinists' and the Valentinian/Basilidean Gnostics (p. 132), while the three key terms (to eph' hemin, prohairesis, autexousion) 'are of Stoic provenance' (p. 133) and 'This is the notion of free will that the Christians take up from the Stoics' (p. 139). 'Often against Stoic determinism and Platonist fate' is not Karamanolis's claim. The 'school of thought' thesis is a book-level claim (pp. ix, 21), not made in ch. 4.
  - « Early Christian thinkers were concerned with refuting both views, those of the astral determinists and those of Valentinian Gnostics » [[Ancient Philosophies] George E. Karamanolis - The Philosophy of Early Christianity (2021, Routledge_Acumen) - libgen.li.md:6573-6574]
  - « All three terms are of Stoic provenance. » [[Ancient Philosophies] George E. Karamanolis - The Philosophy of Early Christianity (2021, Routledge_Acumen) - libgen.li.md:6593]
  - « This is the notion of free will that the Christians take up from the Stoics. » [[Ancient Philosophies] George E. Karamanolis - The Philosophy of Early Christianity (2021, Routledge_Acumen) - libgen.li.md:6876]
- Description proposée : Karamanolis (ch. 4, pp. 130-165): free will, responsibility and providence concerned early Christian philosophers as deeply as their Hellenic contemporaries. Christians (Irenaeus, Tertullian, Clement, Origen, Basil, Gregory) sought to refute astral determinism and the Valentinian view that free will belongs to only one class of humans, and had to articulate a positive theory of agency addressing theodicy. Scripture lacks the terminology; to eph' hemin, prohairesis and autexousion are of Stoic provenance, and the Christians took up the Stoic notion of free will because they shared Stoic theological assumptions (pp. 132-133, 139).
- Pages : 130-133, 139 (chapter 130-165)

Karamanolis - The Philosophy of Early Christianity (2021, Routledge_Acumen) - libgen.li.md:1354-1358]

### `scholarly_argument_still_paul_as_apologist_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Paul as apologist — Raises the question of whether Paul should be considered an apologist, framing it as a matter for scholarly debate rather than asserting a definitive position
- Constat : Afterword 'Was Paul an Apologist?' by Todd D. Still, pp. 291-292 (lines 16266-16352). Still does not leave the question open as a matter for debate: he argues that 1 Thess 2:1-12 is an apologia, cites Phil 1:16, and concludes that Paul 'was clearly an apologist for the gospel', with the qualification (n. 1) that Paul is not best or primarily regarded as an apologist and (p. 292) that he was not an apologist 'in one and the same way' as the later writers.
  - « If Paul was not an apologist in one and the same way as those whose works treated herein were, he was clearly an apologist for the gospel that he unashamedly declared and disseminated “from Jerusalem all the way around to Illyricum” (Rom 15:19). » [Still_Wilhite_2024_Apologists_Paul_EN.md:16343-16345]
  - « To pursue the question of whether Paul might be construed as an apologist is not meant, of course, to suggest that Paul may be best perceived or primarily, much less exclusively, regarded as an apologist. » [Still_Wilhite_2024_Apologists_Paul_EN.md:16292-16294]
- Description proposée : Still (Afterword, pp. 291-292) returns to Wilhite's question whether Paul may be considered an apologist. Paul offers no general defence of Christians against outsiders' accusations as later apologists did; but 1 Thess 2:1-12 is best read as an apologia, Paul mounts defences in the Corinthian letters and is shackled for 'the apologia of the gospel' (Phil 1:16). Conclusion: not an apologist in the same way as the later writers, yet 'clearly an apologist for the gospel' — while not best or primarily regarded as an apologist (n. 1).
- Pages : 291-292

The node is keyed to Still alone. Page range '1-291' covers the whole volume, not the framing.

- Description proposée : The Apologists and Paul (eds Still & Wilhite, 2024). Wilhite's Introduction (pp. 1-20) treats 'apologist' as an unstable, pragmatic, catch-all category, and asks whether Paul himself might be considered an apologist — if so, 'the oldest extant Christian apologist' — noting that Paul's apologiae are self-defences of his authority rather than general defences of Christians (p. 11). Still's Afterword (pp. 291-292) takes up Wilhite's question and concludes Paul was 'clearly an apologist for the gospel', though not in the same way as the later apologists.

### `scholarly_argument_blackwell_irenaeus_on_human_moral_progre_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Irenaeus on human moral progression and deification — Blackwell interprets Irenaeus as holding that humans are not perfect from the beginning but must grow into deification through moral and spiritual development; this suggests a model where human agency and response are integral to the process, though Blackwell does not explicitly frame this in terms of free will versus determinism
- Constat : Content on growth is faithful: Adam 'created in a state of infancy and childlike understanding' (p. 40); §3.1.4 ''Gods' but Not Perfect From the Beginning' (pp. 50-54); §3.4.2 'Progress' (p. 64, line 3229 after header '64' at line 3224): 'progression from infancy to maturity'. But the hedge 'Blackwell does not explicitly frame this in terms of free will' is wrong: he frames it with 'free will' and 'self-determination' (pp. 40, 50, 52, 64 — 'As believers express free will according to rationality, they become like God and grow to maturity'). He does not use 'determinism'.
  - « Central to Irenaeus’ anthropology is also the concept of progression from infancy to maturity, which culminates in, among other things, incorruption. » [[Wissenschaftliche Untersuchungen Zum Neuen Testament  314] Ben C Blackwell - Christosis_ Pauline Soteriology in Light of Deification in Irenaeus and Cyril of Alexandria (2011, Mohr Siebeck).md:3230-3231]
  - « express free will according to rationality, they become like God and grow to maturity (4.4.3; 4.36–41). » [[Wissenschaftliche Untersuchungen Zum Neuen Testament  314] Ben C Blackwell - Christosis_ Pauline Soteriology in Light of Deification in Irenaeus and Cyril of Alexandria (2011, Mohr Siebeck).md:3249-3250]
  - « One aspect of the human person that he notes explicitly as being like God is self-determination (potestas).69 » [[Wissenschaftliche Untersuchungen Zum Neuen Testament  314] Ben C Blackwell - Christosis_ Pauline Soteriology in Light of Deification in Irenaeus and Cyril of Alexandria (2011, Mohr Siebeck).md:2702-2703]
- Description proposée : Blackwell on Irenaeus: humans, like Adam, are created in infancy with only the capacity for perfection if they grow into it (p. 40); Irenaeus explains that human destiny is determined by free will, not by nature, God having designed humans for growth and progress towards him, with judgment for those who turn away (pp. 50-54). Progression from infancy to maturity, moral as well as relational, is central: as believers exercise free will according to rationality they become like God and grow to maturity, and growth continues even after the resurrection (p. 64).

### `scholarly_argument_blackwell_methodology_history_of_interpr_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : methodology: history of interpretation for understanding Paul — Blackwell argues that patristic interpreters like Irenaeus and Cyril provide valuable hermeneutical resources for understanding Pauline soteriology, suggesting that later theological developments can illuminate rather than distort apostolic teaching; this methodological stance has implications for how we understand the historical development of concepts like free will and determinism in Christian thought
- Constat : Section 3 'Route of Study: History of Interpretation', pp. 14-24 (lines 975-1490; headers '23' at 1410, '24' at 1458). Blackwell chooses history of interpretation over history of religions as 'two equally valid (and even compatible) paths' (pp. 14, 24) because patristic interpreters give 'fresh eyes to view Paul' (p. 24) and the focus is 'a comparison of Paul's thought within his context with theological constructions, albeit later' (p. 23). He does not claim that later developments 'illuminate rather than distort apostolic teaching'; he insists the project is 'intentionally not' theological interpretation and that patristic comments are not brought into play when reading the Pauline texts themselves (p. 24). The 'implications for free will and determinism' are the graph's extrapolation.
  - « the focus will not be a search for origins of Paul’s thought but rather a comparison of Paul’s thought within his context with theological constructions, albeit later, that wrestle with similar topics within their own contexts. » [[Wissenschaftliche Untersuchungen Zum Neuen Testament  314] Ben C Blackwell - Christosis_ Pauline Soteriology in Light of Deification in Irenaeus and Cyril of Alexandria (2011, Mohr Siebeck).md:1418-1421]
  - « We have the option to choose one of two equally valid (and even compatible) paths: history of religions or history of interpretation. » [[Wissenschaftliche Untersuchungen Zum Neuen Testament  314] Ben C Blackwell - Christosis_ Pauline Soteriology in Light of Deification in Irenaeus and Cyril of Alexandria (2011, Mohr Siebeck).md:1477-1479]
  - « this project is intentionally not that type of study » [[Wissenschaftliche Untersuchungen Zum Neuen Testament  314] Ben C Blackwell - Christosis_ Pauline Soteriology in Light of Deification in Irenaeus and Cyril of Alexandria (2011, Mohr Siebeck).md:1468]
- Description proposée : Blackwell (pp. 14-24): two equally valid, even compatible, routes exist for assessing deification in Paul — history of religions and history of interpretation; he takes the second, reading Paul in light of patristic interpreters (Irenaeus, Cyril) because their proximity and a tradition not shaped by deification give 'fresh eyes' to view Paul. The aim is comparison of Paul's thought in his context with later theological constructions, not a search for origins; the project is deliberately not theological interpretation, and patristic comments are kept out of the reading of the Pauline texts themselves.
- Pages : 14-24

(2) Dodson 2017 'Paul and the Greco-Roman Philosophical Tradition' -> hint pointed to 'Paul and Seneca in Dialogue' (Dodson/Briones, Brill); the right file is 01_Philosophie_antique/Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md (chapter authors named per node; the Introduction is by A. W. Pitts). All 12 works are in the fonds. Page problems: Fantino 1998 is a HAL preprint ('Version avant édition') paginated 1-14 and page_map returns preprint pages, so VC pages 418-429 cannot be verified from the file; Goodrich 2013 extraction has lost every old-style digit (no page numbers, verse numbers or years in the text) and strict-cite returns no page for it, so printed pages are left blank (inference: the key passage is on the article's second page); Hogeterp pages are read from the explicit running header 'N of 22' (page_map confidence 0.50). Dettwiler 2012 page numbers at lines 86/131/144/189 are footers, which is consistent with page_map (line 92 -> 893, line 172 -> 895). Two-column layouts (Dettwiler 2007 pp. 11-14, Dettwiler 2012 p. 895) interleave columns line by line; quotes were reassembled by column and line-break hyphens joined. Haacker's extraction has degraded umlauts/ß (quoted as in file). Tally: 36 nodes — FAITHFUL 20, OVERSIMPLIFIED 9, DISTORTED 6, PAGE_WRONG 1. Recurrent defect: KG descriptions append an 'X rather than Y' contrast (autonomy, self-determination, free choice) that the authors do not draw (Dettwiler 2007 x2, Dettwiler 2012, Hyatt, Goodrich identity, Fantino), and one outright invented thesis (McFarland on human agency).

### `scholarly_argument_crubellier_integration_of_definitions_and_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : integration of definitions and intellectual virtues — Definition (B), based on causal analysis from natural philosophy, incorporates Definition (A) and paves the way to understanding intellectual virtues and their role in ethical life
- Constat : Matches the résumé/abstract on p. 215 almost word for word (page_map: line 60 -> p. 215).
  - « This paper aims to show that the definition (B) given in Book VI of the Nicomachean Ethics, which is based on a causal analysis pertaining to natural philosophy, incorporates the other, and paves the way to the notion of « intellectual virtues » and to an understanding of their role in the ethical life of human beings. » [Les définitions de la prohairesis dans les Éthiques.md:76-81] (Crubellier 2020, p. 215)

### `scholarly_argument_crubellier_prohairesis_as_principle_of_hu_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : prohairesis as principle of human action — Prohairesis serves to identify and characterize the principle (archê) of human actions; it is not a faculty but a fact or event, and is co-defined with praxis in a specifically human sense
- Constat : All four elements (identifies the principle of human actions; not a faculty; a fact or event; co-defined with praxis) are on p. 216. Crubellier writes 'principe', not 'archê', in these sentences.
  - « la notion de proairesis (προαίρεσις, rendu ici par « décision ») sert à identifier et à caractériser le principe des actions humaines. » [Les définitions de la prohairesis dans les Éthiques.md:91-92] (Crubellier 2020, p. 216)
  - « Elle n’a pas le statut d’une faculté (d’une façon générale, Aristote est assez parcimonieux en matière de facultés). » [Les définitions de la prohairesis dans les Éthiques.md:99-100] (Crubellier 2020, p. 216)
  - « la proairesis doit être pensée comme un fait, voire un événement. » [Les définitions de la prohairesis dans les Éthiques.md:103-104] (Crubellier 2020, p. 216)

### `scholarly_argument_crubellier_two_definitions_of_prohairesis_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : two definitions of prohairesis in the Ethics — There are two definitions of prohairesis in Aristotle's Ethics: Definition (A) as 'deliberative desire of things that depend on us' (βουλευτικὴ ὄρεξις τῶν ἐφ’ ἡμῖν), and Definition (B) as 'desiderative intellect or intellectual desire' (ὀρεκτικὸς νοῦς ἢ ὄρεξις διανοητική) from Nicomachean Ethics VI / Eudemian Ethics V
- Constat : The two definitions are set out on pp. 216-217 (Crubellier's own rendering of (A) is 'un désir délibératif (ὄρεξις βουλευτική) de choses qui dépendent de nous'; the fuller Greek of EN III is quoted later at line 802). The KG's '225-227' is where (A) is analysed (p. 225 'La définition (A) : le rôle crucial de la délibération'); (B) is analysed at p. 234; the contrast of the two at p. 224.
  - « L’une (A) se trouve dans l’introduction générale à l’étude des vertus et définit la décision comme « un désir délibératif (ὄρεξις βουλευτική) de choses qui dépendent de nous ». » [Les définitions de la prohairesis dans les Éthiques.md:105-114] (Crubellier 2020, p. 216)
  - « L’autre (B) se trouve dans l’introduction aux vertus intellectuelles (διανοητικαί), au début du livre VI de l’Éthique à Nicomaque (qui est aussi le livre V de l’Éthique à Eudème) » [Les définitions de la prohairesis dans les Éthiques.md:115-117] (Crubellier 2020, pp. 216-217)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_determinism_and_divine_will_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : determinism and divine will — The phrase 'for this is the will of God in Christ Jesus for you' (1 Th 5:18b) indicates that the triad of joy, prayer, and thanksgiving corresponds to God's will, suggesting a theological framework where human spiritual practices are aligned with divine purpose rather than independent self-determination
- Constat : Dettwiler mentions v. 18b only once, as the formal exception to the rhythmic run of eight imperatives (p. 12). He nowhere argues that 18b shows the triad 'corresponds to God's will', and the contrast with 'independent self-determination' is not his. His genuine claim that something is 'not a human performance but the act of God's Spirit' concerns the community's unity (p. 13) and joy (p. 14), not v. 18b.
  - « En fait, la séquence, à l'exception du v. 18b, contient une succession ininterrompue, à caractère rythmique - dans le texte grec, le verbe est toujours placé en dernière position -, de huit affirmations courtes, formulées toutes à l'impératif présent. » [Une exhortation de Paul aux Thessaloniciens,Priez sans cesse.md:173-180] (Dettwiler 2007, p. 12)
  - « Ainsi, Paul rappelle indirectement que cette unité n'est pas à concevoir comme une performance humaine, mais comme l'agir de l'Esprit de Dieu. » [Une exhortation de Paul aux Thessaloniciens,Priez sans cesse.md:231-235] (Dettwiler 2007, p. 13)
- Description proposée : Dettwiler notes that 1 Th 5:18b is the one break in the rhythmic sequence of eight present imperatives (5:16-22) and then reads vv. 16-18 as a triad 'joy - prayer - thanksgiving'. What he says is not a 'human performance' but 'the act of God's Spirit' is the community's unity (p. 13) and, likewise, joy, understood as the expression of a human who discovers himself receptive to the God of grace (p. 14). He draws no conclusion about divine will versus self-determination from 18b.
- Pages : 12-14

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_free_will_and_prayer_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and prayer — The exhortation to pray continually (1 Th 5:17) is embedded in a framework of divine grace that precedes and enables human response; moralizing readings are excluded, suggesting that prayer is not a purely autonomous human achievement but a response to prior divine acceptance
- Constat : Supported on p. 11 (exhortations presuppose prior experience of grace; moralising reading excluded; ethos = grateful response to unconditional acceptance) and pp. 13-14 (unity and joy not a 'performance humaine'). 'Free will' is the KG's label, not Dettwiler's vocabulary. page_map: line 140 -> p. 11.
  - « rappelons-nous qu'elles s'inscrivent sans exception dans l'expérience préalable de la grâce de Dieu. Ainsi, toute lecture moralisatrice est exclue. Le comportement de l'homme - ou, si l'on veut, son éthos - n'est rien d'autre que sa réponse reconnaissante à l'égard de son acceptation inconditionnelle par Dieu. » [Une exhortation de Paul aux Thessaloniciens,Priez sans cesse.md:136-141,101-105] (Dettwiler 2007, p. 11)
  - « que la joie, elle non plus, n'est pas comprise comme une performance humaine, mais comme l'expression de l'humain qui se découvre réceptif à l'égard du Dieu de la grâce. » [Une exhortation de Paul aux Thessaloniciens,Priez sans cesse.md:286-290] (Dettwiler 2007, p. 14)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_moral_responsibility_and_commu_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : moral responsibility and community ethics — The exhortations in 1 Th 5:12-22 are directed toward concrete communal life and mutual edification, with specific attention to the Thessalonian community's vulnerable situation; ethical behavior emerges from shared spiritual life rather than individual autonomous choice
- Constat : First half is exact (conclusive exhortation to a very young, externally pressured community; concrete acts of support, p. 11 and p. 13). The tail 'rather than individual autonomous choice' is an imported contrast: Dettwiler's contrasts are 'performance humaine' vs 'agir de l'Esprit' and 'plan spirituel' vs 'actes de soutien concrets'; he adds 'Religion et éthos sont indissociables'. He never opposes communal ethics to individual choice.
  - « Le texte se présente pourtant bien comme une exhortation conclusive adressée à une toute jeune communauté, exposée à des répressions venant de l'extérieur, spirituellement encore inexpérimentée, orpheline de la présence physique de son maître à penser » [Une exhortation de Paul aux Thessaloniciens,Priez sans cesse.md:113-121] (Dettwiler 2007, p. 11)
  - « Il est évident que ces deux perspectives ne s'excluent pas mutuellement, tout au contraire ! Religion et éthos sont indissociables. » [Une exhortation de Paul aux Thessaloniciens,Priez sans cesse.md:220-223] (Dettwiler 2007, p. 13)
- Description proposée : Dettwiler reads 1 Th 5:12-22 as a conclusive exhortation to a very young community, exposed to outside pressure, spiritually inexperienced and deprived of its founder (p. 11). Ecclesial unity is experienced not only in prayer but 'perhaps above all' in concrete acts of mutual support and solidarity (v. 11, 13-14); the two perspectives do not exclude each other: 'religion and ethos are inseparable' (p. 13). Ethos is the grateful response to God's unconditional acceptance (p. 11).
- Pages : 11, 13

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_authenticity_of_colossians_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : authenticity of Colossians — The letter is likely post-Pauline, written in the 60s of the 1st century by an anonymous author from Paul's close circle of collaborators, who sought to preserve and actualize Paul's heritage
- Constat : Hinted file ('Démystification céleste', an article on Col 1,15-20) is a different work; the right file is 'Epître aux Colossiens commentaire.md' (Focant/Marguerat, Le Nouveau Testament commenté, 2012, pp. 891-910). The passage sits on p. 892 (the '892' at line 86 is a page footer; page_map places line 92 on p. 893). Dettwiler hedges with 'probablement' and 'si cette hypothèse est correcte'; 'likely' preserves that.
  - « plaident en faveur d'une date de rédaction après la mort de l'apôtre, dans les années 60 » [Epître aux Colossiens commentaire.md:67-68] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 892)
  - « Si cette hypothèse est correcte, l'auteur de Col reste anonyme. Mais sa familiarité avec la personne et la pensée de Paul indique qu'il faisait probablement partie du cercle étroit des collaborateurs de Paul ; il a le souci de préserver et d'actualiser son héritage dans une situation historique changeante. » [Epître aux Colossiens commentaire.md:69-72] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 892)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_freedom_in_christ_vs_ascetic_d_1` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : freedom in Christ vs. ascetic determinism — The Colossian community already possesses the fullness of salvation in Christ (2:6-15) and therefore does not need alternative doctrines of salvation; the author immunizes the community against the 'Colossian philosophy' rather than directly refuting it
- Constat : Description is faithful but both sentences are on p. 893, not 892 (page_map: line 92 'immuniser' -> p. 893; line 109 'plénitude du salut' -> p. 893). The topic label 'ascetic determinism' is the KG's: Dettwiler speaks of 'une religiosité ascétique et extatique' and never of determinism.
  - « l'auteur de Col ne vise pas directement les adversaires et n'essaie pas de déconstruire leur système de pensée par une argumentation détaillée. Son but est plutôt d'immuniser la communauté colossienne contre ce courant alternatif » [Epître aux Colossiens commentaire.md:90-93] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 893)
  - « La partie la plus argumentative de l'écrit postule que la communauté destinataire a déjà accès à la plénitude du salut [2,6-15) et n'a donc pas besoin d'autres doctrines de salut [2,16-23). » [Epître aux Colossiens commentaire.md:108-110] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 893)
- Description proposée : Dettwiler: the author of Colossians does not attack the adversaries directly nor dismantle their system; his aim is to 'immunise' the community against this alternative current by denouncing the judgmental spirit tied to its ascetic and ecstatic practices; his decisive weapon is christological (1,15-20): the community already shares fully in salvation, so adhering to another system would be anachronistic. The argumentative body (2,6-4,1) posits that the community already has access to the fullness of salvation (2,6-15) and needs no other doctrine of salvation (2,16-23).
- Pages : 893

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_human_agency_and_salvation_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : human agency and salvation — The author reinterprets 'hope' not as a human disposition but as a transcendent salvation good located 'in the heavens'; the community's identity is established through reception of the Gospel rather than through ascetic self-determination
- Constat : The reinterpretation of 'hope' is exact (p. 895; page_map line 172 -> 895) and identity reached 'dans l'écoute de l'Évangile' is there too. But 'rather than through ascetic self-determination' is not Dettwiler's contrast: on p. 895 the recipients are 'saints' not because of their moral or religious qualities but because called by God and sanctified in Christ. The asceticism theme belongs to the introduction (pp. 892-893), not to this comment on Col 1,1-8.
  - « « espérance » : l'auteur ne la conçoit plus comme une disposition humaine, mais comme un bien de salut transcendant qui se trouve « aux cieux » [voir Ph 3,20). » [Epître aux Colossiens commentaire.md:171-174] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 895)
  - « Les destinataires sont des « saints » non principalement à cause de leurs qualités morales ou religieuses, mais parce qu'ils ont été appelés par Dieu et sanctifiés « en » Christ. » [Epître aux Colossiens commentaire.md:175-178] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 895)
- Description proposée : On Col 1,1-8 Dettwiler notes that the author reworks the Pauline triad faith-love-hope by reading 'hope' no longer as a human disposition but as a transcendent good of salvation located 'in the heavens' (cf. Ph 3,20). The recipients are 'saints' not chiefly for their moral or religious qualities but because called by God and sanctified 'in' Christ; the author reminds them what their present religious identity is and how they reached it: by hearing the Gospel, received through Epaphras.
- Pages : 895

### `scholarly_argument_dodson_divine_causation_and_determini_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : divine causation and determinism — Orrey McFarland's chapter argues that both Philo and Paul employ 'prepositional metaphysics' where God is the primary cause of all things, but this does not eliminate human agency; rather, human agency operates within divine causation.
- Constat : Hinted file (Paul and Seneca in Dialogue, Dodson/Briones, Brill) is a different volume; the right file is 'Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md' (01_Philosophie_antique). McFarland's chapter (pp. 117-133) is about prepositions defining divine causation (Philo: God as first cause, Logos as instrument; Paul: Rom 11.36, 1 Cor 8.6, Christ as intermediary agent, God/Jesus distinction). The chapter contains no claim that divine causation 'does not eliminate human agency' or that 'human agency operates within divine causation'; every occurrence of 'agency' in it concerns the Logos/Christ as instrument. The KG invents the human-agency thesis.
  - « The argument is that while Philo and Paul both use prepositions differently, and have dissimilar views of causation, Philo’s use of prepositional metaphysics provides a way forward for placing Paul within the tradition of using prepositions with technical meanings for defining causation. » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:826-830] (Pitts, Introduction, p. 7)
  - « for Paul, Jesus is the intermediary agent of divine causation – he would be the ‘instrument’ according to Philo – and yet he is also identified with God through Paul’s reworking of monotheistic formulas. » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:7251-7253] (McFarland 2017, p. 132)
  - « Both Philo and Paul display forms of prepositional metaphysics that are influenced by multiple philosophical traditions, and yet each is able to use prepositions to define causation precisely as they intended to do. » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:7199-7202] (McFarland 2017, p. 131)
- Description proposée : Orrey McFarland (ch. 6) argues that Paul, like Philo, can 'safely be placed' within the traditional use of 'prepositional metaphysics' (prepositions with technical meanings defining causation). Philo is the clear example: God is the first cause 'by which', the Logos the instrument 'through which'. Paul (Rom 11.36; 1 Cor 8.6) uses prepositions to distinguish God and Jesus in divine causation while identifying Jesus with God through reworked monotheistic formulas; Philo and Paul share the grammar but not identical views of causation. Human agency is not at issue.
- Pages : 117-133 (esp. 131-133); editors' summary p. 7

### `scholarly_argument_dodson_free_will_and_determinism_in_p_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and determinism in Pauline studies — The volume as a whole does not take a unified position on free will; it is a collection of essays exploring various philosophical backgrounds to Paul's thought. Dodson's own chapter focuses on apocalyptic eschatology in Seneca and Paul, not directly on free will.
- Constat : A bibliographic description rather than a scholarly claim, but accurate: Contents (p. vii) gives Dodson's ch. 2 at pp. 33-53 on apocalyptic eschatology; Engberg-Pedersen's Foreword (p. xvii) stresses the absence of a uniform approach; the Introduction (by Andrew W. Pitts, not Dodson) calls the project 'fairly eclectic' (p. 11).
  - « 2 Elements of Apocalyptic Eschatology in Seneca’s Writings and Paul’s Letters Joseph R. Dodson 33 » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:120-121] (Contents, p. vii)
  - « There is no uniformity of approach here, but this fact in itself invites us to think harder about how to understand the theoretical issue. » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:452-453] (Engberg-Pedersen, Foreword, p. xvii)
  - « As the reader may have gathered by this point, Paul and the Greco-Roman Philosophical Tradition embodies a fairly eclectic project. » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:995-996] (Pitts, Introduction, p. 11)

### `scholarly_argument_dodson_stoic_fate_and_christian_provi_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic fate and Christian providence — Several contributors engage with Stoic concepts of fate (heimarmene) and providence (pronoia) as background for understanding Paul's thought, but distinguish Christian eschatological hope from Stoic fatalism.
- Constat : Contributors do discuss Seneca's fate, fortune and providence (Dodson ch. 2, pp. 50-51; Nygaard ch. 12, p. 252; Tabb ch. 13, p. 275), but in Seneca's Latin terms, not the Greek 'heimarmene'/'pronoia' the node supplies, and none uses 'Stoic fatalism'. The contrasts are specific: Paul never names fate/fortune among his personified powers and draws on an apocalyptic tradition (Dodson); Seneca and Paul both see God's providence in creation (Dodson); Acts 17 subverts the Stoic worldview (Tabb). The flat 'distinguish Christian eschatological hope from Stoic fatalism' flattens these.
  - « In addition to death, Seneca features fate and fortune as terrorists in this age. » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:3004] (Dodson 2017, p. 50)
  - « Paul never names fate and fortune in his lists of personified powers. This shows that while the Stoic underlines the importance of pursuing philosophy whether life’s suffering is dictated by fate, sovereignty or chance (see Ep. 16.7–8), the apostle draws upon an apocalyptic tradition that encourages the saints to persevere despite the work of evil angels. » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:3061-3066] (Dodson 2017, p. 51)
  - « As a Stoic, Seneca equates god with the material world’s efficient cause and thus views everything as determined by the divine will. Human beings should therefore willingly embrace the decrees of ‘Fate’ (Prov. 5.8) » [Dodson et Pitts - 2017 - Paul and the Greco-Roman philosophical tradition.md:14388-14390] (Tabb 2017, p. 275)
- Description proposée : Several contributors treat Seneca's fate, fortune and providence as comparanda. Dodson (ch. 2) notes Seneca casts fate and fortune as 'terrorists in this age', whereas Paul never names them among his personified powers and draws on an apocalyptic tradition; yet 'Seneca and Paul both conclude that God's providence can be seen in creation' (pp. 50-51). Tabb (ch. 13) observes that Seneca views everything as determined by the divine will and urges embracing the decrees of Fate, while Acts 17 challenges and subverts the Stoic worldview (p. 275). Nygaard (ch. 12) touches on fate and freedom at p. 252.
- Pages : 50-51, 252, 275

### `scholarly_argument_everson_ancient_vs_modern_ethics_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : ancient vs. modern ethics — Ancient ethics is primarily concerned with virtue and eudaimonia (the evaluation of a person's life as a whole), whereas modern moral philosophy has traditionally focused on determining the right action in particular circumstances. However, this contrast has become blurred due to recent revival of virtue ethics.
- Constat : The contrast (virtue/eudaimonia and the life as a whole vs determining the right action) is exact (p. 1). But Everson attributes the blurring to the influence of ancient writings on moral philosophy over the past quarter-century, itself driven by a reaction against Humean assumptions (p. 2); he does not speak of a 'revival of virtue ethics'.
  - « No branch of philosophy has been more influenced by serious consideration of ancient writings in the past quarter of a century than has moral philosophy - or 'ethics', as it is often now called (a change in nomenclature which is itself a sign of that influence). This has made it more difficult to delineate helpful contrasts between ancient and modern moral philosophy. » [[Companions to Ancient Thought] Stephen Everson (ed.) - Ethics (1998, Cambridge University Press).md:182-186] (Everson 1998, p. 1)
  - « whilst modern moral philosophers focused on the question of how to determine the right action in any given circumstance, the ancients were primarily concerned with issues of character and the evaluation of a person's life considered as a whole. » [[Companions to Ancient Thought] Stephen Everson (ed.) - Ethics (1998, Cambridge University Press).md:199-202] (Everson 1998, p. 1)
- Description proposée : Everson: twenty years earlier one could easily contrast ancient ethics (virtue and eudaimonia, the evaluation of a person's life as a whole, character) with modern moral philosophy (utilitarian vs deontological accounts of the right action). Because moral philosophy has since been deeply influenced by ancient writings, largely through a reaction against Humean assumptions about moral psychology, it has become harder to draw usefully broad distinctions between ancient and contemporary moral philosophy.
- Pages : 1-2

### `scholarly_argument_everson_eudaimonia_and_hedonism_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : eudaimonia and hedonism — Epicurus is exceptional among ancient ethicists in offering a hedonistic account of eudaimonia, though he accepts eudaimonia as the final end. The Cyrenaics are even more radical, maintaining hedonism while denying that happiness is the final end.
- Constat : Exact, from n. 1 on p. 1; only the hedge 'seems to accept' is dropped.
  - « Epicurus is exceptional in this respect. Although he seems to accept the status of eudaimonia as the final end, he does attempt to provide a hedonistic account of what it is to be happy. In this, he is less radical than the Cyrenaics, who maintain hedonism whilst denying that happiness is the final end. » [[Companions to Ancient Thought] Stephen Everson (ed.) - Ethics (1998, Cambridge University Press).md:205-208] (Everson 1998, p. 1 n. 1)

### `scholarly_argument_everson_moral_psychology_and_motivatio_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

### `scholarly_argument_fantino_determinism_vs_divine_initiati_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)

Printed pages unverifiable (preprint; passage at preprint p. 4).

Mais, le don de l'Esprit permet d'autre part de rejeter les convoitises, ou plus exactement la soumission aux convoitises, conséquence du péché, tout comme la mort. » [Fantino - 1998 - The passage of the first Adam to the seconde Adam .md:191-195] (Fantino 1998, preprint p. 4; printed page unverified)
- Description proposée : Fantino: for Irenaeus the passage from the psychic to the spiritual condition (1 Cor 15,22 read through 15,44-46) is not the work of the human being but of the Creator (Haer 5,13,3), more precisely of the incarnate Word who gives the Holy Spirit. ### `scholarly_argument_fantino_free_will_and_the_psychike_spi_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : free will and the psychike/spiritual progression — Irenaeus's anthropology involves a progression from psychike to spiritual condition that is not achieved by human effort alone but requires the gift of the Holy Spirit through Christ; the human being's moral choices (doing evil or returning to good) are real but insufficient for attaining life without divine grace
- Constat : 'Not achieved by human effort alone' softens Fantino's 'n'est pas l'œuvre de l'être humain mais du Créateur'; 'free will' and 'moral choices are real' are not his vocabulary. The Haer 5,12,2 quotation does support the asymmetry (turning to evil loses life; returning to good and receiving the Spirit regains it). Printed pages unverifiable (preprint).

- Description proposée : Fantino reads Irenaeus's economy as a passage from the psychic condition of the first Adam to the spiritual condition of the second, accomplished by the incarnate Word who gives the Spirit; it is the Creator's work, not the human being's, and is determined by the incarnation, not by sin. Irenaeus's dissymmetry (Haer 5,12,2): turning to evil loses life, but doing good does not of itself give life; the Holy Spirit is needed in addition. Evil is 'second' and is used by God to bring the economy to completion.

### `scholarly_argument_goodrich_identity_of_in_romans_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : identity of ἐγώ in Romans 7 — The speaker's plight under sin is analogous to Israel's experiences of deception, death, and exile. The Isaianic echoes (Isa 49-50) connect allusions to Israel's early history in Rom 7.7-13 to images of the nation's later history in 7.14-25, suggesting the speaker represents Israel's corporate experience of exile under sin's power rather than individual moral autobiography.
- Constat : The 'analogous to Israel's experiences' and the Isaianic linkage are exact (abstract). But 'rather than individual moral autobiography' inverts Goodrich: he holds that the narrative 'functions as reconstructed autobiography', that ἐγώ stands 'first and foremost for Paul' / 'unbelieving Paul as a representative of Israel', and that it represents any person who encounters the law. Israel is the representative frame of Paul's own story, not an alternative to it.
  - « ἐγώ, while standing first and foremost for Paul, represents any person who encounters the law and thus shares in Israel’s experience of deception, death, and exile begun at Sinai » [sold_under_sin_echoes_of_exile_in_romans_71425.md:716-718] (Goodrich 2013; printed page unverified)
  - « recent studies have shown convincingly that Paul’s narrative functions as reconstructed autobiography » [sold_under_sin_echoes_of_exile_in_romans_71425.md:723-724] (Goodrich 2013; printed page unverified)
  - « Largely assuming, based on other studies, that ἐγώ stands for unbelieving Paul as a representative of Israel » [sold_under_sin_echoes_of_exile_in_romans_71425.md:1048-1049] (Goodrich 2013, Conclusion; printed page unverified)
- Description proposée : Goodrich argues that echoes of LXX Isa 49-50 in Rom 7.14-25 evoke Israel's Babylonian captivity and the sin-exile-restoration paradigm, linking the allusions to Israel's early history (Sinai) in 7.7-13 to its later history in 7.14-25, so that the speaker's plight is analogous to Israel's deception, death and exile. He takes ἐγώ to stand 'first and foremost for Paul' (reconstructed autobiography) as 'a representative of Israel', and thereby for any person who encounters the law.

### `scholarly_argument_haacker_theodicy_and_divine_responsibi_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Theodicy and divine responsibility for Israel's unbelief — Paul in Romans 9-11 develops a historical interpretation that traces Israel's obduracy to God's will and assigns it a positive purpose in God's historical plan, unlike other early Christian explanations that were psychological or moral; this raises theodicy questions about God's own responsibility
- Constat : The core (Paul traces Israel's closure to God's will with a positive purpose; other early Christians explained it psychologically/morally) is exact, pp. 210-211, but Haacker names a third alternative (rationalised as foretold in the OT) and the theodicy question he identifies is whether God's word/promises have failed (Rom 9.6a; 3.1ff.), i.e. God's faithfulness, since Israel is constituted by God's promises. 'God's own responsibility' is the KG's reframing.
  - « kommt Paulus in Rom 9-11 zu einer Geschichtsdeutung, die Israels Verschlossenheit fur die Christusbotschaft auf Gottes Willen zuriickfiihrt und ihr einen positiven Sinn und Zweck im Geschichtsplan Gottes beimiflt. » [die_geschichtstheologie_von_rom_911_im_lichte_philonischer_schriftauslegung.md:88-90,134-135] (Haacker 1997, pp. 210-211)
  - « vor allem psychologisch erklarte und dabei moralisch bewertete oder allenfalls als im Alten Testament schon geweissagt zu rationalisieren suchte » [die_geschichtstheologie_von_rom_911_im_lichte_philonischer_schriftauslegung.md:85-88] (Haacker 1997, p. 210)
  - « Gott selbst ist durch das Erscheinungsbild Israels in Frage gestellt, weil Israel durch die ihm gegebenen VerheiBungen Gottes konstituiert wird. » [die_geschichtstheologie_von_rom_911_im_lichte_philonischer_schriftauslegung.md:156-158] (Haacker 1997, p. 211)
- Description proposée : Haacker: whereas the rest of early Christianity explained the Jewish majority's rejection of the Jesus movement psychologically, judged it morally, or at most rationalised it as foretold in the OT, Paul in Rom 9-11 offers an interpretation of history that traces Israel's closure to the Christ message back to God's will and assigns it a positive meaning in God's plan. For Paul the Israel question is a question of theodicy: God himself is called into question by Israel's condition, because Israel is constituted by God's promises; hence the thesis of 9.6a that God's word has not failed.
- Pages : 210-211

The node's inference from revelation to determinism is its own; the pages should extend to 280-283.

  - « God has created man, and has predestined every man for evil or for good, for destruction or for salvation; and man as a creature is completely dependent upon God. » [the-view-of-man-in-the-qumran-hodayot.md:226-228] (Hyatt 1956, p. 280)
  - « The author has a strong sense of determinism, such as is found in Serek and other documents of Qumran, and in Josephus's description of the Essenes » [the-view-of-man-in-the-qumran-hodayot.md:395-397] (Hyatt 1956, p. 283)
- Description proposée : Hyatt: the author of the Hodayot claims to have received a revelation of secrets from God (p. 277; 'These things I knew from thy understanding, for thou didst reveal to me wondrous secrets', 35.21). On the origin of man's frailty and sin the Hodayot are clear: God created man and predestined every man for good or evil; man is completely dependent on God ('nothing is known without thy will'), a 'strong sense of determinism' shared with the Serek's doctrine of the two spirits (pp. 280-283).
- Pages : 277-278, 280-281, 283

### `scholarly_argument_hyatt_human_nature_and_free_will_in__0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : human nature and free will in Qumran theology — The Hodayot present a deeply pessimistic view of human nature: man is fundamentally a 'creature of clay,' 'a spring of impurity,' 'a crucible of iniquity,' and 'a spirit of error and perversity' without inherent understanding. Human moral capacity is radically deficient without divine intervention.
- Constat : Exact: 'more pessimistic' (p. 278), the 35.21-3 quotation with all four phrases and 'without understanding' (p. 278), weakness and sinfulness in the 'natural' state before God's mercy (p. 283). Pages 278 and 283 rather than 276-278.
  - « It is a view that is consistently maintained, and that is more pessimistic than one usually encounters in Hebrew-Jewish writings. » [the-view-of-man-in-the-qumran-hodayot.md:125-127] (Hyatt 1956, p. 278)
  - « He has a deep sense of man's weakness and sinfulness in the 'natural' state before he experiences God's mercy and hesed. » [the-view-of-man-in-the-qumran-hodayot.md:397-399] (Hyatt 1956, p. 283)

### `scholarly_argument_hyatt_suffering_and_divine_disciplin_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : suffering and divine discipline — The author presents suffering as sent by God Himself as discipline, from which God subsequently rescues the sufferer. This implies that human experiences of affliction are divinely ordained rather than resulting from free human choices, and that deliverance is equally God's prerogative.
- Constat : First sentence exact (p. 277). The second ('affliction divinely ordained rather than resulting from free human choices') is not drawn by Hyatt: in the same paragraph he lists the human agents of the suffering (false teachers, friends who slandered him, exile), and in n. 1 p. 283 he observes that the Covenanters did not see the contradiction between their determinism and their voluntarism.
  - « Yet, the author has experienced much suffering and sorrow, out of which God has rescued him, though it was God Himself who sent the suffering upon him as discipline. Among his enemies are false teachers » [the-view-of-man-in-the-qumran-hodayot.md:81-83] (Hyatt 1956, p. 277)
  - « The author met opposition even from his friends and members of the sect, who slandered him to his enemies (39. 22-5), and he was taken into Egypt (39. 29; cf. 39. 5). » [the-view-of-man-in-the-qumran-hodayot.md:87-89] (Hyatt 1956, p. 277)
- Description proposée : Hyatt: the author of the Hodayot, probably the Teacher of Righteousness, experienced much suffering and sorrow, out of which God rescued him, 'though it was God Himself who sent the suffering upon him as discipline'. The suffering came through false teachers, friends and sect members who slandered him, and exile; he wrote the Hodayot to express gratitude for deliverance. Hyatt draws no inference here about divine ordination versus human choice.
- Pages : 277

- Constat : Preface p. viii confirms every element (brief Augustine/Scotus/Ockham, omission of Neoplatonists, Church Fathers, Abelard, lesser-known mediaevals, reason = concentrating on an Aristotelian outlook). Irwin adds a self-doubt the node drops: the decision 'may have been mistaken'. Page map (native, conf 0.95) gives p. viii.

### `scholarly_argument_nystr_m_justin_martyr_s_logos_doctrine_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)

- Constat : Nyström's frame is functional, not doctrinal: he declines to reconstruct Justin's Logos theology and studies four 'uses' of the doctrine in the argument; the first (p. 85) is to make Christianity 'a rational and sound faith'. Universality is treated under the argument from antiquity (pp. 88-89): every wise person recognized the truth 'through the partly innate Logos', so the wicked 'are without excuse' — they 'have chosen'. 'Sown in all humans' is not Nyström's phrasing. Pages from running headers (page_map AUDIT-FAIL, 4 low).

- Description proposée : Nyström analyses the function, not the content, of Justin's Logos doctrine. Its first and simplest use is to establish Christianity as 'a rational and sound faith' by identifying reason with Christ (p. 85). Its most important use answers the charge of newness: Christian doctrine equals what has been recognized as wise 'in all ages' through 'the partly innate Logos', so it is relevant to everyone and the wicked, who 'have chosen' to live without reason, are 'without excuse' (pp. 88-89).
- Pages : 82-89 (esp. 85, 88-89)

### `scholarly_argument_olive_ancient_causality_vs_modern_ef_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : ancient causality vs. modern efficient causality — The ancient conception of causality was modeled on the logical pattern of essential inherence of the predicate in its subject, whereas the modern notion of efficient cause, derived from theology, led to thinking of causality as a genuine relation between distinct entities. - Constat : The Résumé states the thesis in these exact terms (essential inherence of predicate in subject vs efficient cause 'issue de la théologie' as 'authentique relation'; ancient scheme cannot deduce classical mechanics, which follows validly from the modern one). - Constat : Matches: no 'répugnance à multiplier des causes de natures très diverses', Aristotle's four causes, 'comme s'ils ignoraient' Kim's principle, moderns 'tout à fait avertis'. The passage sits on p. 3 (footer '2' at line 131, '3' at line 185), not pp. 2-3.

Kim appelle le principe d’exclusion explicative, en vertu duquel on ne peut admettre, pour un même événement, deux explications causales complètes et indépendantes, ce dont les modernes étaient par contre tout à fait avertis » [Olive - 2010 - Retour sur l'efficience des causes.md:141-144] (p. 3, from page footer)

### `scholarly_argument_olive_translation_and_interpretation_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : translation and interpretation of aition/aitia — Against Frede's radical suggestion that aition/aitia should not be translated as 'cause', Olive argues that strictly lexical approaches fail; one must study causality as an intellectual operator in authors' reasoning rather than through word etymologies or implicit subtexts of modern terms.
- Constat : Correct in outline, but drops two qualifications: Olive first grants Frede's 'option radicale' a merit (it exposes ill-founded interpretive difficulties such as final causes), and his decisive objection is substantive, not only methodological — the ancients 'semblent n'avoir ignoré ni la notion de cause productrice'. Pages 2-3 confirmed from footers.
  - « Cette option radicale aurait au moins le mérite de faire apparaître le caractère mal fondé de certaines difficultés d’interprétation » [Olive - 2010 - Retour sur l'efficience des causes.md:114-133] (pp. 2-3, from page footers)
  - « La thèse de M Frede se heurte alors à une difficulté de taille : les anciens semblent n’avoir ignoré ni la notion de cause productrice » [Olive - 2010 - Retour sur l'efficience des causes.md:158-159] (p. 3)
- Description proposée : Olive reports Frede's suggestion that aition/aitia were perhaps wrongly translated 'cause', grants this 'option radicale' the merit of dissolving some pseudo-problems (e.g. final causes), but holds that a 'strictly lexicological' approach cannot suffice: translating aition as 'responsable' or 'ce pourquoi' yields meaningless statements. Cause must be studied as an 'opérateur intellectuel' in authors' reasoning. Frede's thesis then meets 'une difficulté de taille': the ancients did know the notion of productive cause.
- Pages : 2-3

The node presents as the authors' thesis what they report as the general view ('on considère en général', 'la plupart acceptent') and drops their caveat that 'certains rejettent ce consensus' (Watson) on the ground that one cannot freely choose the action judged worse.
