  - « Meme si la plupart acceptent que l'akrasie differe des actions compulsives, comme celles causees par un desir ou une emotion irresistible, certains rejettent ce consensus. » (Pironet & Tappolet 2003, p. 630) [Faiblesse de la raison ou faiblesse de volonté- peut-on -- Pironet, Fabienne; Tappolet, Christine -- Dialogue- Canadian Philosophical Review -- 10.1017-S0012217300005680 -- 9886b2062c76beadb46f63e4215fed1e -- Anna’s Archive.md:208-211]
- Description proposée : Reporting the post-Davidson debate, Pironet and Tappolet note that most now hold 'strict akrasia' (Mele) possible: the agent judges y better than x, is or believes himself free and able to do y, yet does x 'librement et intentionnellement'. Postulating that the akratic act is free distinguishes it from compulsive acts and admits that one can freely choose an action judged inferior. They add that some (Watson) reject this consensus precisely because they deny one can freely choose the worse option.
- Pages : 630

### `scholarly_argument_pironet_historical_development_of_prac_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : historical development of practical irrationality from antiquity to early modern — There is significant distance between ancient and contemporary perspectives on akrasia: for Aristotle, it would be extraordinary that someone with knowledge of the good would not act on it, whereas contemporary views treat akrasia as not only possible but relatively common; the article traces the evolution from Socrates to Leibniz
- Constat : The 'Sophie' example on p. 628 says exactly this: contrary to Aristotle, it would not seem extraordinary to us that one who knows the good fails to act on it; contemporary conceptions treat akrasia as 'non seulement possible, mais aussi relativement fréquent', even 'strict akrasia'; second aim = evolution 'de Socrate à Leibniz'.
  - « il ne nous semblerait pas du tout extraordinaire que, possedant ce savoir, elle n'agisse pourtant pas en fonction de ce qu'elle sait. » (Pironet & Tappolet 2003, p. 628) [Faiblesse de la raison ou faiblesse de volonté- peut-on -- Pironet, Fabienne; Tappolet, Christine -- Dialogue- Canadian Philosophical Review -- 10.1017-S0012217300005680 -- 9886b2062c76beadb46f63e4215fed1e -- Anna’s Archive.md:107-109]

### `scholarly_argument_pouderon_human_choice_and_conversion_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)

- Constat : Only the quoted clause exists, and it is from the back-cover blurb (not 'préface p. 9'). ### `scholarly_argument_secord_tatian_challenges_astrological_determinism` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Secord argues that Tatian's reclassification of the Greek gods as demons supplies the mechanism for his challenge to astrological determinism. Tatian belongs to a second-century controversy also represented by Favorinus and Sextus Empiricus: he treats astral fate as the unjust construction of demonic lawgivers, rejects the authority of the wandering astral bodies, and places Christians 'above fate'. Secord's point is historical and contextual: Tatian is participating in the shared intellectual debate about whether astrology entails that everything happens according to fate.
- Constat : All elements verified: demons/gods invented astrology (p. 84); recharacterization of gods as demons enables the challenge to astrological determinism, alongside Favorinus and Sextus Empiricus (p. 85); demons 'defined fate', 'extremely unjust', Tatian's 'We are above fate … we have rejected its lawgivers' and rejection of the 'wandering' astral bodies (p. 87). Running headers confirm pp. 85-87; page_map agrees (conf 0.81-0.86). The demons-invented-astrology point starts on p. 84.
  - « “We are above fate, and instead of wandering demons we have recognized an unwander- » (Secord 2021, p. 87) [(Inventing Christianity_ 2) Jared Secord - Christian Intellectuals and the Roman Empire_ From Justin Martyr to Origen-Penn State University Press (2021).md:3995-3996]
  - « Demons of this sort had no authority over Tatian, who joined Favorinus and others in rejecting what they portrayed as the deterministic claims of astrologers. » (Secord 2021, p. 87) [(Inventing Christianity_ 2) Jared Secord - Christian Intellectuals and the Roman Empire_ From Justin Martyr to Origen-Penn State University Press (2021).md:4007-4009]

### `scholarly_argument_secord_tatian_reworks_astrological_idiom_from_within` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Secord reads Tatian as an informed participant in astrological literature rather than an external denunciator of it. Tatian's surprising overlap with Vettius Valens on demons, his use of technical astrological vocabulary, and his reuse of astrologers' own language of demonic instruction show that he turns the idiom of astrology against its practitioners: their heavenly informants become evil demons and their fate becomes unjust. Demonology is therefore the field in which Tatian has more in common with astrologers than a simple Christianity-versus-paganism model would predict.
- Constat : Verified on pp. 86-87: 'surprising agreement with Vettius Valens' on demons in astrology; technical terms τὸ ἐπικρατῆσαν and ἀστροθεσία (as printed in Secord); demons introduced 'the exceedingly unjust [idea] of fate'; Tatian 'attempts to turn the claims of astrologists back on them'; 'Demonology therefore proved to be an area where Tatian had more in common with astrologers than one would expect'.
  - « As it happens, Tatian is in surprising agreement with Vettius Valens on several points, particularly on the topic of the involvement of demons in astrology. » (Secord 2021, p. 86) [(Inventing Christianity_ 2) Jared Secord - Christian Intellectuals and the Roman Empire_ From Justin Martyr to Origen-Penn State University Press (2021).md:3944-3946]

Paul himself nowhere made the question about the origin of faith a topic of discussion. » (Wolter 2015, p. 78) [Michael Wolter - Paul_ An Outline of His Theology (2015, Baylor University Press) - libgen.lc.md:3806-3808]
- Description proposée : Wolter treats πίστις as the addressees' agreement with the proclaimed gospel — the response the message is designed to find (p. 71). On whether faith is a free 'decision' or a 'gift' given in a 'predestinarian' way, he holds that the problem lies with Paul's interpreters, not with Paul: Paul 'nowhere made the question about the origin of faith a topic of discussion', a gap exegetes fill with their own systems (pp. 78-79).
- Pages : 71-79 (esp. 78-79)

### `scholarly_argument_wolter_free_will_and_divine_predestin_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and divine predestination in Paul's theology — Wolter addresses Paul's theology of election and the relationship between divine initiative and human response, particularly in Romans 9-11, where he examines the 'aporia' of Israel's situation without resolving it into a simple deterministic or libertarian framework
- Constat : Wolter's 'aporia' (§51, pp. 413-422) is not about divine initiative vs human response or determinism vs freedom. It is the contradiction between two tenets Paul wants to hold: 'there is no distinction' between Israel and the nations (Rom 3:22; 10:12) and Israel's abiding election and special place (Rom 11:25-29). Paul 'has not resolved the problem of Israel'; the tension 'separates the Jewish Paul from the Christian Paul' and is 'insurmountable'. 'Deterministic or libertarian framework' is imported vocabulary Wolter never uses.
  - « Thus Paul has not resolved the problem of Israel in Romans. Rather, the consideration concerning Israel has led him to an aporia. » (Wolter 2015, p. 421) [Michael Wolter - Paul_ An Outline of His Theology (2015, Baylor University Press) - libgen.lc.md:20063-20064]
  - « Paul wishes to take seriously two theological tenets that exclude each other. On the one hand stands the tenet formulated identically in two places in Romans: “There is no distinction” (3:22; 10:12). » (Wolter 2015, p. 422) [Michael Wolter - Paul_ An Outline of His Theology (2015, Baylor University Press) - libgen.lc.md:20117-20119]
- Description proposée : In §51 Wolter argues that Romans 9-11 ends in a 'theological aporia': Paul cannot reconcile 'gospel' and 'election'. He holds both that 'there is no distinction' between Israel and the nations, all being justified solely through Christ-faith, and (Rom 11:25-29) that Israel keeps its elected special place; in 11:25-27 he must resort to apocalyptic revelation. Paul has not resolved the problem of Israel; the tension separates 'the Jewish Paul from the Christian Paul' and is 'insurmountable' (pp. 413-422).
- Pages : 413-422

### `scholarly_argument_wolter_gospel_as_divine_power_and_hum_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : gospel as divine power and human proclamation — Wolter analyzes the gospel as both divine event and human message, examining how Paul's theology maintains that the gospel's effectiveness is not dependent on human will or effort but on God's power, yet requires human proclamation and response
- Constat : Wolter does not say the gospel's effectiveness is 'not dependent on human will or effort'. His claim in §8 is the reverse emphasis: 'it is first of all faith that causes the gospel to become the "power of God for salvation" and unleashes its salvific efficacy' (pp. 67-68); the gospel is 'power of God' on the basis of its content, and 'Christ's gospel' is 'in principle independent of the person of its preacher' (p. 59) — a different point from human will. rg for 'human will/effort' finds nothing in §7-8.
  - « Thus it is first of all faith that causes the gospel to become the “power of God for salvation” and unleashes its salv- » (Wolter 2015, p. 67) [Michael Wolter - Paul_ An Outline of His Theology (2015, Baylor University Press) - libgen.lc.md:3377-3378]
  - « “Christ’s gospel” is in principle independent of the person of its preacher. » (Wolter 2015, p. 59) [Michael Wolter - Paul_ An Outline of His Theology (2015, Baylor University Press) - libgen.lc.md:2932]
- Description proposée : Wolter: the gospel is 'God's gospel' and 'Christ's gospel' by its content, independent 'of the person of its preacher' (p. 59). Heard as God's word, it does what God's word always does — it has an effect. 'It is first of all faith that causes the gospel to become the "power of God for salvation"' (Rom 1:16); unbelief gains no part in its efficacy, and Paul speaks of the gospel as 'power' only on the side of faith and salvation, never as directly working perdition (pp. 67-68).
- Pages : 55-70 (esp. 59, 67-68)

### `scholarly_argument_zetterholm_jewishness_of_paul_and_christi_2` — **MISATTRIBUTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Jewishness of Paul and Christian identity — Notes that traditional views of Paul as embodying contradiction between Judaism and Christianity have made him a model for Christian identity formation, which feminist and post-Shoah perspectives challenge
- Constat : The text is a review article of Kathy Ehrensperger's That We May Be Mutually Encouraged. The node label presents it as Zetterholm's own observation. Page 254 correct (running header line 740).

- Description proposée : Zetterholm, reviewing Ehrensperger (2004), reports her point that the traditional image of Paul 'has made him the chief model for the irreconcilable contradiction between Judaism and Christianity', so that the debate about Paul is 'basically a debate about Christian identity'; feminist interpretations, post-Shoah perspectives and studies of Paul's Jewishness have challenged this dominant discourse. Zetterholm's own reservation (p. 256) is that her Paul is 'perhaps too idealistic'.

### `scholarly_argument_destr_e_aristotle_on_eph_h_min_and_det_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle on eph' hêmin and determinism — The volume presents multiple deterministic or anti-indeterministic readings of Aristotle; the expression eph' hêmin is not yet a technical term in Aristotle and he nowhere defines it; alternate-possibilities readings are contested
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim (incl. the typo 'beginning on the 20th century') in the editors' Introduction, printed p. 1 (running header 'Introduction 2' follows at l. 56). The 'multiple deterministic or anti-indeterministic readings' claim is the editors' own sentence on pp. 1-2. Page range 1-3 is slightly wide: p. 3 opens the Stoic section.
  - « But it is not yet a technical term, or at least not as technical as it will be in later authors. In any case, even though Aristotle uses the expression, he does not define it anywhere in his (extant) works. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Destrée - 2014 - What is Up to Us Studies on Agency and Responsibi.md:32-34] (p. 1)
  - « In the present volume, all the chapters dealing with Aristotle uphold a determi- nistic, or at least an anti-indeterministic, reading. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Destrée - 2014 - What is Up to Us Studies on Agency and Responsibi.md:46-47] (pp. 1-2)

- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim on printed p. 3 (agentic_search: l. 114/120 -> p. 3). First sentence is the editors' own claim; the rest summarises Katja Vogt's chapter. One qualification the node omits: the editors report that Vogt 'argues that these matters are best not put in terms of freedom and determinism' and that only-one-option is frustrating 'only for the imperfect reasoner'.
  - « The Stoics attempted to preserve moral responsibility within a causally determined world by means of a re-interpretation of the notion itself of what it is for something to be eph’ hêmin. The chapter by Katja Vogt studies the case of a Stoic agent » [01_Philosophie_antique/Destrée - 2014 - What is Up to Us Studies on Agency and Responsibi.md:108-110] (p. 3)

- Constat : Opening statement of purpose located on printed p. 285 (Cairn header: Les Études philosophiques 2008/3 n° 86, pp. 285-303). Radice's bibliography is used for the statistics in section I, p. 285-286.

### `scholarly_argument_sharples_free_will_and_determinism_in_a_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and determinism in antiquity — The problem of free will and determinism was formulated at particular times in antiquity for reasons that cannot be fully determined; we cannot know if the factors were sufficient conditions since we have no access to other possible worlds
- Constat : Located on p. 285. Sharples frames it as a general limit on historical explanation (never 'entièrement déterministes', plausibility is the most one can expect) and adds that where several explanations each look sufficient one may ask whether all apply — the node keeps the core claim.
  - « Nous ne saurions dire si les facteurs qui ont conduit à ce scénario étaient des conditions suffisant à son apparition, du simple fait que nous n’avons aucun accès à d’autres mondes possibles où il aurait pu en aller autrement. » [01_Philosophie_antique/L'accident du déterminisme, Sharples.md:74-77] (p. 285)

### `scholarly_argument_gerson_plotinus_s_philosophical_signi_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Plotinus's philosophical significance for understanding free will in antiquity — Gerson presents Plotinus as 'the greatest philosopher in the 700-year period between Aristotle and Augustine' and emphasizes that Plotinus produced a 'reinvigorated version of Platonism' that became Neoplatonism, suggesting the volume treats Plotinus's thought (including his voluntarism) as foundational for later philosophical developments including medieval philosophy

Gerson's own Introduction hedges: Plotinus 'is probably the dominant philosopher in the 700-year period'. 'Voluntarism' appears nowhere in the blurb or the Introduction; the blurb says the contributors show 'how he was a founder of medieval philosophy'.

He thought of himself as a disciple of Plato, but in his efforts to defend Platonism against Aristotelians, Stoics, and others, he actually pro- duced a reinvigorated version of Platonism that later came to be known as "Neoplatonism." » [01_Philosophie_antique/The Cambridge Companion to Plotinus -- Plotinus.;Gerson, Lloyd P -- Cambridge Companions to Philosophy, 2006 -- Cambridge University Press -- 20c1b3b27d00ad3f5ede3acb7a8f50e8 -- Anna’s Archive.md:24-29] (front matter, unsigned blurb)

- Description proposée : The publisher's blurb (unsigned front matter) calls Plotinus 'the greatest philosopher in the 700-year period between Aristotle and Augustine' who produced 'a reinvigorated version of Platonism' later called 'Neoplatonism', and says the essays show 'how he was a founder of medieval philosophy'. Gerson's own Introduction is more guarded: Plotinus is 'probably the dominant philosopher' of that period. Neither text mentions voluntarism or free will as foundational.
- Pages : front matter (blurb); Introduction p. 4 [page-conf<0.90]

### `scholarly_argument_tomberlin_divine_omniscience_and_counter_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : divine omniscience and counterfactuals of freedom (James E. Tomberlin and Frank McGuinness, Religious Studies 13, 1977, pp. 455-475) — If God is omniscient, he knows for every free possible person whether that person would, if created, perform morally evil actions; this knowledge, combined with omnipotence and perfect goodness, entails that no free person created by God performs morally evil actions
- Constat : The omniscience premise and the entailment 'no free person created by God performs morally evil actions' are premises (5)-(6) of 'the objection' the authors set out on p. 456 and then defend against Plantinga. But their conclusion (pp. 474-475) makes the entailment hinge on premise (19), i.e. on 'whether or not free will is compatible with causality', which they treat as a 'basic opinion'; they 'would select the first' option. The node states as flat entailment what the authors present as conditional on (19).
  - « It is equally true, so the objection continues, that if God is omniscient, then he knows, for every free person, whether or not this person would, if created, perform morally evil actions. Put in a nutshell, then, if God is omniscient, omnipotent, and wholly good, surely it follows that no free person created by God performs morally evil actions. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/god-evil-and-the-free-will-defence.md:66-71] (p. 456)
  - « if (19) is true, the free will defence fails and (1) is not consistent with (3); but if (19) is false, the free will defence succeeds and (1) is consistent with (3). » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/god-evil-and-the-free-will-defence.md:968-970] (p. 474)
- Description proposée : Tomberlin and McGuinness set out an objection to the free will defence: if God is omniscient he knows, for any free possible person, whether that person would, if created, perform morally evil actions; with omnipotence and wholly-goodness this yields that no free person created by God performs moral evil. They defend the objection against Plantinga but concede at the end that its success turns on premise (19), i.e. on whether free will is compatible with causality; they themselves opt for (19)'s truth.
- Pages : 456-458, 474-475

### `scholarly_argument_tomberlin_free_will_defence_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will defence — The free will defence fails; the truth of moral evil constitutes excellent grounds for concluding that God (as defined by omnipotence, omniscience, and perfect goodness) does not exist. The argument is advanced by James E. Tomberlin and Frank McGuinness, Religious Studies vol. 13 (1977), pp. 455-475
- Constat : Thesis stated in the authors' own words on p. 456 ('excellent grounds for concluding that (1) is false'). Bibliographic details (Rel. Stud. 13, pp. 455-475; Plantinga) confirmed. Note the closing qualification (pp. 474-475): the verdict depends on (19), and the authors 'would select the first' of two options.
  - « What is more, we think that the truth of (2) constitutes, contrary to Plantinga and his defence of the free will defence, excellent grounds for concluding that (1) is false. The purpose of the present study, therefore, is to explain in some detail why we take this to be so. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/god-evil-and-the-free-will-defence.md:79-82] (p. 456)
  - « Of these two options, the present authors - as this study makes clear - would select the first. » [07_Libre_arbitre_theologie/god-evil-and-the-free-will-defence.md:982-983] (p. 475)

- Constat : Located verbatim in the editors' Introduction, printed pp. 1-2 (Springer folio '1' at l. 250, '2 M. Hausmann and J. Noller' at l. 255).

### `scholarly_argument_barclay_methodology_of_mirror_reading__0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

- Description proposée : Dettwiler treats vv. 12c-13 as the evangelist's exegetical comments on 12ab. Theologically, 12c makes reception of the Logos explicit as believing in his name, showing the evangelist's early interest in faith; v. 13 adds that becoming children of God is possible only through God's own action, believers having their origin in God and not in a purely human sphere — a double explanation that anticipates the second-birth theme of ch. 3. He does not phrase this as divine generation versus human decision.

He cites Vollenweider's description of Rom 6 as the difference between two lordships marked by a death boundary, but warns that lordship-change language suggests a static anthropology: the new life under Christ is of another nature, essentially qualified by liberty.

- Nœud actuel : free will vs. determinism in Romans 9-11 — Paul's statements in Romans 9-11 have been interpreted to support positions ranging from fatalism to free will, but Oropeza does not take a definitive stance favoring one side; he presents the passage as a theodicy focused on God's faithfulness rather than resolving the philosophical debate
- Constat : p. 57 does survey the range 'from fatalism to the free will' and p. 58 frames Rom 9-11 as a theodicy of God's faithfulness. But Oropeza does take positions the node denies: the Jacob/Esau section 'does not address predestination'; Paul 'focuses on election instead of predestination', corporate rather than individual (p. 63); teaching double predestination as 'a permanent or fixed state' in ch. 9 would contradict ch. 11 (p. 75); double predestination cannot account for Paul's anguish (p. 78 n. 66). 'Does not take a definitive stance' is false.
  - « This sec- tion does not address predestination; neither Paul nor his immediate sources speak to the eternal destinies of the historical persons he mentions. Paul focuses on election instead of predestination, and his emphasis rests on corporate rather than individual election. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/OROPEZA - 2007 - Paul and Theodicy Intertextual Thoughts on God's Justice and Faithfulness to Israel in Romans 9–11.md:331-335] (p. 63)
  - « If Paul were teaching double predestination as a permanent or fixed state in ch. 9, this could hardly be the case in ch. 11 without contradicting himself. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/OROPEZA - 2007 - Paul and Theodicy Intertextual Thoughts on God's Justice and Faithfulness to Israel in Romans 9–11.md:970-971] (p. 75)
  - « Romans 9–11 can be understood, then, as a theodicy focusing on the question of God’s faithfulness to the people of Israel. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/OROPEZA - 2007 - Paul and Theodicy Intertextual Thoughts on God's Justice and Faithfulness to Israel in Romans 9–11.md:66-67] (p. 58)
- Description proposée : Oropeza notes that Rom 9-11 has been used to support 'everything from fatalism to the free will, divine foreknowledge to double predestination' (p. 57) and reads the chapters as a theodicy of God's faithfulness to Israel (p. 58). He then argues that the Jacob/Esau and Pharaoh sections concern corporate election, not individual predestination or eternal destinies (p. 63), that double predestination as a fixed state in ch. 9 would contradict the openness of ch. 11 (p. 75), and that it cannot explain Paul's anguish over Israel (p. 78 n. 66).
- Pages : 57-58; 63; 75; 78

### `scholarly_argument_richardson_free_will_in_early_christianit_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will in early Christianity — The volume is primarily a source collection and translation of early Christian texts rather than a work of original scholarship advancing interpretive positions on free will; the editor's introduction focuses on literary-historical classification and transmission of texts rather than philosophical or theological analysis of determinism, fate, or moral responsibility
- Constat : Richardson's general Introduction (pp. 15-27: Cotelier, Gallandi, Byrennios 1873) is literary-historical, as stated. But the volume's section introductions do take positions on free will: the Justin section, edited and translated by Edward Rochie Hardy (not Richardson), says Justin 'presents a simple doctrine of free will' yet knows 'it is only by the gift of God that men are able to understand his truth' (p. 236); the index has 'Free will, 259, 327; and destiny, 269 f.' The node's blanket 'no philosophical or theological analysis' is too strong and would misattribute Hardy's judgement if extended to Richardson.
  - « Formally Justin presents a simple doctrine of free will which would suggest that the only salvation man would need is information, and the removal of the obstacles interposed by the demons. But as incidental references show, he is well enough aware that it is only by the gift of God that men are able to understand his truth. » [06_Patristique/(The Library of Christian Classics) Cyril C. Richardson - Early Christian Fathers-Westminster Press (2006).md:9655-9660] (p. 236, section by E. R. Hardy)
  - « and Translated by Edward Rochie Hardy) » [06_Patristique/(The Library of Christian Classics) Cyril C. Richardson - Early Christian Fathers-Westminster Press (2006).md:286]
- Description proposée : Richardson's Early Christian Fathers (LCC 1) is a source collection with translations; his general Introduction (pp. 15-27) treats the history and classification of the 'Apostolic Fathers' corpus (Cotelier, Gallandi, the 1873 Didache discovery), not free will. The section introductions do contain theological judgements: Edward Rochie Hardy's introduction to Justin states that Justin 'formally' presents 'a simple doctrine of free will' while recognising that understanding God's truth comes only by God's gift (p. 236).
- Pages : 15-27; 236 (Hardy)

### `scholarly_argument_rousseau_editorial_methodology_for_reco_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : editorial methodology for reconstructing Irenaeus's text on free will — The critical edition establishes the Greek text (where extant) and Latin version as the basis for understanding Irenaeus's arguments against Gnostic determinism; the Armenian tradition and Syriac fragments are collated to recover Irenaeus's original formulations on human freedom and divine justice
- Constat : The Avant-propos (OCR-degraded but legible) is the history of the edition: Sagnard's Book III (1952), his death in October 1957 while preparing Book IV, and the new team (Hemmerdinger, Mercier, Doutreleau). The Armenian and Syriac witnesses are handled in the Introduction ('Les traditions arménienne et syriaque', p. 95) as general text-critical work on the Latin translation. Nothing frames the edition as recovering Irenaeus's 'formulations on human freedom and divine justice'; rg -i 'libre arbitre|liberté' returns no hit in tome I. That purpose is the node's invention.

  - « brillantes conjectures que confirme la version arménienne » [10_Ouvrages_reference/(Sources Chrétiennes  100, 1) A. Rousseau, B. Hemmerdinger, C. Mercier, L. Doutreleau - Irénée de Lyon _ Contre les hérésies, Livre IV, tome I-Cerf (1965).md:489] [OCR-degraded: SC 100/1 Introduction]
- Description proposée : SC 100 tome I is the introduction and apparatus volume for Irenaeus, Adversus haereses IV. Its Avant-propos recounts the edition's history (Sagnard's Book III, his death in 1957, the new team of Rousseau, Hemmerdinger, Mercier and Doutreleau); its Introduction studies the Latin translation, the Greek fragments and the Armenian and Syriac traditions as witnesses for establishing the text of Book IV as a whole. It does not present the edition as aimed at Irenaeus's teaching on human freedom.

### `scholarly_argument_sagnard_free_will_and_moral_responsibi_0` — **NOT_FOUND** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and moral responsibility in Irenaeus — Sagnard presents Irenaeus as holding that human beings have free will and moral responsibility, created by God with the capacity for both obedience and disobedience; this freedom is essential to Irenaeus's anti-gnostic polemic against deterministic systems
- Constat : Searched the SC 34 file (Avant-propos, Introduction, l. 66-2100) for 'libre arbitre', 'liberté', 'libre', 'obéiss', 'désobéiss', 'responsab', 'déterminis'. No passage presents Irenaeus as holding that humans were created with a capacity for obedience and disobedience, nor ties freedom to an anti-gnostic polemic against deterministic systems. 'Liberté' occurs only as Irenaeus's theme of the new economy of liberty (Introduction, p. 34-35 by folio), and obedience/disobedience only in the Eve/Mary recapitulation (p. 36). The node's own reference concedes the locus is AH IV.37-39 (SC 100), not Book III.
  - « (p. 177) i Tous ces textes nous montrent un seul Dieu ouvrant aux hommes une économie nouvelle de liberté par la • disposi tion » nouvelle de la venue de son Fils ». » [06_Patristique/(Sources Chrétiennes 34) Irénée de Lyon, François Sagnard (Ed.) - Contre les hérésies, Livre III-Editions du Cerf (1952).md:1660-1662] (Introduction, p. 34-35 [page-conf<0.90])
- Description proposée : Sagnard's Introduction to SC 34 (Book III) treats Irenaeus and martyrdom, the plan and sources of Book III, and Irenaeus's vocabulary of newness — a 'new economy of liberty' opened by the coming of the Son — and the Eve/Mary recapitulation. It contains no exposition of free will, of a created capacity for obedience and disobedience, or of freedom as the pivot of the anti-gnostic polemic; those themes belong to Book IV (SC 100).
